## Introduction: A Provincial Master and the Confluence of Tuscan Currents

*Allegretto Nuzi* — recorded in documents and on his own panels as Allegretto di Nuzio, Allegrittus Nuttii, and Allegritus de Fabriano — occupies a singular position in the history of fourteenth-century Italian painting. Born at Fabriano, in the mountainous interior of the Marche, around 1315–1320 (the National Gallery of Art's systematic catalogue and the Kress Foundation give the birth range as 1316–1320, and his death as 1373 or 1373/1374, while Italian scholarship conventionally cites "Fabriano, 1315 circa – 1373"), he was at once a provincial artist and a cosmopolitan one: a painter whose formation drew on the two dominant schools of the Tuscan Trecento, Florence and Siena, and who then transplanted that synthesis into the Adriatic hinterland, where it took root and flowered into a genuine local school. If the Marche possessed, before the middle of the fourteenth century, only sporadic and anonymous traces of monumental painting, it was Allegretto who gave the region its first artistic personality of enduring consequence, the figure around whom a recognisable "Fabrianese school" crystallised and from whose achievement the line runs, across two generations, to Gentile da Fabriano and the splendours of the International Gothic.

The essential paradox of Allegretto's career is that his greatness is inseparable from his provinciality. He was not an innovator in the sense that [Giotto](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Giotto-di-Bondone.html) or Ambrogio Lorenzetti were innovators; his historical importance, as one of his earliest modern biographers observed, rarely transcends the "communal glory" of his native city and the merit of having disseminated Tuscan forms across the Apennine watershed. And yet, precisely because he stood at the meeting point of currents — Florentine plasticity and Sienese line, Riminese Giottism and, in his final years, an incipient Venetian decorativism — his oeuvre offers an unusually legible cross-section of the artistic geography of the mid-Trecento. He absorbed the monumental gravity of Maso di Banco and [Bernardo Daddi](/Content/Artists/XIV-c/Bernardo-Daddi.html), the tender expressivity of the Lorenzetti, and the ornamental sumptuousness that would become the hallmark of the courtly Gothic; and he fused these into a language of his own, at once grave and decorative, plastically weighty and increasingly linear, capable of both the grand altarpiece and the intimate devotional panel.

This essay seeks to reconstruct Allegretto Nuzi's life, work and afterlife on the basis of primary documents — his 1346 enrolment in the Florentine painters' confraternity, the scatter of notarial records that place him back in Fabriano, and his testament of 1373 — and of the most authoritative secondary scholarship. In keeping with the principle that uncertainty should be named rather than smoothed over, particular attention is paid throughout to the points at which the evidence is fragmentary, the attributions disputed, or the dates in conflict. The catalogue of his securely dated works, fortunately, is comparatively rich for a fourteenth-century provincial master: a series of dated panels, several of them signed, provides a firm chronological armature onto which the larger body of attributed works, and the great fresco cycles of Fabriano, may be fastened.

## Fabriano in the Fourteenth Century: Paper, Piety and Patronage

To understand Allegretto Nuzi one must first understand Fabriano, the town that made him and that he, in turn, made famous. Nestled in the Esino valley among the foothills of the central Apennines, Fabriano in the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries was no sleepy mountain settlement but a prosperous and technologically precocious commune, and the source of its wealth was paper. The earliest surviving evidence of the industry is a parchment document of 1264, preserved today at Matelica, recording the neighbouring municipality's purchase of Fabriano-made paper for its official acts — sized with animal gelatine and made from hemp and linen rags — and by the late thirteenth century the town had become the leading centre of paper manufacture in medieval Europe. Fabriano's rise rested on three documented technical innovations that transformed the economics and quality of European papermaking: animal-gelatine sizing, the watermark, and the multiple (water-powered) hammer mill for macerating rags into pulp. These gave Fabriano a near-monopoly on high-quality paper that would endure for centuries, and would furnish the sheets prized by merchants, princes and popes across the Mediterranean world. The first documentary reference to professional papermakers in a Fabriano notarial register dates to 1282.

The prosperity generated by paper, alongside a substantial wool industry, underwrote a communal society of considerable sophistication. By the thirteenth century the government of Fabriano had evolved from seigneurial rule by noble families into a republican commune administered through established guilds; a public act of 1278 lists twelve artisan corporations, among which the merchants and the wool-workers were dominant. Crucially, the Marche belonged to the Papal States, and the town's prosperity was played out against the backdrop of papal overlordship, ecclesiastical patronage and the intense devotional life of the mendicant orders. The great churches of Fabriano — the cathedral of San Venanzio, San Domenico (later Santa Lucia Novella), Sant'Agostino, Santa Lucia — were the theatres of that devotional life, and it was for them, and for the confraternities and religious houses attached to them, that Allegretto would produce his frescoes and altarpieces. A society wealthy enough to prize luxury, pious enough to demand a constant supply of sacred images, and mercantile enough to be plugged into wider currents of exchange: this was the milieu in which Allegretto's art of gold and colour found its natural audience.

Yet before Allegretto, the artistic record of Fabriano is thin and largely anonymous. Scholarship has identified a handful of shadowy but distinctive personalities active in and around the town in the first half of the Trecento. The most important of these is the so-called Master of Campodonico, named for the frescoes — a Crucifixion and other scenes — in the [Benedictine](/endnotes.html#fn-benedictine-order) abbey of San Biagio in Caprile at Campodonico, and also known as the Master of San Biagio in Caprile. Active around the middle of the century, this anonymous master was an interpreter of the Giottesque tradition of unusual power and formal control, described by the art historian Luigi Serra as "the highest expression of the Giottesque tradition in the Marchigian sphere"; the plasticity and sculptural volume of his figures, and the refined lapidary inscriptions in his works, have even led to the suggestion that he was a miniaturist and sculptor as well as a painter.

Alongside him stand the Master of Sant'Emiliano — whose Maestà from Sant'Emiliano in Congiuntoli is now in Fabriano's civic gallery — and the anonymous fresco painter of San Biagio in Caprile, figures who together laid the foundations of what would become the Fabrianese school. It was through such local intermediaries, according to one strand of scholarship, that echoes of Sienese art, and specifically of Ambrogio Lorenzetti, first reached Fabriano, preparing the ground for Allegretto's own more direct engagement with Tuscan painting. The town also lay open to the Riminese Giottesque painters then active across the Marche, so that the young Allegretto grew up in an environment already saturated, if at second hand, with the achievements of the central-Italian mainstream.

## The Biography and the Documents: A Life Reconstructed

The documentary record of Allegretto Nuzi, while incomplete, is unusually informative for a provincial painter of his generation, and it anchors what would otherwise be a purely stylistic reconstruction. The point of departure is his birth at Fabriano, placed by scholarly consensus around 1315–1320. That he was a native of Fabriano is not in doubt: he signed several of his works "Allegritus de Fabriano," proclaiming his origin, and the family name derived from his father, Nuzio (a diminutive of a longer baptismal name), so that "Allegretto di Nuzio" means simply "Allegretto, son of Nuzio."

The first firm date in his biography is 1346, and it locates him not in Fabriano but in Florence. In that year he enrolled in the [Compagnia di San Luca](/endnotes.html#fn-compagnia-san-luca), the lay confraternity of Florentine painters, and his name also appears in the register of foreign members of the [Arte dei Medici e Speziali](/endnotes.html#fn-guild-physicians-apothecaries), the guild that incorporated painters. The transcription of these Florentine records was published by the scholar [Ugo Procacci](/scholars.html#procacci-ugo) in his 1961 study "Il primo ricordo di Giovanni da Milano a Firenze" (the relevant notice appears on page 65). What makes the entry especially revealing is the form of the name: "Allegrettus Nuccii de Senis" — Allegretto, son of Nuccio, "of Siena." The designation "de Senis" has generated considerable discussion. It cannot mean that Allegretto was Sienese by birth, for his Fabrianese origin is otherwise secure; the most economical explanation, widely accepted, is that he arrived in Florence after a formative period spent in Siena, so that the Florentine authorities registered him according to his most recent place of residence. This documentary hint of a Sienese sojourn dovetails precisely with the profound imprint of Sienese art — above all of Ambrogio Lorenzetti — that pervades his mature painting.

Allegretto's Florentine sojourn was not prolonged. He returned to Fabriano at the end of the 1340s — the recent scholarship of [Andrea De Marchi](/scholars.html#de-marchi-andrea) and [Matteo Mazzalupi](/scholars.html#mazzalupi-matteo) dates the return firmly to 1347, from which year he is said to have worked continuously ("stabilmente") at Fabriano until his death, while the older tradition represented by the Treccani Enciclopedia dell'Arte Medievale places the return around 1348 and links it explicitly to the flight from the Black Death.

Whichever year is preferred, notarial documents place him back in his native city in 1348 and 1350, and his name also appears, undated, in the register of a Fabriano confraternity begun in 1343 — a record published by Stefano Felicetti in his "Regesti documentari, 1299–1499" appended to the 1998 volume on the Master of Campodonico edited by Fabio Marcelli (the notice on page 215). From this point until his death, Allegretto worked principally at Fabriano and in the surrounding Marchigian centres — Macerata, Apiro, San Severino and others — producing altarpieces for churches and confraternities, small portable panels for private devotion, and extensive fresco cycles. Scholarship has suggested, on stylistic grounds, that he may have renewed his Florentine contacts in the 1350s, drawing closer to the flourishing workshop of [Andrea Orcagna](/Content/Artists/XIV-c/Andrea-di-Cione-Orcagna.html) and his brother Nardo di Cione; some have even hypothesised a second Florentine journey around 1360. Whether or not he travelled again to Tuscany, the evidence of his panels makes clear that he remained in living dialogue with Florentine developments throughout his career.

The most poignant of the documents is the last. Allegretto's testament was drawn up on 26 September 1373 at Fabriano, before the notary Diotisalvi di Bonaventura; it was first published by Anselmi in 1893. He died shortly afterwards, in any case before 20 November 1373, so that his death can be placed with precision between late September and late November of that year (some museum catalogues, allowing for the interval between the will and the settlement of his affairs, extend the possible death date into 1374, hence the occasional "1373/1374"). He was buried, according to a second study by Anselmi published in 1906, in the church of San Niccolò at Fabriano.

The substantive contents of the will — its bequests, its heirs, any endowment of a chapel or altar — are not reported in the standard reference literature, and in the absence of direct consultation of Anselmi's 1893 transcription no claim about them can responsibly be made here. What the testament does establish beyond doubt is the terminus of a working life of nearly three decades in his native city. Two minor bibliographical discrepancies should be flagged for the specialist: the journal in which Anselmi published the will in 1893 is cited variously as the Archivio Storico Italiano and as the Archivio Storico dell'Arte, and the title of his 1906 essay appears as both "La data della morte…" and "L'anno della morte…"; the author, year and pagination are nonetheless consistent across these citations.

Around Allegretto there gathered, as around every successful Trecento master, a workshop and a family enterprise. The most important of his associates was his fellow Fabrianese [Francescuccio di Cecco Ghissi](/Content/Artists/XIV-c/Francescuccio-Ghissi.html), at once his pupil, emulator and occasional collaborator, whose independent activity is documented from 1359 to 1395. Equally significant for the reconstruction of his career is his documented professional association with the Florentine painter [Puccio di Simone](/Content/Artists/XIV-c/Puccio-di-Simone.html), with whom he collaborated on a major altarpiece destined for Fabriano — a partnership discussed in detail below. The extensive fresco cycles attributed to him in the churches of Fabriano were, the sources agree, executed with substantial workshop participation, and at least two or three additional hands besides Allegretto's own have been distinguished within them.

## Florentine Formation and Sienese Sensibility: The Making of a Style

The stylistic personality of Allegretto Nuzi is best understood as a controlled tension between two poles: the sculptural, space-making tradition of Florence and the linear, colouristic, ornamental tradition of Siena. His earliest surviving works reveal a painter steeped in the Florentine idiom of the second quarter of the century. The foundational Florentine component of his art derives, according to the systematic scholarship, essentially from Maso di Banco — the gravest and most monumental of Giotto's immediate followers — with a strong admixture of Bernardo Daddi, the Florentine master who had himself effected a graceful compromise between Giottesque volume and Sienese sweetness. From Daddi, Allegretto took the softly rounded contours, the tender exchanges of glance between Mother and Child, the intimate scale of the portable tabernacle; from Maso, the sense of figures possessing real weight and occupying real space.

To this Florentine foundation Allegretto joined, from the outset, an intimate familiarity with Sienese painting, and specifically with the art of the Lorenzetti brothers, [Pietro](/Content/Artists/XIV-c/Pietro-Lorenzetti.html) and Ambrogio. The documentary suggestion that he had spent time in Siena before 1346 is fully borne out by the character of his work, in which the influence of Ambrogio Lorenzetti is, in the words of his principal twentieth-century biographer, "dominant throughout the whole activity of the Fabrianese." From Ambrogio came the softly modelled, gently melancholic faces, the caressing line, and above all the love of richly patterned textiles and ornamental surface that would grow ever more pronounced as Allegretto matured. His art is thus a personal resolution of the great dialectic of the Tuscan Trecento — the Florentine-Sienese synthesis that Daddi had pioneered and that Allegretto carried across the Apennines.

Around the middle of the 1350s a discernible shift occurs. Allegretto begins to move away from the Daddesque manner and to show a vivid interest in the art of Andrea Orcagna and Nardo di Cione, the brothers whose severe, hieratic, monumentally simplified style dominated Florentine painting in the aftermath of the Black Death. Orcagna's Strozzi Altarpiece of 1357 in [Santa Maria Novella](/Content/Churches/Basilica-Santa-Maria-Novella.html) has been invoked as the touchstone for the new gravity and iconic frontality of Allegretto's mature figures, which emerge, in one modern formulation, "from a gold ground with sharp outlines, isolating them in a dimension of solemn and arcane sacredness."

Yet even as he absorbed Orcagna's monumentality, Allegretto increasingly subordinated it to a decorative impulse of his own. The plastic, space-making conception of the figure that he had learned in Florence gradually yielded, over the 1360s, to a flatter, more linear and more ornamental manner in which the modelling of form matters less than the play of pattern across the surface — the sumptuous brocades, the tooled and punched gold, the fantastical textiles woven with birds and other motifs. In his final years, some scholars have detected a further inflection: the penetration into the Marche of the Venetian painters Paolo and Lorenzo Veneziano, whose flat, richly decorative treatment of fabrics may have reinforced Allegretto's own late linearism, prompting the hypothesis — advanced cautiously and never documented — of a journey into Veneto territory.

## The Catalogue of Dated and Documented Works

The reconstruction of Allegretto's artistic trajectory rests on a spine of dated works, several of them signed, and it is worth setting these out with care, for it is precisely in the dates and inscriptions that the discipline of primary evidence must be exercised most rigorously.

### Allegretto Nuzi and Puccio di Simone, The Fabriano Triptych of 1354: Madonna and Child Enthroned with Saints and Angels, with Saints Anthony Abbot and Venantius

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-18.jpg" alt="Gothic triptych on gold ground by Allegretto Nuzi and Puccio di Simone, 1354: the Virgin in a blue mantle enthroned with the Christ Child holding a goldfinch, surrounded by eighteen angels and four saints; on the left wing Saint Anthony Abbot in a brown habit with staff; on the right wing the young Saint Venantius with a banner, in a red-gold brocade tunic and blue cloak; Crucifixion and Annunciation in the pinnacles">
  <figcaption>Allegretto Nuzi and Puccio di Simone, Madonna and Child Enthroned with Saints and Angels, and Saints Anthony Abbot and Venantius, 1354, tempera and gold on panel, Left panel: 123.8 x 45.7 x 8.3 cm (framed); Middle panel: 150.5 x 66.7 x 8.3 cm (framed); Right panel: 123.8 x 43.2 x 8.3 cm (framed); Washington D.C., National Gallery of Art, inv. 1937.1.6.a–c.</figcaption>
</figure>

The first securely dated surviving work in which Allegretto Nuzi's hand can be recognised is the triptych now in the National Gallery of Art in Washington, executed in 1354. This celebrated altarpiece was painted jointly by the Florentine Puccio di Simone and Allegretto Nuzi. Its central panel shows the Madonna and Child Enthroned with Four Saints and Eighteen Angels, flanked by lateral panels of Saint Anthony Abbot on the left and Saint Venantius on the right. The date 1354 is recorded, in partly damaged form, in the vernacular inscription along the lower edge of the central panel, which also preserves the name of the commissioning friar, "frate Giovanni."

The collaboration is instructive. The two artists were not based in the same city: Puccio was among the leading painters of Florence, while Allegretto, after his Florentine sojourn, had returned to the Marche. The Florentine master was evidently called to Fabriano to work on an altarpiece for a church dedicated to Saint Anthony Abbot, who accordingly appears twice, in the left wing and among the saints gathered around the Virgin's throne. According to the National Gallery of Art's catalogue, Puccio painted the central panel and the right wing with Saint Venantius, while Allegretto's contribution was the left wing with Saint Anthony. The Florentine painter was long known to scholarship only by the provisional name "Master of the Fabriano Altarpiece," before that anonymous personality was identified with Puccio di Simone. Fifteen years later, Allegretto painted alone a practically identical version of the altarpiece, now in the Duomo of Macerata, for the church of Sant'Antonio Abate in that town. The inscriptions of both works name a commissioning friar called Giovanni, which suggests that the same Antonine patron commissioned both. The Washington triptych entered the collection of the American financier Andrew W. Mellon, having passed through the hands of the collector Carl Hamilton and the dealer Joseph Duveen, and came to the National Gallery of Art in 1937. On the strength of its left wing, the museum holds the cornerstone of Allegretto's dated oeuvre.

Local Fabrianese sources report earlier dated works, namely panels in the church of Santa Lucia dated 1345 and 1349. These are lost, their inscriptions can no longer be verified, and the reliability of the reported dates cannot now be tested. In 1834 the antiquarian Amico Ricci tentatively identified the work of 1345 with a Madonna and Child Enthroned with Eight Angels formerly in the D'Abernon collection in London. Because that panel's inscription is now illegible, however, the identification remains unprovable. A street tabernacle with a fresco of the Madonna and Child, formerly on the Via di San Filippo in Fabriano and now in the town's Pinacoteca, may also date to around 1354. Its inscription too is lost, and the date recorded by local tradition appears to have been incorrectly transcribed. Such cases are salutary reminders of how much of the early record rests on testimony that can no longer be verified.

#### Left Panel: Saint Anthony Abbot

Saint Anthony Abbot, the father of Christian monasticism, stands alone beneath a cusped Gothic arch, set against a burnished gold ground. He is the elder of the two titular saints who frame the Virgin's court. He is rendered as a venerable hermit with a long, flowing grey beard, and his face is furrowed and grave. His head is covered by the hood of a heavy brown habit whose dark cowl gathers around his shoulders. His body turns in three-quarter view toward the central panel, so that he becomes not an isolated devotional icon but a participant in the sacred conversation unfolding beside him. His gaze is fixed on the enthroned Virgin, and his whole figure is oriented toward her as an intercessor presenting the faithful to Christ and his Mother.

The saint's gestures are economical and eloquent. His right hand is raised before his chest, with the fingers loosely opened in a gesture of address or witness that recalls the rhetorical language of Giottesque painting. His left hand grasps a tall staff with a curved handle, the traditional attribute of the aged hermit who supported himself in the Egyptian desert. The staff descends in a single, unbroken line to the ground, giving the composition a firm vertical axis that echoes the colonnettes of the frame. At the lower left, partly absorbed into the shadow of the habit, a small dark animal can be discerned: the pig that accompanies the saint in Western iconography. The motif alludes to his victory over the demonic, but also, more concretely, to the Hospital Brothers of Saint Anthony, whose pigs were permitted to roam freely through medieval towns to raise alms for the care of the sick. As the inscription on the halo makes explicit, it is precisely this Antonine saint whom the panel presents.

The figure possesses a monumental, almost architectonic weight that reveals how deeply Allegretto had absorbed the Florentine tradition during his years in Tuscany. The habit falls in broad, heavy folds whose volumes are modelled through gradations of brown and deep umber rather than through linear pattern. The body thus reads as a solid mass occupying real space, in the manner of Maso di Banco and Bernardo Daddi, whose works shaped the Marchigian painter's early style. This severe, sculptural conception, with its restraint of colour and its avoidance of ornament on the garment itself, sets the Anthony panel apart from the richly brocaded Venantius on the opposite wing, painted by Puccio di Simone. The contrast between the two lateral figures thus makes the collaboration legible at a glance. The difference lies less in quality than in temperament: the Fabrianese painter's grave, massive saint stands against the Florentine's more graceful and ornate youth.

The head merits particular attention. The beard is built up with fine, fluid strokes of grey and white that evoke the texture of hair without dissolving the overall form. The flesh is modelled with the soft greenish underlayer characteristic of Tuscan tempera technique, over which warmer tones have been applied. The deep-set eyes, the heavy brows and the downturned mouth convey an ascetic seriousness appropriate to the desert father. In type, this head recalls the patriarchs and hermits of Bernardo Daddi's workshop, from whose orbit both Puccio di Simone and the young Allegretto emerged.

Around the saint's head, the gold of the halo is tooled with an inscription reading

> .S[AN]C[TU]S.ANTONIVS.D[E].VIENA,

"Saint Anthony of Vienne."

The lettering is incised and punched into the gold leaf within a circular band, surrounded by concentric rings of ornament. The epithet is more than a formula. It names the saint not simply as the Egyptian desert father but as the Anthony venerated at Saint-Antoine-en-Viennois, near Vienne in the Dauphiné, where his relics had been translated in the eleventh century. That shrine was also the mother house of the Hospital Brothers of Saint Anthony, whose hospitals cared for sufferers of ergotism, known as "Saint Anthony's fire."

The inscription therefore places the panel within the orbit of the Antonine cult, which by the fourteenth century had spread widely across Italy, and it strengthens the view that the altarpiece was connected with a church or altar under Anthony's dedication. Inscribed haloes of this kind, common in Florentine painting of the period, also had a practical function, securing the identification of the figure for a congregation that might encounter the altarpiece from a distance. The tooling of the gold ground, with its punched and incised cusped arcading following the profile of the arch, displays the sophisticated goldsmith-like technique shared by the two painters.

Beneath the saint's feet lies a red floor or carpet decorated with a gold pattern of small trefoils and lozenges. This device appears on both lateral panels and establishes a common ground plane for the two titular saints. It is at once a spatial indication, anchoring the figures on a stable surface, and an ornamental one, since the carpet evokes the precious textiles laid before altars and thrones. The motif also creates a chromatic link between the lateral panels and the red and orange tones of the central throne, contributing to the unity of the ensemble.

In the gable above the arch, within a trefoil medallion, appears the half-length figure of the Archangel Gabriel, holding a slender stem and turning toward the opposite wing. Together with the Annunciate Virgin in the corresponding gable of the right panel, he forms an Annunciation divided across the two wings. This arrangement, standard in Tuscan triptychs of the period, frames the whole altarpiece within the mystery of the Incarnation. The small figure is painted with a delicacy and brilliance of colour that contrasts with the sober brown of the saint below. The blue spandrels surrounding the medallion are enlivened with foliate ornament.

The frame, with its spiral colonnettes, pointed gable and moulded cornice, gives the panel the character of a small architectural shrine. Whether every element of the present engaged frame is original is a question that belongs to the technical literature on the object. The panel as a whole, however, exemplifies the refined articulation of the mid-Trecento polyptych, in which painting, carpentry and gilding combine to produce a unified sacred architecture. The austere presence of Saint Anthony in this setting, grave, monumental and turned in humble address toward the Virgin, provides the altarpiece with its note of monastic gravity.

#### Central Panel: Madonna and Child Enthroned with Four Saints and Eighteen Angels

The central panel, taller and broader than the lateral wings, presents the Virgin enthroned as Queen of Heaven, surrounded by a dense celestial court. Mary is seated frontally at the axis of the composition on a throne whose seat and back are covered with a cloth of orange-red brocade shot with gold. Its polygonal base, stepped and decorated with a band of geometric ornament, projects forward into the foreground. The composition follows the type of the Florentine Maestà as it had developed in the circle of Bernardo Daddi. In this type, the monumental enthroned Virgin is combined with a crowded, vertically stacked assembly of angels and saints that fills the gold field on either side and almost entirely conceals the throne's architecture.

The Virgin wears a mantle of deep ultramarine, bordered with a band of gold, which envelops her head and falls in broad folds across her knees to the foot of the throne. A small gold star shines on her shoulder, the traditional emblem of Mary as Stella Maris. Beneath the mantle, a gold-embroidered garment is visible at the breast. Her head inclines gently toward the Child in an attitude of tender contemplation, and the long, oval face, with its narrow eyes and small mouth, conveys a reserved melancholy. Her halo, the largest and most elaborately tooled in the altarpiece, carries the inscription

> .S[AN]C[T]A.MARIA.MATER.DEI.,

"Holy Mary, Mother of God." The title Mater Dei, the Latin equivalent of the Theotokos affirmed at the Council of Ephesus, states in words what the image states in form: the woman enthroned at the centre is honoured because she bore God incarnate. The massive blue form of her body, unbroken by ornament apart from the gold border, is the chromatic and structural anchor of the entire altarpiece. It stands out against the gold ground and the multicoloured angelic host with remarkable clarity.

The Christ Child sits on his Mother's left knee, dressed in a tunic of orange-red brocade that echoes the cloth of honour on the throne. He turns toward her and reaches up to her, while in his other hand he holds a small bird with black and red plumage: a goldfinch. His halo bears the letters IIS . XRO . M, an abbreviated form of the holy name. The first group corresponds to the familiar IHS, and XRO renders the Greek-derived monogram of Christus (usually written XPO, with the Greek rho transcribed here as a Latin R); the final M remains uncertain. In late medieval devotional imagery the goldfinch carried a Passion symbolism. According to legend, the red spot on its head recalled the blood of Christ, or the thorns of the crown that the goldfinch had tried to remove. The playful, childlike motif thus foreshadows the sacrifice depicted in the Crucifixion of the central gable directly above. In this way, a vertical theological axis runs through the panel, from the Incarnation embodied in the Child and named in his halo to the Passion accomplished on the Cross.

On either side of the throne the angels rise in tiers, their haloed heads arranged in rows that recede toward the upper part of the arch. Most turn in profile or three-quarter view toward the Virgin. Some are dressed in blue and red; others, in the lowest rows, wear tunics of gold and green brocade and cross their hands over the breast in adoration. The multiplication of heads and haloes produces a rhythmic, almost musical pattern across the gold field. Here the Florentine monumentality of the central group gives way to a decorative animation in which each halo becomes a small disc of tooled light.

In the foreground, flanking the throne, stand four saints. On the left, a young female saint accompanied by the spiked wheel of her martyrdom is identifiable as Saint Catherine of Alexandria. She wears a mantle of blue and gold richly patterned in the manner of an oriental silk. Beside her stands a bearded elder in a black habit with his hands joined, a monastic figure whose precise identity should be checked against the museum's catalogue. On the right, a second bearded saint in a black habit leans on a tau-headed staff, the attribute of Saint Anthony Abbot, while a young female saint in a rose-coloured dress and white veil gathers roses in her skirt. The roses and the veil suggest Saint Elizabeth of Hungary, although this reading too requires verification. The four figures, smaller in scale than the Virgin, form a terrestrial choir that mediates between the viewer and the celestial court behind them.

The treatment of the gold is exceptionally refined. The cusped arcading punched along the inner profile of the arch, the finely tooled concentric bands of the Virgin's halo, and the individually decorated haloes of the angels and saints together display the full repertory of Trecento gold technique. The brocades of the throne cloth, of the Child's tunic and of the saints' garments, rendered with gold beneath or over the paint, anticipate the love of sumptuous textile that would become a hallmark of Allegretto's mature works in the Marche. This precious surface does not undermine the plastic gravity of the principal figures; rather, it surrounds and enhances it.

Along the lower edge of the painted field runs the inscription

> [MCCC]LIIII.QUESTA TA[VOLA HA F]ATTA F[A]RE. FRATE GIOVANNI DA [...],

recording that in 1354 Friar Giovanni had this panel made. Although damaged, it is one of the most important documents for the chronology of both painters. The hundreds of the date are lost, but the surviving LIIII, read together with the style of the painting and the careers of the two artists, admits only the reading 1354.

It is notable that the dedication is written in the vernacular rather than in Latin, a choice that addresses the altarpiece to a lay as well as a clerical public. The context of the commission, however, points in a clear direction. The altarpiece was made for a church dedicated to Saint Anthony Abbot, and the saint's halo on the left wing names him as Anthony of Vienne, the patron of the Hospital Brothers of Saint Anthony.

A friar named Giovanni also appears in the inscription of Allegretto's triptych of 1369, now in the Duomo of Macerata, which came from the church of Sant'Antonio Abate in that town. That coincidence led [Bernard Berenson](/scholars.html#berenson-bernard) to suppose a common patron for the two works. His hypothesis has since gained considerable support from the recognition that the Macerata triptych is a practically identical version of the Washington altarpiece, repeating even the rare double representation of Saint Anthony. It therefore seems likely that the same Antonine friar commissioned both works fifteen years apart, and that the later altarpiece was deliberately modelled on the earlier one. Even so, in the absence of the lost toponym, the identity of the two patrons remains a strong probability rather than a documented fact.

In the central gable, within a gold quatrefoil set in a blue field, appears the crucified Christ. The small, pale body is suspended on the Cross against the gold with remarkable concision. The Crucifixion crowns the whole altarpiece and completes its programme: the Annunciation in the lateral gables announces the Incarnation, the enthroned Virgin and Child manifest it, and the Crucifixion reveals its redemptive purpose. The central panel is therefore not only the visual core of the triptych but also its theological summary, in which Florentine monumentality and Marchigian ornament unite in the service of a coherent devotional narrative.

#### Right Panel: Saint Venantius

Saint Venantius, the youthful martyr of Camerino and patron of Fabriano, stands beneath the cusped arch of the right wing as the civic counterpart to the monastic Saint Anthony. His presence is the most eloquent indication of the altarpiece's Fabrianese destination. The cult of Venantius, who according to legend was martyred as a boy of fifteen under the emperor Decius, was deeply rooted in the Marche, and the cathedral of Fabriano bears his dedication. By placing him in a position of honour at the Virgin's left hand, the patron integrated the universal hierarchy of the Maestà with the devotional identity of the local community.

The saint is represented as a beardless youth with long, wavy golden hair falling to his shoulders. His face is turned in three-quarter view toward the central panel. The features are delicate and slightly elongated, with a high forehead, narrow eyes and a small mouth, and the flesh is modelled in pale, luminous tones. The expression is serene and somewhat distant, conveying the idealised composure of the martyr rather than the psychological intensity of the elderly Anthony opposite. Around his head, a finely tooled halo bears the inscription

> .S[AN]C[TU]S . VENANCIVS . M[A]RTIRI,

"Saint Venantius Martyr."

The spelling Venancius, with c in place of t before the vowel, reflects ordinary medieval Latin orthography. The epithet martir stresses the claim on which his cult rested: his death for the faith as a youth under Decius. The inscription thus mirrors the halo of Saint Anthony on the opposite wing, and the two lettered haloes frame the Virgin's court with a pair of named protectors, one monastic, the other civic.

In his right hand Venantius holds a tall staff bearing a banner, the standard of victory associated with the martyr and with the city that claimed him as protector. The banner is decorated with a lattice or chequered pattern in its upper section, over a red field below. It functions both as an attribute of triumph over death and as a heraldic reference to the civic realm. The staff rises parallel to the spiral colonnettes of the frame, balancing the long staff of Saint Anthony on the left wing, so that the two lateral figures correspond to each other in symmetrical opposition.

The saint's costume is the most sumptuous element of the whole altarpiece. He wears a long tunic of orange-red brocade densely covered with gold patterning and punctuated by large blue rosettes, and its hem is bordered with a broad band of gold bearing ornamental lettering. Over his left shoulder falls a cloak of intense azure blue, which descends in long, simple folds to below the knees. His legs are clad in blue hose. The juxtaposition of the flat, glittering expanse of brocade with the smooth, luminous blue of the cloak produces an effect of extraordinary chromatic splendour. It contrasts sharply with the restrained brown of the Anthony panel.

The Saint Venantius is the work of Puccio di Simone, and it displays the refined decorative manner that the Florentine painter had developed in the orbit of Bernardo Daddi. The brocade of the tunic is conceived less as a modelled volume than as a precious surface spread across the body, and the elongated, slender proportions and the delicate modelling of the face give the figure a courtly elegance. Unusually for an altarpiece of this kind, the lateral saints are painted on a larger scale than the enthroned Madonna of the central panel, whose court of angels and saints is composed of numerous small figures. The juxtaposition of this polished Florentine youth with Allegretto's austere Anthony on the left wing offers an instructive image of the encounter between the two painters. It is worth noting, finally, that the love of sumptuous textile displayed here would become a hallmark of Allegretto's own mature works in the Marche. The collaboration with Puccio may have been one of the channels through which this taste reached him.

The saint's left hand, drawn back to his waist, gathers the edge of the blue cloak in a gesture of quiet elegance. His stance is slightly swaying, with the weight resting on one leg. This courtly poise, with its hint of Gothic *hanchement*, distinguishes Venantius from the firmly planted Anthony and gives the right wing a lighter, more graceful rhythm. The feet, clad in blue, stand on the same red carpet with gold ornament that appears beneath Saint Anthony, confirming the common ground plane of the two titular saints and the unity of the lateral panels.

The gold ground behind the saint is tooled with the same cusped arcading and punched ornament found on the other panels, following the profile of the arch. The tooling of the halo and the gold-rich brocade of the tunic create a surface of dense, shimmering light around the figure. In the context of a church interior lit by candles and oil lamps, such surfaces would have animated the image with shifting reflections. The saint would thus have appeared to the faithful as a radiant presence emerging from a field of celestial light.

In the gable above, within a trefoil medallion, appears the Annunciate Virgin, veiled in blue, with her hand raised to her breast in a gesture of humble acceptance. She receives the message of the Archangel Gabriel from the opposite wing. The small figure completes the Annunciation that frames the triptych and binds the two lateral panels together across the central Maestà. In this way, the right wing combines the civic, local devotion embodied in Venantius with the universal mystery of the Incarnation announced in its gable, and the altarpiece as a whole appears as a coherent statement of faith addressed to a Fabrianese community.

The next dated work is a much-damaged Madonna and Child, formerly in the Sabatucci collection and now in a private collection, which bears the date 1358 on the base of the throne. On the occasion of the 2021 Fabriano exhibition it was presented as the centre of a triptych whose lateral panels, with Saints Anthony Abbot and John the Evangelist and Saints John the Baptist and Venantius, are in the Museo Diocesano of Fabriano.

### Triptych of 1365: Madonna and Child Enthroned with Donors between Saint Michael the Archangel and Saint Ursula

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-13.jpg" alt="Gothic triptych on gold ground by Allegretto Nuzi, 1365: in the centre the Virgin in a dark blue mantle enthroned before a red cloth of honour, holding the blessing Christ Child, with small kneeling donors at the foot of the throne and a signed inscription along the base; on the left wing the Archangel Michael in armour with sword and shield trampling a dragon; on the right wing Saint Ursula in a rose gown and dark mantle holding a white banner with a red cross and the palm of martyrdom">
  <figcaption>Madonna and Child Enthroned between Saint Michael the Archangel and Saint Ursula, 1365, tempera and gold on panel, 123 x 65.8 cm; left side panel: 91.3 x 41.8 cm; right side panel: 92 x 41.4 cm, Vatican City, Pinacoteca Vaticana, MV.40204.0.0.</figcaption>
</figure>

The single most important dated altarpiece of Allegretto's mature period is the triptych now in the Pinacoteca Vaticana, signed and dated 1365. Its central panel shows the Virgin enthroned with the blessing Child and, at the foot of the throne, the kneeling figures of the donors. It is flanked by Saint Michael the Archangel, in armour with sword and shield, on the left wing, and by Saint Ursula, with banner and the palm of martyrdom, on the right. The painter's signature and the date run along the base of the central panel:

> ALEGRITTUS NUTII ME PINXIT A(NNO) MCCCLXV.

The central panel measures 123 × 65.8 cm, the left wing 91.3 × 41.8 cm and the right wing 92 × 41.4 cm. According to the Vatican catalogue, the altarpiece was probably commissioned by Nofrio di Santi for the chapel of Saints Ursula and Michael the Archangel in the church of Santa Lucia at Fabriano; the kneeling figures have accordingly been read, with due caution, as the family of Onofrio Santi. In 1634 it belonged to the Santi family of Fabriano, who in 1664 donated it to the church of Saints Leonard and Romuald alla Lungara in Rome, demolished in 1863.

By 1800 it was in the collection of Agostino Mariotti (1724–1806), passed by inheritance to Donato Luparelli, and in 1820 was purchased by Pope [Pius VII](/endnotes.html#fn-pope-pius-vii) for the Museo Cristiano of the Biblioteca Apostolica. On 19 December 1908 it entered the Pinacoteca of Pius X. In the central panel the Virgin is built with a powerful plasticity that is still Florentine and close to Orcagna, while the lateral saints already display the heightened taste for the decoration of vestments and ornament that betrays the deepening pull of Sienese art. It is in this work, above all, that the transition from Allegretto's monumental to his decorative manner can be observed in a single object.

#### Central Panel: Madonna and Child Enthroned with Donors

The central panel presents the Virgin as Queen of Heaven in a composition of striking concentration. Beneath a pointed arch, against a burnished gold ground, Mary is seated frontally on a tall throne, and she occupies almost the entire height and breadth of the painted field. Unlike the crowded Maestà of the Washington triptych, with its tiers of angels and saints, the Vatican panel dispenses with the celestial court altogether. The Virgin and Child are alone before the viewer, apart from the small donors at their feet. This reduction to essentials gives the image an iconic austerity that looks back to the great enthroned Madonnas of the Florentine tradition, and it concentrates the whole devotional charge of the altarpiece on the two sacred figures at its centre.

The Virgin wears a voluminous mantle of deep blue, now darkened to a cool, greenish tone in places, which is edged with a gold border. It falls from her head over her shoulders and spreads across her knees in broad, heavy folds that almost reach the lower edge of the panel. A small gold star shines on her right shoulder, the emblem of Mary as *Stella Maris*, and a gold clasp fastens the mantle at her throat. Beneath it, a rose-red gown and a white veil framing the face provide warm accents against the dark mass of the mantle. The face is long and oval, with a straight nose, narrow eyes and a small mouth. Its expression is solemn and withdrawn, addressed less to the Child than to the faithful standing before the altar.

The monumentality of the figure is the most Florentine aspect of the painting. The body is conceived as a single, massive, pyramidal form, and the fall of the mantle is modelled to suggest the weight of the knees and the breadth of the lap beneath. The contour of the figure is firm and continuous, isolating it from the gold ground with sharp clarity. This severe, almost sculptural conception, with its frontality and its simplified volumes, reflects Allegretto's renewed dialogue with the art of Andrea Orcagna and Nardo di Cione, whose hieratic Madonnas dominated Florentine painting in the years after the Black Death.

The Christ Child sits on his Mother's left knee, turned in three-quarter view toward the viewer. He wears a rose-coloured tunic under a paler garment and is barefoot, his small feet resting on the folds of the Virgin's mantle. With his right hand raised he gives the blessing, a gesture that transforms the intimate group of mother and son into an image of divine authority addressed to the donors and to the congregation. The Virgin supports him with her hands, one resting across his body, in a gesture of protection that also presents him to the viewer. The Child's rounded head, with its short curls and gravely attentive expression, gives him the air of a small sovereign rather than an infant.

Behind the Virgin rises the throne, whose high back is covered by a cloth of honour of deep red patterned with gold. The fabric falls over the uprights on either side of the seat and frames the dark blue silhouette of the Virgin with a field of warm colour. The contrast of red and blue against gold is the chromatic key of the panel. It also anticipates the rich textiles that fill the lateral wings. The patterning of the cloth, rendered through a combination of painted ornament and gold, shows the growing attention to precious surface that would characterise Allegretto's works of the later 1360s.

At the foot of the throne, on a scale drastically reduced in relation to the sacred figures, kneel the donors. On the left, figures in dark, hooded garments turn in prayer toward the Virgin; on the right, a group of smaller figures in red and patterned clothing kneels in corresponding devotion. The difference in scale follows the hierarchical conventions of fourteenth-century painting. It expresses the distance between the heavenly and the earthly orders, while at the same time placing the patrons permanently within the sacred space, under the direct protection of the Mother of God. All of them raise their faces toward the enthroned group above, and this upward gaze is precisely the attitude that the inscription between them puts into words. If, as the Vatican catalogue suggests, the altarpiece was commissioned by Nofrio di Santi, these figures would represent members of his family, commemorated before the altar of their chapel in an act of perpetual supplication.

On the front of the throne's step, between the two groups of donors, a painted cartouche opened by a red initial carries the first two verses of Psalm 122 (123 in the Hebrew numbering):

> Ad te levavi oculos meos, qui habitas in celis,

"Unto thee lift I up mine eyes, O thou that dwellest in the heavens."

The psalm, one of the gradual psalms or Songs of Ascents, continues with a double image of dependence: as the eyes of servants look to the hands of their masters, and as the eyes of a maidservant look to the hands of her mistress, so the eyes of the faithful look to God until he has mercy on them. The choice of text is remarkably apt. The kneeling donors literally enact the words of the psalm, lifting their eyes toward the Child enthroned in glory, and the reference to the ancilla looking to her domina acquires a particular resonance before an image of the Virgin, the handmaid of the Lord who has become the Lady of heaven.

The inscription thus serves as a prayer spoken on the donors' behalf, a plea for mercy that the image keeps perpetually before God. Beneath it, along the lower edge of the panel, runs the painter's signature in Roman capitals:

> ALEGRITTUS NUTII ME PINXIT A(NNO) MCCCLXV,

"Allegretto di Nuzio painted me in the year 1365."

The phrasing in which the painting itself speaks, me pinxit, is a common formula in Trecento signatures. Allegretto signed a considerable number of his works, and this habit reflects the professional self-awareness of a master who ran a successful workshop in his native city.

The central panel thus summarises the character of the whole altarpiece. It is a devotional image of great simplicity and dignity, built on the monumental Florentine model, but enriched by the red and gold of the cloth of honour and by the precise record of its commission. The altarpiece was painted in the same year in which, according to local sources, the Dominicans began to rebuild their church in Fabriano under the title of Santa Lucia Novella. The chapel of Saint Ursula in that church preserves an extensive fresco cycle by Allegretto and his workshop. Whether the triptych was made for the same devotional setting is a question that the documentary record, as far as it can be verified here, does not allow us to settle.

#### Left Panel: Saint Michael the Archangel

Saint Michael the Archangel stands beneath the pointed arch of the left wing, filling the narrow field almost from top to bottom. He is represented as the captain of the heavenly host: a youthful warrior in armour, with outspread wings, triumphing over the dragon that writhes beneath his feet. His figure faces the viewer almost frontally, with his head turned slightly toward the central panel, and his firm, planted stance gives him an air of watchful guardianship. As the leader of the angels who cast Satan out of heaven, according to the Book of Revelation, and as the protector of souls at the hour of death, Michael was among the most widely venerated saints of the Middle Ages.

The archangel's head is youthful and round, framed by short, curly auburn hair and a gold halo. The features are regular, with wide-set eyes and a small, firm mouth, and the expression is calm and composed. This impassivity is characteristic of the medieval representation of Michael, whose victory over evil is presented not as a struggle but as an effortless manifestation of divine power. Behind him rise two great wings with dark, finely articulated feathers, which fill the upper part of the arch on either side of the head and give the figure an imposing breadth.

Michael holds an upright sword in his right hand, its blade rising along his side and its gold hilt resting near his waist. On his left arm he bears a large shield decorated with a pink ground and fine patterned ornament, which spreads across the right half of the figure like a great fan. Sword and shield are the traditional attributes of the warrior archangel, and they define him as the defender of the Church and of the faithful. The shield's delicate colour and ornate surface, however, suggest a ceremonial rather than a martial object, in keeping with the courtly refinement of the whole figure.

The archangel's costume is one of the most elaborate passages in Allegretto's work. He wears a cuirass of pale, cream-coloured armour, modelled to the form of the chest, with a red sash tied at the waist. A skirt of strips edged with dark fringes hangs from the cuirass, and a band of red and gold runs across the lower edge. A dark mantle falls from his shoulders behind the body, and his legs are protected by pale greaves. This fanciful armour, derived from the antique military costume that painters of the period used for warrior saints, allows Allegretto to display his talent for the depiction of ornament and texture across a large area of the panel.

Beneath the archangel's feet lies the dragon, a dark, serpentine creature coiling across the red ground of the lower field. Its sinuous body and small head contrast with the vertical stability of the saint who stands upon it. The motif illustrates the passage of the Apocalypse in which Michael and his angels fight against the dragon, the ancient serpent, and cast him down from heaven. In the context of a funerary or family chapel, the image of the conqueror of Satan would also have evoked Michael's role as the weigher and guide of souls at the moment of judgement.

Stylistically, the Saint Michael shows the direction Allegretto's art was taking in the mid-1360s. The figure retains the solid, frontal construction learned from Florentine painting, but the emphasis has shifted from the modelling of volume to the play of decorative detail across the surface. The pale armour, the fringed skirt, the patterned shield and the dark wings together create a composition of rich contrasts. Here the Sienese love of ornament, which the painter had absorbed from the Lorenzetti, begins to prevail over the Florentine gravity of the central Madonna.

The red ground on which the saint stands establishes a common base line with the right wing, where Saint Ursula stands on a similar field. The two lateral figures are thus set on a shared ground plane, distinct from the throne and step of the central panel. The gold background, framed by the pointed arch, isolates the archangel in the same abstract, luminous space as the Virgin, affirming that the three panels, although separately framed, belong to a single celestial realm.

In the programme of the altarpiece, Saint Michael occupies the place of honour at the Virgin's right hand. If the triptych was indeed made for a chapel dedicated to Saints Ursula and Michael, as the Vatican catalogue suggests, the two lateral figures would represent the titular saints of the chapel itself, presenting the donors to the Virgin and Child. The warrior archangel, protector against evil and guide of souls, would then have served as the particular patron under whose protection the Santi family placed its hopes of salvation.

#### Right Panel: Saint Ursula

Saint Ursula stands beneath the pointed arch of the right wing as the counterpart to Saint Michael. According to the legend popularised in the thirteenth century by the *Golden Legend*, Ursula was a British princess who, betrothed to a pagan prince, undertook a pilgrimage to Rome accompanied by eleven thousand virgins. On the return journey she was martyred with her companions by the Huns at Cologne. Her cult enjoyed immense popularity in the late Middle Ages, and in Fabriano it is attested by the extensive fresco cycle of her legend painted by Allegretto and his workshop in the chapel of Saint Ursula in the Dominican church.

The saint is represented as a young woman standing in a slightly swaying pose, with her body turned toward the central panel. Her face is oval and pale, with delicate features, and her hair is gathered around the head. The expression is serene and withdrawn, conveying the composure of the martyr rather than the drama of her death. A gold halo frames her head, and the figure is set against the gold ground of the arch, which isolates her in the same abstract, luminous space as the Virgin and the archangel.

In her right hand Ursula holds a staff bearing a white banner marked with a red cross, one of her characteristic attributes. The banner, the symbol of Christ's victory over death, identifies her as a leader of the faithful and alludes to her role as the head of the great company of virgins who followed her to martyrdom. In her left hand she holds the palm, the universal emblem of martyrdom. Together, the two attributes define her as a victorious martyr, whose death is understood as a triumph rather than a defeat.

Ursula wears a long gown of rose-pink, which falls to her feet in simple, vertical folds, and over it a mantle of dark blue lined and bordered with gold. The mantle, drawn across the front of the body and gathered at the side, frames the figure with a band of dark colour. The contrast between the delicate rose of the gown and the deep blue of the mantle recalls the palette of the central Madonna, but inverted, and establishes a chromatic dialogue across the altarpiece. The gold border of the mantle, following its edges in continuous curves, gives the figure an elegant linear rhythm.

Compared with the archangel on the left wing, Ursula's figure is simpler and more restrained. The gown is barely modelled, and the figure is defined above all by its outline and by the contrast of colour fields. In this respect it demonstrates more clearly than any other part of the altarpiece the shift in Allegretto's manner toward a flatter, more linear style. The slender, elongated proportions and the gentle sway of the body give Ursula a courtly grace that anticipates the elegance of the International Gothic.

The saint's pose and gesture are directed toward the centre. Her body turns toward the Virgin and Child, and the banner leans slightly inward, so that the right wing, like the left, participates in the sacred conversation unfolding in the central panel. This orientation of the lateral figures, standard in Trecento triptychs, turns the three separately framed panels into a single devotional unity. It also creates a visual movement that leads the viewer's eye from the wings toward the enthroned Madonna.

Beneath the saint's feet extends the same red ground found on the left wing, which gives the two titular saints a common base. The lower hem of her gown falls in soft folds over this field, and the continuity of the red ground between the wings balances the composition and frames the central panel. The symmetry between the two lateral figures, the armoured archangel and the virgin martyr, is thus not only compositional but chromatic: the dark mantles and the rose tones of both figures correspond across the altarpiece.

In the context of the altarpiece's presumed destination, Ursula represents the second titular saint of the chapel of Saints Ursula and Michael. As the leader of a great company of virgins, she was invoked as a protector of young women and of a good death, which made her an especially fitting patron for a family chapel. The pairing of Michael and Ursula, the warrior of heaven and the virgin martyr, gives the altarpiece a complementary programme. Both saints are represented as victors over evil and death, and both present the donors, gathered at the foot of the throne, to the Virgin and Child as their intercessors.

The year 1366 yields two works. The first is the polyptych in the Palazzo Comunale at Apiro, in the province of Macerata, today displayed in the council chamber. It is composed of five gabled compartments on a gold ground, with the Madonna and Child enthroned in the centre between Saints Catherine of Alexandria and [Francis of Assisi](/endnotes.html#fn-francis-assisi) on the left, and Saints Martin and Lucy on the right.

An inscription in Gothic letters on the base of the throne records the commission:

> HOC OPUS FECIT FIERI FRATER OFREDUTIUS GUALTERUTII SUB ANNO DOMINI MCCCLXVI,

"Friar Ofreduzio di Gualteruccio had this work made in the year of the Lord 1366."

In the central panel the contour of the Virgin's mantle falls vertically from shoulder to lap, a motif found repeatedly in the Madonnas of Orcagna. The ministry's catalogue observes that the references to Daddi and to Sienese painting remain evident. Although the hems of the garments now fall rigidly rather than in sinuous curves, the faces, such as those of the Child and of Saint Catherine, are still soft and delicately modelled, in the memory of Ambrogio Lorenzetti. The second work of that year is the Madonna of Humility in the Pinacoteca of San Severino Marche.

### Polyptych of 1366: Madonna and Child Enthroned with Saints Catherine of Alexandria, Francis of Assisi, Martin and Lucy

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-19.jpg" alt="Gothic polyptych on gold ground by Allegretto Nuzi, 1366, in five pointed compartments: in the centre the Virgin in a dark mantle over a red gown, enthroned on a pink marble throne, holding the Christ Child in a yellow tunic, with a small Crucifix in the gable; on the left Saint Catherine, crowned, in pink, with palm and wheel, and Saint Francis in a brown habit showing the stigmata and holding a cross; on the right Saint Martin in a yellow tunic cutting his red cloak with a sword for a small half-naked beggar, and Saint Lucy in yellow and lilac holding a burning lamp and a palm; small half-length Franciscan saints in the side gables">
  <figcaption>Madonna and Child Enthroned between Saints Catherine of Alexandria, Francis of Assisi, Martin and Lucy, 1366, tempera and gold on panel, Apiro (Macerata), Palazzo Comunale, Sala Consiliare.</figcaption>
</figure>

#### Central Panel: Madonna and Child Enthroned

The central panel, taller and broader than the four lateral compartments, presents the Virgin enthroned as Queen of Heaven beneath a round-headed arch set within a steep triangular gable. Mary is seated frontally at the axis of the polyptych, and her figure fills almost the entire painted field, from the halo that touches the arch to the hem of the mantle spreading across the base of the throne. As in the Vatican triptych of the previous year, Allegretto dispenses with angels and attendant saints within the central panel. The Virgin and Child appear alone before the viewer, and the whole devotional weight of the altarpiece is concentrated on their encounter.

The Virgin wears a mantle that now appears almost black, fastened at the breast and edged with a narrow gold border. It envelops her head and shoulders and falls in a vast, unbroken mass over her knees to the step of the throne. The present darkness of the mantle probably results in part from the alteration of the original blue pigment over time, a common phenomenon in Trecento panels. Beneath it she wears a bright vermilion gown, visible at the breast and across the lap, and a gold star shines on her right shoulder, the emblem of Mary as *Stella Maris*. Her face is long and pale, with narrow, almond-shaped eyes, a straight nose and a small mouth, and she gazes out toward the viewer with a grave, slightly melancholy expression.

The most distinctive feature of the figure is the treatment of the mantle's contour. Its edge, lined with white, falls vertically from the Virgin's left shoulder toward the lap and then sweeps across the knees in a broad curve that exposes the pale lining. This motif, as the Enciclopedia dell'Arte Medievale observes, recurs in many Madonnas by Orcagna, and it testifies to Allegretto's continuing dialogue with Florentine painting after the Black Death. The strong vertical and the broad curve give the figure a simple, monumental geometry, in which the dark mantle acts as a frame for the brilliant red of the gown and the golden yellow of the Child.

The Christ Child sits on his Mother's left arm, turned toward her with his cheek pressed against hers. He wears a tunic of golden yellow and is barefoot. With his right hand he grasps the neckline of the Virgin's gown, a gesture of childlike affection drawn from the Sienese tradition of the *Madonna della tenerezza*, while the Virgin supports him with her right hand laid gently across his body. The Child's round face, with its large eyes and softly modelled cheeks, looks outward with an expression of solemn curiosity. The intimacy of the group tempers the hieratic frontality of the enthroned Virgin with a note of human tenderness.

The throne is rendered as a piece of carved architecture in pale pink and lilac stone. Its sides are shaped into volutes and its uprights are articulated with mouldings and panels. Behind the Virgin hangs a cloth of honour of red patterned with gold, whose warm colour echoes her gown and sets off the dark mass of the mantle. The perspective of the throne is not systematic, but the projecting armrests and the stepped base create a convincing sense of depth. This attention to the three-dimensional structure of the seat recalls the Florentine thrones of the Giottesque tradition, from which Allegretto had learned the construction of pictorial space.

At the base of the throne, on the pale surface of the step, runs the commissioning inscription in Gothic minuscule:

> hoc opus fecit fieri frater Ofredutius Gualterutii sub anno domini MCCCLXVI,

"Friar Ofreduzio di Gualteruccio had this work made in the year of the Lord 1366."

The ministry's catalogue records the date as DCCCLVI, but this is evidently a misreading, since the photograph shows the numerals MCCCLXVI. Below, on the front of the green-edged step, further lettering in capitals can be discerned in three sections; it presumably contains the painter's signature but is only partly legible in the photograph. The inscription identifies the donor as a friar, and the Franciscan character of the saints in the lateral compartments suggests that he belonged to the Order of Friars Minor.

In the gable above the arch, against the gold ground, appears a small Crucifix. The body of Christ hangs on a slender cross that rises from a small red base. The motif, far simpler than the Crucifixion scenes in the gables of the Washington triptych, is presented almost as an object of devotion in its own right, a painted crucifix set above the image of the Virgin. As in Allegretto's other altarpieces, the Crucifixion crowns the image of the Incarnation below. The Child in his Mother's arms is thus seen in the light of his future sacrifice, and the vertical axis of the central panel joins the beginning and the end of the story of salvation.

The central panel shows Allegretto at the moment of his full maturity. The monumental construction of the Virgin, with her Orcagnesque contour and her massive, geometric silhouette, belongs to the Florentine tradition. The tender embrace of the Child and the softly modelled faces recall the Sienese art of Ambrogio Lorenzetti. The flat, bright fields of colour and the reduction of ornament point toward the linear, simplified style of the painter's last years. The panel thus embodies in a single image the confluence of Florentine and Sienese forms that the Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani identified as the essence of Allegretto's art.

#### Inner Left Panel: Saint Francis of Assisi

Saint Francis occupies the place of honour at the Virgin's right hand, immediately beside the central panel, and his presence defines the Franciscan character of the whole polyptych. He stands frontally beneath a round-headed arch, against the gold ground, on a floor of pale yellow before a band of red. His body is enveloped in the brown habit of the Friars Minor, girded with a knotted white cord whose long end falls almost to the hem. The founder of the order is presented not as a narrative protagonist but as an iconic figure, the *alter Christus* whose life and wounds conformed him to the crucified Lord.

The saint's head is shown with the tonsure, short brown hair and a short beard, and the cowl of the habit is gathered around his neck. His face is lean and gaunt, with prominent cheekbones, deep-set eyes and a serious, penetrating gaze directed at the viewer. The features are more individualised than those of the idealised female saints, and they convey the ascetic severity of the Poverello. The gold halo, finely tooled, frames the head and isolates it against the gold ground.

The principal subject of the image is the stigmata. Francis raises his right hand to show the wound in its palm, and with the same gesture he opens the habit at his side to reveal the wound in his chest, marked by a small red stain. His feet, visible beneath the hem, also bear the marks of the nails. The display of the wounds received on Mount La Verna in 1224 was the central element of Franciscan iconography, since it presented the saint as a living image of Christ crucified. It also links the figure directly to the Crucifix in the gable of the central panel, establishing a visual dialogue between the wounded saint and the crucified Christ.

In his left hand Francis holds a slender red cross on a staff, with trefoil terminals at the ends of the arms. The cross is at once an attribute of the saint's devotion to the Passion and an emblem of the Franciscan mission of preaching. Its bright red contrasts with the brown of the habit and echoes the red of the Virgin's gown and of the cloth of honour, creating a chromatic link between the lateral and central panels.

The treatment of the habit is exemplary of Allegretto's mature manner. The brown fabric falls in long, vertical folds, modelled through gradations of warm and dark tones, and the sleeves, wide and heavy, hang from the arms in simple forms. The figure has a solid, columnar presence, and the volume of the body beneath the habit is suggested with restraint. Yet the modelling is less emphatic than in the Florentine works of the 1350s, and the folds tend toward a linear regularity that anticipates the simplified forms of the painter's last years.

In the gable above the arch appears a half-length Franciscan friar, tonsured and holding a red book. He is probably Saint Anthony of Padua, the most celebrated saint of the order after its founder, whose attribute is the book of Scripture he preached. The identification cannot be confirmed from the attribute alone, however. The figure stands against the gold ground of the gable, above the arch, in a format repeated across all four lateral compartments, and the four small figures together constitute a secondary register of Franciscan saints.

The juxtaposition of Francis with the Virgin carries a particular significance in the Franciscan tradition, which cultivated an intense devotion to Mary as the humble mother of the poor Christ. The saint who had embraced absolute poverty in imitation of Christ stands beside the Mother who had given birth to him in poverty. In an altarpiece commissioned by a Franciscan friar, the figure of the founder also served as a reminder of the order's spiritual ideals and as a model for the community gathered before the altar.

The Saint Francis is one of the best-preserved panels of the polyptych, and it displays Allegretto's gift for the grave, dignified single figure. The severity of the image, its sober palette and its direct frontality give it a devotional intensity that sets it apart from the more elegant female saints of the outer compartments. It is a figure of powerful simplicity, in which the essential message of Franciscan spirituality, the conformity to Christ crucified, is conveyed with economy and conviction.

#### Outer Left Panel: Saint Catherine of Alexandria

Saint Catherine of Alexandria occupies the outer left compartment, beneath a round-headed arch set within a steep gable. According to her legend, she was a royal princess of Alexandria, learned in philosophy, who confuted the pagan scholars summoned by the emperor Maxentius, was condemned to be broken on a spiked wheel and, when the wheel was shattered by divine intervention, was beheaded. Her cult was among the most widespread of the Middle Ages. She was the patron of philosophers and scholars, and a model of wisdom and virginity.

The saint is represented as a young woman standing in a slightly swaying pose, with her head turned in profile toward the central panel. She wears a crown of gold set with red and blue stones, the sign of her royal birth, and her hair is gathered at the nape. Her face is pale and delicate, with a long, straight nose, a small mouth and a gentle, contemplative expression. As the ministry's catalogue notes, her face, like that of the Child, retains a soft, delicate modelling that recalls Ambrogio Lorenzetti.

Catherine wears a long, loose gown of pale rose-pink, which falls from her shoulders to her feet in broad folds. Its hem and sleeves are edged with narrow gold borders, and it is gathered at one side to reveal a glimpse of a yellow lining. The simple, almost unornamented garment contrasts with the richly patterned textiles of Allegretto's later works. Its pale colour gives the figure a luminous, ethereal quality against the gold ground.

In her left hand the saint holds the palm, the universal emblem of martyrdom, which rises beside her head in a tall, slender form. Her right hand rests on the rim of the great wheel that stands at her side, its spokes and felloe rendered in brown and gold. The wheel, the instrument of her intended martyrdom, is her most characteristic attribute. Its large circular form fills the right half of the compartment and balances the vertical figure of the saint.

The construction of the figure shows the direction of Allegretto's mature style. The body is slender and elongated, and the fall of the gown is conceived in long, rhythmic folds that emphasise the vertical axis. The modelling of the drapery is soft and restrained, and the contours are clear and continuous. The figure is defined above all by its outline and by the harmony of its pale colours, in a manner that anticipates the linear elegance of the International Gothic.

In the gable above the arch appears a half-length female saint in the dark habit and veil of a nun, holding a red object before her. She is presumably a Franciscan saint, possibly Saint [Clare](/endnotes.html#fn-chiara-assisi), the founder of the Poor Clares, or another female saint of the order. A similar figure appears in the gable of the outer right compartment, so that the two nuns frame the polyptych at its extremities. Their precise identities should be verified.

Catherine's placement at the outer left of the polyptych, beside Saint Francis, reflects the hierarchical arrangement of the altarpiece. The founder of the order occupies the inner place, closer to the Virgin, and the virgin martyr the outer. The female saints on either side, Catherine and Lucy, frame the male saints and the Virgin at the centre. This symmetrical composition, in which virgin martyrs occupy the outer positions, was common in Trecento polyptychs and gave the altarpiece a clear and balanced structure.

The figure of Saint Catherine is one of the most refined in the polyptych. The delicacy of the face, the pale luminosity of the gown and the graceful sway of the body give her an air of courtly elegance. She embodies the ideal of the learned and noble virgin, and her presence in a Franciscan altarpiece reflects the universal appeal of her cult, which crossed the boundaries of religious orders and social classes.

#### Inner Right Panel: Saint Martin

Saint Martin occupies the inner right compartment, at the Virgin's left hand, opposite Saint Francis. According to his legend, Martin, a young soldier in the Roman army, met a nearly naked beggar at the gate of Amiens on a winter's day and, having nothing else to give him, cut his military cloak in half with his sword and gave one part to the poor man. That night Christ appeared to him in a dream wearing the half cloak. Martin later became bishop of Tours and one of the most venerated saints of the Western Church.

Allegretto represents the saint not as a bishop but as the young soldier of the legend, at the moment of his act of charity. Martin is a beardless youth with short, curly golden hair, standing in a slightly swaying pose with his head bent downward toward the beggar at his side. His face is gentle and thoughtful, with lowered eyes, and it expresses the compassion that moved him to share his cloak. The gold halo frames his head against the gold ground of the arch.

The saint wears a knee-length tunic of golden yellow, girded with a dark belt ornamented with gold, and over his shoulders a red cloak lined with pale grey fur, rendered as vair. The fur lining, turned outward over his right shoulder, falls in a long, heavy fold along the left side of the figure. It is a mark of the saint's noble rank, which heightens the significance of his renunciation. The warm red and yellow of the costume echo the colours of the Virgin's gown and of the Child's tunic.

With his right hand Martin draws his sword and cuts through the cloak, while with his left he holds one end of the fabric. At his feet, on a much smaller scale, the beggar, naked apart from a loincloth and with a band around his head, grasps the cloak and pulls it toward himself. The reduction in scale follows the hierarchical conventions of medieval painting and emphasises the distance between the saint and the poor man. The beggar's emaciated body and eager gesture convey the urgency of his need. The image is a moving illustration of the virtue of charity.

The theme of Martin sharing his cloak had a particular resonance in a Franciscan altarpiece. The Franciscan ideal of poverty and charity toward the poor found in Martin's act a model of Christian compassion. It is significant that the saint is placed opposite Francis, who had himself, according to his early biographers, given his clothing to the poor on several occasions. The two saints on either side of the Virgin thus embody the virtue of charity in complementary forms, the one through total renunciation, the other through the gift of half of what he possessed.

The Saint Martin panel has suffered some losses of paint, visible in the areas of exposed ground on the cloak and at the saint's feet, but its composition remains clearly legible.
