The figure is constructed with the solid, columnar form of Allegretto's mature style, and the fall of the tunic and cloak is rendered in long, vertical folds. As the ministry's catalogue notes, the hems of the garments in this polyptych fall rigidly rather than in sinuous curves, a sign of the painter's movement away from the Sienese linearism of his earlier works toward a more simplified, geometric manner.

In the gable above the arch appears a half-length figure of a bishop, wearing a mitre and a red cope over a brown habit, raising his right hand in blessing and holding a crozier. The combination of episcopal vestments and a Franciscan habit points to Saint [Louis of Toulouse](/endnotes.html#fn-louis-toulouse), the Franciscan bishop and member of the Angevin royal house, whose cult was widely promoted by the order in the fourteenth century. The identification is suggested by his dress and should be confirmed.

The Saint Martin is the most narrative of the five panels, and the only one in which a secondary figure appears beside the saint. The inclusion of the beggar transforms the iconic image of the standing saint into a scene of action and emotion, which engages the viewer directly in the drama of charity. This combination of devotional image and narrative episode, rare in the lateral compartments of Trecento polyptychs, testifies to Allegretto's inventiveness within the conventions of the genre.

#### Outer Right Panel: Saint Lucy

Saint Lucy occupies the outer right compartment, corresponding to Saint Catherine on the left. According to her legend, Lucy was a young noblewoman of Syracuse who distributed her dowry to the poor, was denounced as a Christian by her rejected suitor and, after surviving a series of torments, was put to death by the sword under Diocletian. Her cult, centred on the invocation of her protection against diseases of the eyes, was widespread throughout Italy. Her name, derived from the Latin *lux*, light, made her a symbol of spiritual illumination.

The saint is represented as a young woman standing in a slightly swaying pose, with her head turned in profile toward the central panel. Her hair is braided and bound around her head with a band, and her face is pale and delicate, with a long, straight nose, a small mouth and a serene expression. The treatment of the face corresponds closely to that of Saint Catherine, so that the two virgin martyrs appear as a matched pair at the extremities of the polyptych.

Lucy wears a long gown of pale golden yellow, and over it a mantle of lilac and violet lined with a warm colour, which she gathers across her body with her left hand. The mantle falls in broad, heavy folds to her feet, and its cool colour contrasts with the warm yellow of the gown. The combination of yellow and violet creates a chromatic harmony distinct from the pink of Catherine's gown, while maintaining the pale, luminous palette of the female saints.

In her right hand the saint holds a small vessel with a flame, a burning lamp that alludes to her name and to the light of faith. In her left hand, together with the gathered folds of the mantle, she holds the palm of martyrdom, which rises beside her body. The lamp is the attribute that distinguishes Lucy from other virgin martyrs, and its small flame echoes, on a reduced scale, the symbolism of light associated with her cult.

The figure of Lucy displays the same qualities of linear elegance as that of Catherine. The body is slender and elongated, and the fall of the mantle is conceived in long, rhythmic folds. The gathering of the mantle across the body creates a pattern of curves and diagonals that animates the figure and gives it a sense of graceful movement. The treatment of the drapery, with its restrained modelling and clear contours, belongs to the linear and decorative style of Allegretto's maturity.

In the gable above the arch appears a half-length female saint in the dark habit and veil of a nun, holding a red book. She corresponds to the nun in the gable of the outer left compartment, and she too is presumably a Franciscan saint, perhaps Saint Clare. As with the other gable figures, her identity should be verified. The upper edge of this compartment and its gable show visible damage to the wood and gilding.

Lucy's placement at the outer right, corresponding to Catherine on the left, completes the symmetrical structure of the polyptych. The two virgin martyrs frame the composition, while the two male saints, Francis and Martin, occupy the inner positions beside the Virgin. This arrangement reflects both the hierarchy of the saints and the balance of the composition. The choice of Lucy, whose legend emphasised the distribution of her wealth to the poor, also harmonises with the Franciscan theme of charity embodied by Francis and Martin.

The figure of Saint Lucy completes the programme of the Apiro polyptych, in which the Virgin and Child are surrounded by saints who embody the virtues of charity, poverty and faithful witness. The polyptych, commissioned by a Franciscan friar in 1366, is a coherent expression of Franciscan spirituality, articulated through a carefully chosen group of saints. In its combination of monumental construction, tender expression and linear elegance, it shows Allegretto at the height of his maturity.

The second is the Madonna of Humility now in the Pinacoteca Civica "P. Tacchi Venturi" at San Severino Marche, which comes from the church of San Domenico and bears Allegretto's signature and the date 1366. Despite heavy restoration in the nineteenth century, the art historian [Lionello Venturi](/scholars.html#venturi-lionello) in 1915 was able to reconstruct the fragmentary inscription on the base of the painting and to secure the attribution to Allegretto. He further proposed that this panel served as the model for the majority of the works of Francescuccio Ghissi. After being lent to the 2021 Fabriano exhibition *Oro e colore nel cuore dell'Appennino*, the panel was restored by Giacomo Maranesi of Fermo in a workshop set up within the galleries of the Pinacoteca, so that visitors could follow the work as it progressed. These two dated works of 1366, together with the Vatican triptych of the previous year, define the artist's fully mature phase, in which the forms are gentled into a more linear and decorative language and the figures increasingly simplified.

### Our Lady of Humility, 1366

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-12.jpg" alt="Gothic panel by Allegretto Nuzi, 1366: the Virgin of Humility seated low on a large red cushion with gold pattern and tassels, nursing the Christ Child held in her arms; she wears a dark blue mantle decorated with gold vine leaves and grapes over a red gown patterned in gold; a crown of twelve dark stars surrounds her head, a crescent moon lies at her feet, and a cusped arch in raised gilding frames the scene on a gold ground; a damaged inscription runs along the lower edge">
  <figcaption>Madonna of Humility (after the 2022 restoration), 1366, tempera and gold on panel, 164.5 × 125 cm, San Severino Marche, Pinacoteca Civica P. Tacchi Venturi, from the church of San Domenico, San Severino Marche.</figcaption>
</figure>

The San Severino panel is one of the most monumental representations of the Madonna of Humility painted in central Italy in the fourteenth century. It is also the image through which Allegretto contributed most decisively to the diffusion of this type in the Marche. The Virgin does not sit on a throne but low upon a great cushion laid on the ground, the posture that gives the type its name, from the Latin *humilitas*, rooted in *humus*, earth. The iconography, which emerged in the early decades of the century and spread rapidly through the preaching and patronage of the mendicant orders, presented the Mother of God as humble and close to the faithful, in deliberate contrast to the regal Maestà. The panel's provenance from the Dominican church of San Severino places it squarely within this mendicant devotional culture. Its scale, more than one and a half metres high, shows that the humble Virgin could command an altar with all the authority of an enthroned Queen.

The Virgin is seated in three-quarter view, turned toward the left, with her body forming a broad, stable pyramid that rises from the spreading hem of her mantle to the gently inclined head. She wears a mantle of deep blue, lined with gold and edged with a gold border, which covers her head and falls across her knees to the ground. Beneath it, a red gown patterned with gold is visible at the breast and along the arm, and a white veil frames her face. The face is long and oval, with a straight, narrow nose, almond-shaped eyes and a small mouth, and its expression, melancholy and inward, is directed toward the viewer rather than toward the Child. This tension between the tender action of nursing and the grave, contemplative gaze is characteristic of Allegretto's Madonnas and gives the image its solemn devotional tone.

The Humility type is here combined with that of the nursing Virgin, the *Madonna del latte*. The Child, held across his Mother's lap in her right arm, clasps her breast with both hands and suckles, with his eyes turned outward toward the viewer. He is wrapped in a pink cloth edged with gold that leaves his chest and legs bare, and around his neck he wears a necklace of red coral. Coral was traditionally worn by children as an amulet against evil, and in religious painting it also carried an allusion to the blood of Christ's Passion, so that the necklace quietly foreshadows the sacrifice to come. On one finger of the hand with which the Virgin supports the Child, a ring can be seen. It is a small but eloquent detail, which may be read as a sign of Mary's role as the bride of Christ, the *Sponsa* of the Song of Songs. Together, the nursing, the coral and the ring enrich the intimate image with layers of theological meaning.

Around the Virgin's head, painted on the gold ground, a crown of twelve dark, eight-pointed stars forms an arc from shoulder to shoulder, and at the lower left of the panel, beneath the hem of her mantle, lies a crescent moon. These attributes derive from the vision in the Book of Revelation of the woman clothed with the sun, with the moon under her feet and a crown of twelve stars on her head. The fusion of the humble, nursing Virgin with the Woman of the Apocalypse, which had been established in Sienese painting around the circle of [Simone Martini](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Simone-Martini.html), gives the image a cosmic dimension. The Mother seated on the ground is at the same time the Queen of Heaven, crowned with stars and exalted above the changeable world symbolised by the moon.

The great cushion on which the Virgin sits is one of the most striking elements of the composition. Rendered in brilliant red and covered with a regular pattern of gold trefoils, it spreads across the whole width of the panel, and its corners are gathered into tassels. Behind it a green band, perhaps a low wall or a receding ground, separates it from the gold background, while in front a pale yellow ground stretches to the lower edge. The cushion provides a warm, luminous field against which the dark blue of the mantle stands out with great force. Its patterned surface, together with the gold decoration of the gown and mantle, demonstrates the love of precious textiles that the Pinacoteca's own description associates with Sienese painting.

The decoration of the garments is among the richest in Allegretto's oeuvre. The deep blue mantle is strewn with gilded vine leaves and clusters of grapes, linked by fine, curling tendrils that spread across its whole surface. The motif carries a clear Eucharistic meaning. It alludes to the wine of the Mass and to Christ's words "I am the true vine," and it accords with the nursing that nourishes the Child, so that the Virgin's mantle becomes a garment woven with the promise of the sacrament. The red gown is patterned with rows of gold palmettes, and the borders of the mantle and gown are defined by bands of gold. This ornament is not merely applied to the figure but spread across its whole surface, turning the Virgin's body into a field of shimmering pattern. Here the linear, decorative direction of Allegretto's mature style appears with particular clarity.

The figure is framed by a cusped arch in raised gilding, which rises from the level of the cushion and follows the outline of the panel in a series of rounded lobes. This moulded arch, modelled in relief on the gesso ground and then gilded, gives the image the character of a sacred niche and articulates the flat expanse of the gold ground. Along the lower edge of the panel, on a red band between green borders, runs the inscription in Roman capitals:

> [AL]EGRE[C]T[US DE FA]B[R]IANO ME PINXIT AN(N)O D[(OMI)NI MCCC]LXV[I?],

"Allegretto of Fabriano painted me in the year of the Lord 1366."

Although much of the lettering has been lost, the surviving letters are sufficient to identify the painter. As on the Macerata triptych of 1369, Allegretto signs here not with his patronymic, as on the Vatican triptych and the Urbino Madonna, but with the name of his native city, a proud declaration of his Fabrianese identity in a work destined for a neighbouring town. The spelling of the name, with E in place of the I of Alegrittus found in his other signatures, is a variant typical of the fluid orthography of the period. Venturi's reconstruction of this fragmentary inscription in 1915 allowed him to establish the painter's authorship and the date, and it remains the basis for the work's place in Allegretto's chronology.

The panel has suffered considerable damage over the centuries. The most extensive losses affect the lower part of the mantle and the inscription band, where the paint has flaked away to reveal the preparation beneath, and the gold of the background is worn in many places, exposing the red bole. The heavy restorations of the nineteenth century recorded in the literature further complicated the reading of the surface. The recent intervention by Giacomo Maranesi, carried out after the 2021 exhibition, has recovered much of the original brilliance of the colours: the ultramarine of the mantle, the vermilion of the gown and cushion, and the delicate pink of the Child's cloth now appear with renewed intensity. The lacunae, as the photograph shows, have been left visible rather than reconstructed, so that the viewer can distinguish clearly between the surviving original paint and the areas of loss. Despite these losses, the grandeur of the composition and the refinement of its execution remain fully legible. As Venturi recognised, the San Severino Madonna became the model for Francescuccio Ghissi's many Madonnas of Humility. It thus stands at the head of one of the most characteristic devotional traditions of Trecento painting in the Marche.

To 1369 belongs the signed and dated triptych in the Duomo di San Giuliano at Macerata, the city's cathedral until 2023. It came from the demolished church of Sant'Antonio Abate in the same town. The central panel shows the Madonna and Child enthroned, with angels arranged in three superimposed tiers and, in the foreground flanking the throne, Saints Anthony Abbot, Rose, Benedict and Catherine of Alexandria. The lateral panels show Saint Julian and Saint Anthony Abbot, and the gables are decorated with the Crucified Christ between Elijah and Moses at the centre and, within quatrefoils, the Annunciate Virgin and the Archangel Gabriel at the sides. The work is of exceptional interest because it repeats, on a slightly larger scale, the composition of the Washington triptych of 1354, which the National Gallery of Art's catalogue describes as practically identical.

The correspondences extend to one of the Washington altarpiece's rarest features: in both, Saint Anthony Abbot appears twice, once among the saints around the throne and again on a lateral panel. Bernard Berenson had already noted the similarity of layout between the two works and observed that the inscriptions of both name a commissioning friar called Giovanni, leading him to suppose a common patron. The subsequent recognition of the near-identity of the two compositions, together with the Antonine dedication of both destinations, lends that hypothesis considerable weight. It now seems likely that the same friar, presumably a member of the Hospital Brothers of Saint Anthony, commissioned both altarpieces fifteen years apart, first for an Antonine church at Fabriano and then for the church of Sant'Antonio Abate at Macerata, and that he asked the painter to repeat the programme devised for the earlier work. The later work, however, is not a simple copy. Whereas the Washington triptych was the product of a collaboration between Allegretto and the Florentine Puccio di Simone, the Macerata version was painted by Allegretto alone. It translates the earlier composition into the idiom of his late style: the flattening of volume is fully evident, the faces are elongated and sharp, and the fabrics are rendered as broad, flat surfaces. The comparison between the two works thus offers a rare opportunity to measure, on the basis of a shared model, the distance Allegretto had travelled between 1354 and 1369.

The last dated work is the Madonna and Child Enthroned now in the Galleria Nazionale delle Marche in the Ducal Palace at Urbino, signed and dated 1372, painted, that is, in the year before the artist's death. The inscription on the base of the throne reads [H]OC OPUS PINXIT ALEGRITTUS NUTII DE FABRIANO ANNO MCCCLXXII. The panel measures 167 by 96 centimetres. It was first recorded by Amico Ricci in 1834 in the collection of Alessandro Castrica at Fabriano, was noted by [Crowe](/scholars.html#crowe-joseph-archer) and [Cavalcaselle](/scholars.html#cavalcaselle-giovanni-battista) in 1864 in the Fornari collection in the same city, and entered the Galleria Nazionale delle Marche in 1914. The panel probably formed the central compartment of a triptych. This is the culminating statement of Allegretto's final manner: the forms are more rigid, the monumentality intense but abstracted, and the decorative surface paramount. It is here, more than anywhere, that scholars have detected the possible influence of contemporary Venetian painting.

Alongside these securely dated panels, the sources place the three panels with Saint [Augustine](/endnotes.html#fn-augustine-of-hippo) between Saint Nicholas of Tolentino and Saint Stephen in the Pinacoteca of Fabriano, long regarded as the artist's final work. Recent studies have shown, however, that the ensemble is not an original triptych but three lateral compartments of a dismembered polyptych, later remounted in triptych form, and probably painted for the [Augustinian](/endnotes.html#fn-augustinian-order) church of Santa Maria Nova in Fabriano. Its date is disputed: some accounts place it about 1362, while others, including reports on the 2021 Fabriano exhibition, date it about 1372. The emphatic decoration of the vestments and the abstraction of the figures accord well with a late date, but the traditional view of the ensemble as Allegretto's last work can no longer be taken for granted.

### Madonna and Child Enthroned, 1372

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-03.jpg" alt="Gothic panel by Allegretto Nuzi, 1372, with a pointed top: the crowned Virgin seated frontally on a pale throne draped with a white patterned cloth, wearing a red gown with gold motifs and a dark mantle covered with gold palmettes; the Christ Child, in a gold tunic with blue sleeves and a blue sash, stands on her lap and touches her cheek while looking out at the viewer; a cusped arch in raised gilding frames the scene on a gold ground, and a signed inscription runs along the base of the throne">
  <figcaption>Madonna and Child Enthroned, 1372, tempera and gold leaf on panel, 167 × 96 cm, Urbino, Galleria Nazionale delle Marche, inv. D24.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Urbino Madonna, painted in 1372, is the last dated work of Allegretto Nuzi and the fullest expression of the style of his final years. Beneath a pointed arch, framed by a cusped arcade modelled in relief and gilded, the Virgin sits enthroned against a burnished gold ground, holding the standing Christ Child on her lap. The panel is unusually tall and imposing, and the figure of the Virgin, seated on a broad throne that fills the lower two thirds of the field, has a commanding monumentality. The Galleria Nazionale's catalogue speaks of a new kind of monumentality in this work, marked by the sharp contours of figures that stand out against the gold ground, isolated in a dimension of solemn and arcane sacredness. The image thus combines the gravity of the enthroned Maestà with an abstract, almost hieratic stillness that sets it apart from the more naturalistic Madonnas of Allegretto's earlier career.

The Virgin is represented as Queen of Heaven. A gold crown with tall, finely punched pinnacles rests on her veiled head, and it is surrounded by a halo tooled with delicate floral ornament. Her face is long and narrow, with a straight, elongated nose, almond-shaped eyes and a small, closed mouth, and its pale flesh is modelled with the soft greenish underlayer of Trecento tempera technique. She inclines her head slightly toward the Child, but her gaze is directed out toward the viewer, grave and withdrawn. This detachment, characteristic of Allegretto's late Madonnas, gives the image the solemn distance of an icon. Motherly intimacy is conveyed through the gestures of the figures rather than through the expression of the face.

The Virgin's costume is the most splendid in Allegretto's oeuvre. She wears a gown of vermilion patterned with rows of gold rosettes and lozenges, and over it a dark mantle, lined with golden yellow and edged with a broad gold border, which is strewn with large gold palmettes arranged in regular rows. The mantle falls from her shoulders and spreads across her knees to the step of the throne, and its lining is turned back across the lap in a broad fold. The gold ornament is spread across the dark surface with little regard for the folds of the fabric, so that the mantle reads less as a modelled garment than as a flat field of precious pattern. The catalogue rightly stresses the rare elegance of this decoration, in which the alternation of red, blue, white and gold is enriched by punchwork.

The Christ Child stands on his Mother's lap, supported by her hands, one at his back and the other holding his feet. He wears a tunic of gold patterned with a fine lattice of small ornaments and lozenges, with blue sleeves and a blue sash tied at the waist, and its scalloped hem falls to his knees. With one arm he reaches up to touch his Mother's cheek, and with the other he rests his hand on her breast, while he turns his face outward toward the viewer. The gesture of the caress, drawn from Byzantine images of the Virgin of Tenderness and absorbed into Tuscan painting, introduces a note of affection into the otherwise solemn composition. It is a characteristic feature of Allegretto's art that the tenderness between Mother and Child is expressed through such delicate gestures, even in his most hieratic images.

The throne is a broad structure of pale green and yellow stone, with square uprights crowned by small, knobbed finials and a stepped base. Its back and sides are hung with a white cloth decorated with small blue and red fleurons, and a red cushion, gathered with tassels, lies on the seat beside the Virgin. The pale tones of the throne and its cloth create a luminous field against which the dark mantle stands out with great force, and the three principal colours of the image, red, dark blue and white, are thus set off by the surrounding gold. The perspective of the throne is simplified, and its masses are treated as flat, geometric planes. This reduction of spatial depth, in comparison with the more convincingly three-dimensional thrones of the Apiro polyptych and the Vatican triptych, is another sign of the abstract tendency of Allegretto's late style.

Along the front of the throne's base, in three sections separated by the projecting corners of the step, runs the inscription in Roman capitals on a dark ground:

> [H]OC OPUS / PINXIT ALEGRITTUS NUTII DE FABRIANO ANNO / MCCCLXXII,

"Allegretto di Nuzio of Fabriano painted this work in the year 1372."

The signature combines the two forms that Allegretto used elsewhere: the patronymic *Nutii*, found on the Vatican triptych of 1365, and the toponym *de Fabriano*, found on the Macerata triptych of 1369 and on the San Severino Madonna. This full formula, the most complete in his oeuvre, gives the last dated work an air of solemn summation. It records not only the painter's name and the date but also his lineage and his native city, as if the master, in the year before his death, wished to leave a complete record of his identity.

In its style, the Urbino Madonna represents the culmination of the tendencies that had been developing in Allegretto's art since the mid-1360s. The plastic modelling of form, learned in Florence, has given way almost entirely to a linear and decorative manner in which the figures are defined by their contours and their surfaces are covered with ornament. The forms are rigid and simplified, and the monumentality of the image is intense but abstract, achieved not through the suggestion of mass and volume but through the scale of the figures and the clarity of their outlines. The Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani suggests that in this final phase Allegretto may have been influenced by the Venetian painting that was then penetrating the Marche, and the flat, richly decorated textiles of the Urbino panel recall the art of Paolo and Lorenzo Veneziano. The Venetian influence, however, remains a hypothesis, and it is not documented by any evidence beyond the style of the works.

The panel probably formed the central compartment of a dismembered triptych, as the Galleria Nazionale's catalogue proposes. Its history can be traced from the early nineteenth century: it was recorded by Amico Ricci in 1834 in the collection of Alessandro Castrica at Fabriano, was noted in 1864 in the Fornari collection in the same city, and entered the Galleria Nazionale delle Marche in 1914. Painted in the year before Allegretto's death in 1373, it can be regarded as his artistic testament. In its fusion of monumental grandeur and decorative splendour, it points toward the courtly art of the International Gothic, which would find its supreme expression in the work of another painter of Fabriano, Gentile, born around 1370. The Urbino Madonna thus closes Allegretto's career while opening the way to the next chapter in the history of the Fabrianese school.

Beyond the dated works stands a large body of panels attributed to Allegretto on stylistic and technical grounds, now dispersed across the museums of Europe and North America — a diaspora that is itself a testament to the esteem in which his shimmering gold grounds were held by collectors from the nineteenth century onward.

### Virgin and Child Enthroned between Saints Bartholomew and Catherine of Alexandria (wing of a diptych)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-20.jpg" alt="Small gabled panel by Allegretto Nuzi on gold ground: the crowned Virgin in a red gown and a dark mantle with gold ornament, seated before a red and gold brocade cloth of honour, holding the Christ Child in a blue tunic who reclines across her lap with his hand at his mouth and a small bird; on the left Saint Bartholomew, bearded, holding a knife and a red book; on the right Saint Catherine, crowned, in yellow and orange, holding a palm; inscriptions on the step of the throne and along the base">
  <figcaption>Virgin and Child Enthroned between Saints Bartholomew and Catherine of Alexandria, c. 1350–70, tempera and gold on panel, 51.5 × 28.2 cm, Berlin, Staatliche Museen, Gemäldegalerie, inv. 1076.</figcaption>
</figure>

This small panel, just over half a metre high, is one wing of a devotional diptych whose other wing showed the Crucifixion. According to the Enciclopedia dell'Arte Medievale, the work appears to belong to Allegretto's early years of activity, although [Fernanda Imondi Romagnoli](/scholars.html#imondi-romagnoli-fernanda), in her 1927 monograph *Allegretto Nuzi pittore fabrianese*, dated it to 1365 on the basis of the inscription on the step of the throne. Its modest dimensions and the pairing of the enthroned Virgin with the Crucifixion identify it as an object for private devotion, a type in which Allegretto excelled. The Berlin diptych was among the loans to the 2021 Fabriano exhibition, and one reviewer observed that the painter's innovations concerned not only style but also type, notably small diptychs and triptychs intended for personal devotion, signs of a spirituality increasingly close to the devotio moderna. The Berlin panel shows how Allegretto could concentrate the monumental forms of his altarpieces into an image of intimate scale.

The Virgin sits frontally on a throne at the centre of the composition, her figure occupying almost the whole height of the painted field. She wears a gold crown set with red stones, surrounded by a finely punched halo, and her fair hair falls beneath a white veil. Her gown is of vermilion sprinkled with small gold stars, an allusion to her title of Queen of Heaven, and her mantle, dark and strewn with large gold leaf-shaped ornaments, falls from her shoulders and spreads across her knees to the step of the throne. The pale lining of the mantle, turned back along its edge, descends vertically from her left shoulder and then sweeps across her lap in a broad curve. This motif, derived from Orcagna and repeated in the Apiro polyptych of 1366, gives the figure a clear and monumental geometry despite the small scale of the panel. The Virgin's long face, with its almond-shaped eyes and small mouth, gazes out toward the viewer with an expression of grave serenity.

The Christ Child, dressed in a pale blue tunic, reclines across his Mother's lap, supported by her hands, and turns his head toward the viewer. He raises one hand to his mouth in a spontaneous childlike gesture, and in the other he holds a small bird by the wing, which hangs down beside the Virgin's hand. The bird is almost certainly a goldfinch, the traditional symbol of the Passion. Its presence here acquires a particular meaning, since the other wing of the diptych showed the Crucifixion. The small bird held by the playful Child thus anticipates the sacrifice depicted on the opposite panel, and the two wings of the diptych, when opened before the devout owner, presented together the beginning and the end of Christ's earthly life.

Behind the throne rises a cloth of honour of red and gold brocade, patterned with scrolling ornament, which extends upward into the gable of the panel in a pointed form. This textile frames the figure of the Virgin and establishes her regal status, while at the same time it fills the upper part of the panel with a dense surface of precious pattern. Its tapering shape echoes the outline of the gable, so that the cloth of honour, the frame and the figure of the Virgin form a series of concentric pointed forms converging on her crowned head. The gold ground visible around the cloth and the punched haloes of the figures complete the effect of luminous preciousness, characteristic of the small devotional panels of the Trecento.

On the left of the throne stands Saint Bartholomew, one of the twelve apostles, who according to tradition was flayed alive in Armenia. He is shown as a man in the prime of life, with curly dark hair and a full beard, turning in three-quarter view toward the Virgin. He holds a knife, the instrument of his martyrdom, and a book bound in red, the attribute of the apostles. He wears a tunic of pale lilac and a mantle whose colours shift from yellow to blue, falling in broad, rhythmic folds to his feet. This treatment of colour, in which the drapery changes hue as it turns in the light, is the technique known as cangiante, and reviewers of the 2021 exhibition singled out the luminous shot-colour effects of this diptych, with its solid yet gentle and elegant bodies.

On the right stands Saint Catherine of Alexandria, crowned as a royal princess, with fair hair and a delicate, pale face turned toward the Virgin. She wears a gown of golden yellow and a mantle of orange-red, which she gathers across her body in a broad fold. In her right hand she holds the palm of martyrdom, and in her left a small object that appears to be a fragment of the spiked wheel on which she was to be broken. The two saints correspond to each other across the throne: Bartholomew, the bearded apostle, and Catherine, the young virgin martyr, represent two complementary forms of holiness, and their presence may reflect the particular devotion of the owner for whom the diptych was painted.

The panel bears two inscriptions. On the upper step of the throne, just beneath the Virgin's feet, runs the Angelic Salutation,

> AVE.GRATIA.PLENA,

"Hail, full of grace,"

the words with which the Archangel Gabriel greeted Mary at the Annunciation. The inscription invites the viewer to address the Virgin with the angel's words, and it links the image to the recitation of the Ave Maria, the prayer central to private Marian devotion. On the base of the panel, in three sections, runs the painter's signature:

> ALEGRICTUS. EN. FABRIANO ME PIMXIT,

"Allegretto of Fabriano painted me."

The spelling Alegrictus is another variant of the painter's name, alongside Alegrittus and Alegrectus; EN stands here for the de of his other signatures, the vernacular preposition slipping into the Latin formula; and pimxit for pinxit is a common scribal slip. As in the Macerata triptych and the San Severino Madonna, Allegretto identifies himself by his native city.

Despite its small size, the Berlin panel displays all the qualities of Allegretto's art. The monumental construction of the Virgin, with its Orcagnesque contour, is combined with the intimate tenderness of the reclining Child, the luminous shot colours of the saints' draperies and the rich decoration of the textiles and punched gold. Reviewers of the 2021 exhibition noted that the preciousness characteristic of Gothic painting is amplified in Allegretto's work through an extraordinary variety of punches and the richness of his fabrics. The date of the diptych remains uncertain: the museum's own range, 1350–70, reflects this uncertainty, as does the divergence between the prevailing early dating and Romagnoli's proposal of 1365. Whatever its precise date, the panel stands as one of the finest examples of Allegretto's devotional painting on a small scale.

### The Crucifixion with the Virgin, Saint John the Evangelist and Mary Magdalene (wing of a diptych)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-21.jpg" alt="Small gabled panel by Allegretto Nuzi on gold ground: Christ dead on the Cross with blood flowing from his wounds down to a rock where a skull lies; Mary Magdalene in red kneels and kisses his feet; the Virgin in a dark mantle stands on the left and Saint John in a blue tunic and pink mantle on the right; above the red titulus INRI the top of the Cross sprouts leaves, and a pelican feeds its young with its blood in a nest in the gable">
  <figcaption>The Crucifixion with the Virgin, Saint John the Evangelist and Mary Magdalene, c. 1350–70, tempera and gold on panel, 51.5 × 28.2 cm, Berlin, Staatliche Museen, Gemäldegalerie, inv. 1078.</figcaption>
</figure>

This panel, identical in size and frame to the Virgin and Child Enthroned between Saints Bartholomew and Catherine in the same collection (inv. 1076), formed the second wing of a small devotional diptych. The pairing of the enthroned Virgin with the Crucifixion was one of the most common arrangements for portable diptychs in fourteenth-century Tuscany and the Marche. When the two wings were opened, the owner would have had before him, side by side, the Incarnation and the Passion, the Mother presenting the Child and the Son offering himself on the Cross. The Enciclopedia dell'Arte Medievale describes the diptych in precisely these terms, and places it among Allegretto's early works. Its intimate dimensions and its richly worked gold identify it as an object for private prayer, of the type that the 2021 Fabriano exhibition recognised as one of Allegretto's most characteristic contributions.

At the centre of the composition, the Cross rises from the rock of Golgotha to the gable of the panel. Christ hangs dead upon it, his head bowed onto his right shoulder, his eyes closed and his long hair falling over his breast. The body is slender and softly modelled, with the ribs and the muscles of the torso indicated by delicate gradations of greenish flesh tone, and a transparent white loincloth, edged with gold, is knotted at the hip. The arms, stretched almost horizontally along the crossbar, sag slightly under the weight of the body, and the legs are bent, with the feet crossed and fixed by a single nail to a small projecting support. This type of the dead Christ, the Christus patiens, had been established in Tuscan painting since the late thirteenth century, and Allegretto treats it here with a restrained pathos that emphasises the silent dignity of death rather than the violence of suffering.

The blood of Christ is given particular prominence. It drips in regular drops from the wounds in his hands, flows in a long red stream from the wound in his side, and runs from the nailed feet down the upright of the Cross to the rock below. There, in a cleft of the rock, lies a skull, the skull of Adam, who according to a widespread medieval legend was buried on the site of the Crucifixion. The blood of the new Adam falling on the bones of the first man expresses in visual form the doctrine of redemption: the sacrifice of Christ cleanses the sin inherited from Adam and opens to humankind the way to salvation. For the owner of the diptych, meditating on the image in prayer, the stream of blood would have evoked the Eucharist, in which the same blood was offered daily on the altar.

Kneeling at the foot of the Cross, Mary Magdalene embraces the wood with both arms and presses her lips to the bleeding feet of Christ. She wears a gown of brilliant vermilion, and her long, loose hair falls down her back, the traditional sign of the repentant sinner who had once dried Christ's feet with her hair. Her gesture of intense, bodily devotion offered the viewer a model of prayer. In the practice of affective meditation promoted by the mendicant orders and by devotional texts such as the Meditationes Vitae Christi, the faithful were urged to imagine themselves present at the Passion and to share the grief of those who had witnessed it. The Magdalene, prostrate at the Cross and kissing the wounds, embodied the compassion and the penitence that such meditation sought to awaken.

On the left stands the Virgin, wrapped in a dark mantle marked with a gold star on the shoulder, over a red gown. She raises her face toward her dying Son with an expression of silent grief, and extends her right hand toward him while her left hand descends toward the kneeling Magdalene, as if to include her in her sorrow. On the right stands Saint John the Evangelist, the beloved disciple, in a blue tunic and a rose-pink mantle edged with a broad gold border. He lowers his head and gazes down in sorrow, while raising his right hand in a gesture of lament and drawing his mantle around him with the left. The two figures, standing on either side of the Cross in symmetrical correspondence, follow the traditional composition of the Crucifixion with the Virgin and Saint John, in which the Mother and the disciple recall Christ's words from the Cross, "Woman, behold thy son" and "Behold thy mother."

Above the titulus, a red tablet inscribed INRI (Iesus Nazarenus Rex Iudaeorum, "Jesus of Nazareth, King of the Jews"), the upright of the Cross sprouts a spray of green leaves, which spreads into the gable. The motif transforms the instrument of death into a living tree, the lignum vitae or Tree of Life. According to medieval tradition, the wood of the Cross came from a sapling of the Tree of Life in Paradise, and the Franciscan theologian [Bonaventure](/endnotes.html#fn-bonaventure), in his treatise Lignum vitae, had taught the faithful to meditate on the Cross as a tree bearing the fruits of salvation. Nesting in the foliage, at the summit of the panel, a pelican pierces her breast with her beak to feed her young with her own blood. The image derives from the Physiologus, the early Christian bestiary, according to which the pelican revives her dead young with her blood. It became one of the most widespread symbols of Christ, who gives his blood to redeem humankind and to nourish the faithful in the Eucharist.

The pelican and the living Cross thus give the Crucifixion a symbolic superstructure that complements the narrative scene below. The blood that flows from Christ's wounds to the skull of Adam is echoed by the blood that the pelican gives to her young, and the dead wood of the Cross is transformed into a flourishing tree. The vertical axis of the panel leads the viewer's eye from death, represented by the skull at the foot of the Cross, to life, represented by the green branches and the nourished young at its summit. This theological programme, compact yet rich, is well suited to an object of private devotion, in which the image served as a support for prolonged meditation rather than for liturgical display.

In its style, the Crucifixion confirms the early dating proposed for the diptych. The graceful proportions of the figures, the gentle modelling of the faces and the soft, rhythmic folds of the draperies recall the art of Bernardo Daddi, whom Allegretto had encountered in Florence in the 1340s. The Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani describes the works of Allegretto's early Daddesque period as graceful works on the path of the Sienese-Florentine compromise established by Daddi. The brilliant colours of the Magdalene's gown and John's mantle stand out against the gold ground, which is enriched by a punched border along the edges of the panel and by the finely tooled haloes of the figures. The panel demonstrates Allegretto's ability to combine narrative clarity, emotional intensity and decorative refinement within the small format of a devotional image, and it forms a worthy counterpart to the enthroned Virgin of the opposite wing.

### Portable Triptych with the Crucifixion, Saints and the Annunciation

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-22.jpg" alt="Small portable triptych by Allegretto Nuzi on gold ground with hinged wings: in the centre Christ on the Cross between the Virgin in blue and Saint John, with Mary Magdalene in red kneeling and embracing the Cross and a Dominican saint presenting a small kneeling woman in green; the blessing Christ in the gable; on the left wing Saint Francis in a brown habit and a bearded saint with staff and red book; on the right wing a bearded saint with book and knife and a female saint in yellow with palm and red cross; the Archangel Gabriel and the Virgin Annunciate in quatrefoils in the wing gables">
  <figcaption>Portable Triptych with the Crucifixion, Saints and the Annunciation, 1350–55, tempera and gold on poplar panel, original frame; left wing 47.2 × 12.8 cm, central panel 47.2 × 25.2 cm, right wing 47.3 × 12.6 cm, Bern, Kunstmuseum, bequest of Adolf von Stürler, Versailles, inv. G 0198.</figcaption>
</figure>

#### Central Panel: The Crucifixion with Saints and a Donor

The Bern triptych is one of the finest surviving examples of the small portable altarpieces for private devotion in which Allegretto excelled. It consists of a central panel with a steep gable and two narrow hinged wings, each almost exactly half the width of the centre. When closed, the wings folded over the central panel to protect it; when opened, they revealed the complete programme, arranged like a miniature church altarpiece. The work retains its original frame and its metal hinges, a rare survival that allows us to understand how such objects were handled and used. The Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani places the tabernacle in Bern, with the Crucifixion, saints and Annunciation, among the works of Allegretto's early Daddesque period, and the museum's dating to 1350–55 accords with this view.

The Crucifixion occupies the whole height of the central panel. Christ hangs on a tall, dark Cross that rises against the gold ground, with his arms stretched along the crossbar, his head bowed onto his right shoulder and his eyes closed in death. His slender body, modelled in pale, greenish flesh tones, is girded by a transparent loincloth, and his crossed feet are nailed to a small support. A red titulus inscribed INRI is fixed above his head. The figure is conceived in the Tuscan type of the *Christus patiens*, and its gentle modelling and elegant proportions recall the Crucifixions of Bernardo Daddi, whose workshop Allegretto had come to know in Florence during the 1340s.

On the left stands the Virgin, wrapped from head to foot in a mantle of deep ultramarine, with a small gold star on the shoulder. She raises her face toward her dead Son with an expression of silent grief, and her hands are hidden in the folds of her mantle. The broad, simple mass of blue gives her figure a monumental gravity that belies the small scale of the panel. Opposite her stands Saint John the Evangelist, youthful and beardless, in a blue tunic and a pale mantle lined with rose. He lowers his gaze and raises his right hand before his chest in a gesture of lament. The two figures, arranged in symmetrical correspondence on either side of the Cross, follow the traditional composition of the Crucifixion with the mourning Mother and the beloved disciple.

At the foot of the Cross, Mary Magdalene kneels in a gown of brilliant vermilion, her long, loose hair falling down her back, and embraces the wood with both arms, raising her face toward the feet of Christ. Her figure, set on the rocky ground of Golgotha, is the most intensely expressive in the composition. The Magdalene's gesture of physical devotion, clasping the instrument of the Passion, provided the owner of the triptych with a model of affective prayer. It invited the viewer to imagine himself or herself at the foot of the Cross and to share the grief and the penitence of the saint.

On the right, in front of Saint John, a tonsured saint in the black mantle and white habit of the Dominican order, with a halo, kneels and extends his hands toward the Cross. Beside him kneels a much smaller female figure in a green gown with her hands joined in prayer, whom he appears to present to the crucified Christ. The Dominican saint is probably Saint [Dominic](/endnotes.html#fn-dominic-guzman) himself, founder of the Order of Preachers, although no distinctive attribute is visible that would exclude another saint of the order, such as Peter Martyr. The small kneeling woman is the donor, the person for whom the triptych was made. Her presence within the sacred scene, under the protection of a Dominican saint, suggests that she had a particular bond with the order, perhaps as a tertiary or as a patron of a Dominican house.

The inclusion of the donor in the Crucifixion itself, rather than at the margins of the image, is significant. It places the owner of the triptych permanently at the foot of the Cross, in the company of the Virgin, Saint John and the Magdalene, and it expresses in visual form the hope of salvation through the blood of Christ. The difference in scale between the donor and the saints follows the hierarchical conventions of fourteenth-century painting. Yet it also conveys a sense of intimacy and humility: the small figure, kneeling among the mourners, appears to participate personally in the scene she contemplates in prayer.

In the gable above the Cross, against the gold ground, appears the half-length figure of Christ, bearded and haloed, raising his right hand in blessing and holding a green book in his left. This image of the risen and glorified Christ, the Redeemer and Judge of the world, completes the programme of the central panel. The dead Christ on the Cross below is thus answered by the living Christ in glory above, and the vertical axis of the panel leads the viewer's eye from the sacrifice of the Passion to the triumph of the Resurrection. The gable is framed by a punched border that follows its outline, and the gold ground of the whole panel is enriched with delicate tooling along the edges.

The central panel exemplifies the qualities of Allegretto's early devotional painting. The composition is clear and balanced, the figures are graceful and softly modelled, and the colours, the blue of the Virgin and John, the vermilion of the Magdalene and the black and white of the Dominican saint, stand out with brilliant clarity against the gold. The small format demands a refined execution, and Allegretto achieves it with a precision worthy of the Florentine masters he had studied. In the Daddesque grace of its figures and the Sienese tenderness of its expressions, the panel embodies the Sienese-Florentine synthesis that the scholarship has identified as the foundation of his art.

#### Left Wing: Saint Francis and Saint James the Greater (?)

The left wing, hinged to the central panel, is a narrow, gabled panel with a pointed arch framing two standing saints against a gold ground. Above the arch, in a quatrefoil set within a dark spandrel decorated with foliate ornament, appears the half-length figure of the Archangel Gabriel. The wing was designed to fold over the left half of the central panel, and its dimensions, almost exactly half the width of the centre, reflect this function. When the triptych stood open on a table or a small altar, the left wing formed with its companion a symmetrical frame for the Crucifixion, turning the whole into a miniature image of a church altarpiece.

On the left of the wing stands Saint Francis of Assisi, in the brown habit of the Friars Minor girded with a cord. He is represented with the tonsure and a short beard, and he raises his right hand before his chest, with the palm turned outward, in a gesture that may display the wound of the stigmata or express devotion. His figure, simple and austere, is modelled with restraint, and its dark silhouette stands out clearly against the gold ground. The presence of Saint Francis beside the Dominican saint in the central panel brings together the founders of the two great mendicant orders, whose preaching had shaped the devotional culture of the fourteenth century.

Beside Francis stands a bearded saint with long hair, dressed in a yellow tunic and a pale rose mantle lined with green. He holds a red book in his left hand and a tall staff in his right. The combination of the book, the attribute of the apostles, and the staff, the pilgrim's staff, suggests that he is Saint James the Greater. The apostle, whose relics were venerated at Santiago de Compostela, was the patron of pilgrims, and his image was widespread in fourteenth-century Italy. The identification, however, is based on these attributes alone and should be confirmed.

The two saints are placed side by side on a pale ground, turned slightly toward the central panel, so that they appear to participate in the scene of the Crucifixion. Their haloes, finely punched, overlap the edge of the arch, and their figures fill almost the entire height of the wing. The difference between the dark, monastic Francis and the colourful, elegant apostle creates a chromatic contrast that enlivens the narrow panel, while the gentle inclination of both figures toward the centre links them to the sacred drama unfolding beside them.

In the quatrefoil above the arch, the Archangel Gabriel appears in half-length, dressed in a red mantle, raising his right hand in greeting. He is turned toward the right, toward the Virgin Annunciate in the corresponding quatrefoil of the right wing. The Annunciation, divided between the two wings, frames the whole triptych within the mystery of the Incarnation. When the triptych was open, the angel and the Virgin faced each other across the Crucifixion, so that the beginning of Christ's earthly life was set above its end.

The spandrels around the quatrefoil are painted dark and decorated with fine foliate patterns in gold. This decoration, which survives remarkably well, gives an idea of the original appearance of the framing elements of small portable altarpieces, which are often lost or heavily restored. The dark ground sets off the gold of the quatrefoil and the arch and creates a rich contrast with the luminous gold of the main field.

The style of the left wing is consistent with that of the central panel. The figures are slender and graceful, the modelling of the faces and draperies is soft and delicate, and the colours are clear and luminous. The treatment of the apostle's mantle, with its lining turned back to reveal a contrasting colour, and the careful rendering of the gold borders show Allegretto's attention to the decorative qualities of dress even in his early works. These qualities would become increasingly prominent in his later paintings.

The pairing of Saint Francis with an apostle probably reflects the particular devotion of the owner of the triptych, although the precise reasons for the choice of saints cannot be established without further evidence. Such small altarpieces were often commissioned for individuals, whose name saints or patron saints were included among the figures depicted. If the donor's identity could be discovered, the choice of saints might become more intelligible.

#### Right Wing: A Bearded Apostle (Saint Bartholomew?) and a Virgin Martyr

The right wing corresponds to the left in shape, size and structure. Beneath a pointed arch, against a gold ground, stand two saints, and above the arch, in a quatrefoil framed by dark spandrels with gold foliate ornament, appears the half-length figure of the Virgin Annunciate. The wing folded over the right half of the central panel, and when the triptych was open it completed the symmetrical frame around the Crucifixion. The measurements of the two wings, which differ by only a few millimetres, show the care with which the carpenter constructed the portable altarpiece.

On the left of the wing stands a bearded saint with dark hair and a dark complexion, dressed in a blue tunic and a white mantle patterned with gold. He holds a dark book in his left hand and, in his right, a small blade that appears to be a knife. The knife, the instrument of his martyrdom by flaying, would identify him as Saint Bartholomew the Apostle, whom Allegretto also represented in the Berlin diptych. Should the object prove to be a different attribute, the identification would need to be revised.

Beside him stands a young female saint with fair hair, crowned or bound with a diadem, wearing a gown of golden yellow and a mantle of green. She holds a palm, the emblem of martyrdom, and a small red cross, which she presses against her breast. The cross and palm identify her as a virgin martyr, but they do not permit a secure identification, since several martyrs were represented with a cross. Saint Margaret of Antioch, who overcame the dragon with the sign of the cross, is one possible identification, but others cannot be excluded.

The two saints are turned slightly toward the central panel, like those of the left wing, so that the four saints of the wings form a single company around the Crucifixion. The apostle's white mantle patterned with gold and the martyr's yellow gown create a luminous chromatic harmony that balances the darker tones of Saint Francis on the left wing. The pale ground on which the figures stand continues the ground plane of the left wing and links the wings to the rocky ground of the central panel.

In the quatrefoil above the arch, the Virgin Annunciate appears in half-length, dressed in a blue mantle, with her hands crossed on her breast in a gesture of humble acceptance. She is turned toward the left, toward the Archangel Gabriel in the corresponding quatrefoil of the left wing. The Annunciation divided between the two wings was a common feature of Trecento triptychs, and it gave the whole ensemble a theological structure that embraced the Incarnation and the Redemption.

The spandrels around the quatrefoil are decorated, like those of the left wing, with fine gold foliate patterns on a dark ground. The survival of this decoration, together with the original frame and hinges, makes the Bern triptych a document of great importance for the study of the construction and appearance of portable altarpieces of the fourteenth century. The wear visible on the lower parts of the wings, where the paint of the ground has been rubbed, bears witness to the repeated handling of the object over many years of devotional use.

The style of the right wing is consistent with that of the rest of the triptych. The figures are slender and elegant, their faces delicately modelled, and their draperies fall in soft, rhythmic folds. The gentle, contemplative expression of the female saint, in particular, recalls the tender faces of the Sienese painting that Allegretto had absorbed alongside the Florentine example of Daddi. The combination of these two traditions, evident throughout the triptych, defines the character of Allegretto's early work.

The Bern triptych as a whole represents a complete devotional programme in miniature. The Crucifixion at the centre, with the donor at the foot of the Cross, is framed by the blessing Christ above, by the Annunciation in the gables of the wings, and by four saints who form a heavenly company of intercessors. For its owner, the triptych offered a portable image of the whole history of salvation and of the communion of saints, to be opened in private prayer and closed for safekeeping or travel. It is one of the most complete and best-preserved testimonies to the type of devotional object that Allegretto helped to diffuse in the Marche.

### Polyptych with the Virgin and Child and Saints Mary Magdalene, James the Greater, Stephen and a Bishop Saint

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-06.jpg" alt="Gothic polyptych by Allegretto Nuzi with five half-length figures on gold ground under cusped arches, in a gilded frame with pinnacles and twisted columns: in the centre the Virgin in a dark mantle and red gown tenderly holding the Christ Child, who holds a goldfinch; on the left Saint Mary Magdalene in red with long hair and an ointment jar, and Saint James with a book and a pilgrim's staff; on the right the young deacon Saint Stephen in a patterned gold dalmatic with palm and red book, and a bearded bishop saint with mitre and crozier, blessing; the names of the figures are inscribed on the base">
  <figcaption>Virgin and Child, with Saints Mary Magdalene, James the Greater, Stephen, and a Bishop Saint, c. 1346, tempera, silver and tooled gold on panel; central panel 72.1 × 41.9 cm, side panels 60 × 29.8 cm each, Philadelphia Museum of Art, John G. Johnson Collection, 1917, inv. Cat. 5.</figcaption>
</figure>

#### Central Panel: The Virgin and Child

The Philadelphia polyptych, dated by the museum to about 1346, the year in which Allegretto is documented in Florence as a member of the Compagnia di San Luca, belongs to the earliest phase of his known activity. It follows a Florentine type that Bernardo Daddi had made popular: a central panel with the Virgin and Child flanked by four saints, all shown in half-length and set beneath cusped arches against a gold ground. The half-length format brings the figures close to the viewer and creates an intimate, direct relationship between the sacred figures and the faithful. In the central panel, taller and broader than the lateral ones, the Virgin holds the Child in a tender embrace, and her figure establishes the devotional focus of the whole altarpiece.

The Virgin is shown from the waist up, wrapped in a mantle of deep blue, now darkened almost to black, which covers her head and falls over her shoulders. Beneath it she wears a vermilion gown and a white veil that frames her face. She inclines her head toward the Child and presses her cheek against his, while her eyes gaze downward with an expression of gentle melancholy. Her face, with its long, straight nose, narrow eyes and small mouth, is softly modelled in the Florentine manner, and its expression of quiet sorrow suggests her foreknowledge of her Son's Passion.

The Child is seated on his Mother's left arm, turned toward her with his cheek pressed to hers. His body is partly bare, wrapped at the waist and legs in a transparent pink cloth, and around his neck hangs a small horn of red coral. Coral amulets of this shape were commonly worn by children in fourteenth-century Italy as protection against the evil eye, and Berenson, cataloguing the polyptych in 1913, already recognised the pendant as this charm. In religious painting, however, coral also carried a further meaning: its red colour evoked the blood of Christ, so that the amulet that protected an ordinary child here alludes to the sacrifice awaiting the Son of God. The Child reaches up with one hand toward the Virgin's neck, in a gesture of affection, while the Virgin supports him with her hands. The motif of the Mother and Child cheek to cheek, derived from the Byzantine type of the Virgin of Tenderness (Glykophilousa), had been adopted by Tuscan painters, and Allegretto renders it here with a naturalness and warmth that reflect his Sienese as well as his Florentine sources.

Near the Child's hand, against the red of the Virgin's gown, is a small bird, a goldfinch, which he holds. The goldfinch, whose red markings were associated with the blood of Christ, was a common symbol of the Passion in Trecento painting, and its presence here introduces a note of foreboding into the tender scene. The Child who plays with the bird is the same who will shed his blood on the Cross. This juxtaposition of intimacy and sacrifice, of the Mother's affection and her foreknowledge of the Passion, is central to the devotional meaning of the image.

The gold ground behind the figures is framed by a cusped arch, and the haloes of the Virgin and Child are finely tooled with punched ornament. The cusped arch of the central panel is carved in relief and gilded, and the gable above it is painted blue and decorated with gold foliate patterns, with a carved trefoil roundel at its centre. These elements, together with the elaborate pinnacles and crockets of the frame, give the polyptych the appearance of a miniature Gothic building. How much of the present frame is original, however, is a question that the museum's technical documentation would need to answer.

On the base beneath the central panel runs the inscription

> AVE MARIA GRATIA PLENA,

"Hail Mary, full of grace," the words of the Archangel Gabriel at the Annunciation, which formed the opening of the *Ave Maria*. The inscription invites the viewer to greet the Virgin with the angel's words, turning the image into a focus of prayer. The Marian greeting on the central panel is echoed by the names of the saints inscribed on the bases of the lateral panels, so that the whole polyptych is articulated by a sequence of inscriptions that identify its figures and direct the viewer's devotion.

The style of the central panel reflects Allegretto's close study of Bernardo Daddi. The soft modelling of the faces, the graceful inclination of the Virgin's head, the tenderness of the embrace and the rich but restrained colour are all characteristic of Daddi's devotional panels of the 1330s and 1340s. The Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani notes that in 1346 Allegretto came into contact with Daddi, and it describes the works of his early Daddesque period as graceful and aligned with the Sienese-Florentine compromise established by Daddi. The Philadelphia Virgin is one of the clearest expressions of this phase.

The central panel thus establishes the tone of the whole polyptych: intimate, tender and devotional, with a sense of quiet sorrow that anticipates the Passion. The half-length format, the cheek-to-cheek embrace, the goldfinch and the Marian inscription together create an image of great emotional immediacy. Painted at the beginning of Allegretto's documented career, the panel shows that the young painter from Fabriano had fully mastered the language of Florentine devotional painting and was able to use it with sensitivity and conviction.

#### Outer Left Panel: Saint Mary Magdalene

The outer left panel presents Saint Mary Magdalene in half-length, beneath a cusped arch, against a gold ground. The base bears the inscription S·M·MAGDALENA, "Saint Mary Magdalene." According to the Gospels, Mary Magdalene was the woman from whom Christ cast out seven devils, who stood at the foot of the Cross and who was the first witness of the Resurrection. Medieval tradition identified her with the sinful woman who anointed Christ's feet and with Mary of Bethany, and she became the model of the repentant sinner and of contemplative love.

The saint is shown frontally, with long, loose, golden hair falling over her shoulders and down to her waist. Her face is pale and oval, with a straight nose and a small mouth, and her large eyes gaze directly at the viewer with an intense, fixed expression. The directness of her gaze, unusual among the figures of the polyptych, establishes a personal relationship with the viewer and invites reflection on her example of penitence and devotion.

The Magdalene is dressed entirely in red: a gown of vermilion and a mantle of the same colour, edged with gold, which falls over her shoulders and arms. Red was her traditional colour, symbolising both the ardour of her love for Christ and, in some interpretations, her former life of sin. The broad expanses of red, modelled with restraint, give the figure a strong, unified presence that balances the dark mantle of the Virgin in the central panel.

In her hands the saint holds her characteristic attribute, the jar of ointment with which she anointed Christ's feet and with which she went to the tomb on Easter morning to anoint his body. The jar, white and cylindrical, is decorated with gold ornament and closed with a lid, and she holds it delicately in both hands before her breast. Its precious appearance recalls the costly spikenard of the Gospel account and alludes to the value of her devotion.

The loose hair, falling freely over her shoulders, is another traditional attribute of the Magdalene. It recalls the episode in which she dried Christ's feet with her hair, and it distinguishes her from the veiled matrons and crowned virgin martyrs of medieval iconography. Allegretto renders the hair with fine, flowing strokes of gold and light brown that create a rhythmic pattern framing the figure.

The panel is framed by twisted colonnettes and surmounted by a gable painted blue with gold foliate ornament, a carved trefoil roundel and crockets. The cusped arch that frames the saint's head is carved and gilded. These elements are repeated on each of the lateral panels, creating a unified and rhythmic structure across the polyptych.

The placement of the Magdalene at the outer left, balanced by the bishop saint at the outer right, reflects the hierarchical arrangement of the polyptych. She is paired with Saint James on the left, forming with him a group of figures closely associated with the life and Passion of Christ. Her presence in the polyptych may reflect the particular devotion of the patron or the dedication of the church or chapel for which the altarpiece was made.

Stylistically, the figure of the Magdalene is consistent with the Daddesque character of the central panel. The soft modelling of the face, the graceful fall of the drapery and the restrained use of colour are all characteristic of Allegretto's early manner. At the same time, the fixed, frontal gaze and the somewhat hieratic pose suggest a certain severity that distinguishes the figure from the gentler saints of Daddi's workshop and may reflect Allegretto's own temperament.

#### Inner Left Panel: Saint James the Greater

The inner left panel presents Saint James the Greater, the apostle and brother of Saint John the Evangelist. The base bears the inscription S·IACOBUS, "Saint James." According to tradition, James preached the Gospel in Spain and was martyred in Jerusalem under Herod Agrippa, and his body was later translated to Santiago de Compostela, which became one of the greatest pilgrimage destinations of the Middle Ages.

The apostle is shown in half-length, turned in three-quarter view toward the central panel, so that his gaze is directed toward the Virgin and Child. He has long, wavy brown hair and a full beard, and his face is grave and dignified, with a long nose and deep-set eyes. The expression of calm concentration suits the apostle's role as a witness to Christ and a preacher of the Gospel.

James wears a tunic of green and a mantle of rose-pink, lined with a lighter colour and edged with gold, which falls over his left shoulder and arm. The combination of green and pink, one of the favourite colour harmonies of Florentine painting in the circle of Daddi, gives the figure a luminous and elegant appearance. The folds of the mantle are modelled with soft gradations of tone that suggest the volume of the body beneath.

In his right hand the apostle holds a large book bound in black with gold ornament, the attribute of the apostles and of the Gospel they preached. In his left hand he holds a pilgrim's staff, to which is attached a scallop shell, the badge of the pilgrims of Santiago de Compostela. The shell and the staff identify him unmistakably as Saint James the Greater, patron of pilgrims.

The pilgrim's attributes had a particular resonance in the fourteenth century, when the pilgrimage to Compostela attracted large numbers of the faithful from all over Europe. The inclusion of James in the polyptych may reflect the devotion of a patron who had made the pilgrimage or who belonged to a confraternity dedicated to the saint. In the absence of documentary evidence, however, the reasons for his inclusion cannot be determined.

The saint's orientation toward the central panel links him to the Virgin and Child and creates a sense of movement toward the centre of the polyptych. His figure, turned inward, balances that of Saint Stephen on the opposite side, who also turns toward the centre. This symmetrical arrangement of the inner saints, facing the Virgin, frames the central image and directs the viewer's attention toward it.

Stylistically, the figure of James is one of the most characteristically Daddesque in the polyptych. The graceful proportions, the soft modelling and the harmonious colour recall the apostles in Daddi's polyptychs of the 1340s, and the figure demonstrates how closely Allegretto had studied the Florentine master's work. The fine punched decoration of the halo and the gold border of the mantle add a touch of refinement.

The panel of Saint James thus combines iconographic precision, stylistic elegance and devotional directness. It presents the apostle as a dignified witness to Christ, turned in contemplation toward the Virgin and Child, and it offers the viewer a model of faith and pilgrimage. Together with the Magdalene, he forms a harmonious group on the left side of the polyptych.

#### Inner Right Panel: Saint Stephen

The inner right panel presents Saint Stephen, the first martyr of the Church, whose stoning is recounted in the Acts of the Apostles. The base bears the inscription S·STEPHANUS, "Saint Stephen." Stephen was one of the seven deacons appointed by the apostles to serve the needs of the early Christian community in Jerusalem, and his death, witnessed by Saul, the future Saint Paul, made him the protomartyr, the model of all Christian martyrs.

The saint is shown in half-length, turned in three-quarter view toward the central panel, with his head inclined and his gaze directed downward. He is represented as a beardless young man with the tonsure, and on his head rest small stones, the instruments of his martyrdom. The motif of the stones placed on the head was a common iconographic convention, allowing the painter to identify the saint while preserving the serenity of his image.

Stephen wears the dalmatic, the liturgical vestment of the deacon, which is the most richly decorated garment in the polyptych. It is of golden yellow, adorned with broad bands of geometric ornament in which lozenges and squares alternate in red, black and gold, and it is completed by a collar of red. The museum's technical description records the use of silver in the polyptych, and the dark areas in the patterns of the dalmatic may be silver leaf that has oxidised over time. The rich decoration of the vestment reflects Allegretto's love of ornament, already evident in this early work.

In his right hand the saint holds a palm, the emblem of martyrdom, and in his left a large book bound in red, which alludes to his role as a deacon, charged with the reading of the Gospel in the liturgy. The palm and the book together define Stephen as both martyr and minister of the Word. The red of the book echoes the red of his collar and of the Virgin's gown, creating a chromatic link between the panels.

The saint's youthful face, with its soft modelling, delicate features and gentle expression, conveys the serenity of the martyr who, according to the Acts, saw the heavens open and the Son of Man standing at the right hand of God at the moment of his death. The inclination of his head toward the central panel links him to the Virgin and Child and balances the figure of Saint James on the opposite side.

The rich geometric patterns of the dalmatic contrast with the simpler draperies of the other saints and create a focus of decorative interest on the right side of the polyptych. This contrast between plain and ornate surfaces, between modelled drapery and flat pattern, would become one of the defining features of Allegretto's mature style. In the Philadelphia polyptych it is still subordinated to the overall harmony of the composition.

The inclusion of Saint Stephen alongside Saint James may reflect the patron's devotion or the dedication of the altar for which the polyptych was made. Stephen was widely venerated throughout Italy, and many churches and altars were dedicated to him. His presence in the polyptych, as the first martyr, complements that of James, the first apostle to be martyred, and gives the altarpiece a coherent programme of witness and martyrdom.

The panel of Saint Stephen is one of the most refined in the polyptych. The delicate modelling of the face, the rich decoration of the dalmatic and the harmonious colour combine to create an image of youthful holiness and liturgical splendour. It shows that Allegretto, even at the beginning of his career, was capable of combining Florentine grace with a decorative sensibility that would become increasingly prominent in his later work.

#### Outer Right Panel: A Bishop Saint

The outer right panel presents a bishop saint in half-length, beneath a cusped arch, against a gold ground. The base bears the inscription S·NICHOLAUS, "Saint Nicholas," and the identification is at least a century old: Berenson, in his 1913 catalogue of the Johnson Collection, already described the figure as Saint Nicholas, wearing a white mitre and a bluish-green mantle trimmed with ochre. The museum today, however, catalogues him simply as "a Bishop Saint." This caution is justified. The inscribed bases may be part of a later frame, and the figure lacks the three golden balls or purses that were Nicholas's usual attribute in Italian painting. The identification therefore remains possible but unproven.

The saint is represented as an elderly man with a short grey beard and a lined, grave face, looking out toward the viewer. He wears a tall mitre richly decorated with geometric patterns in red, gold and black, and he is dressed in a green chasuble adorned with a broad orphrey of gold with a cross-shaped pattern. The rich episcopal vestments, rendered with great attention to detail, contrast with the simpler garments of the other saints.

With his right hand, covered by a white glove, the bishop raises two fingers in blessing, and in his left, also gloved, he holds a crozier, the pastoral staff of the bishop, whose curved head is visible at the upper right of the panel. On the finger of his left hand he wears an episcopal ring with a red stone. The gloves, the ring and the crozier are all insignia of episcopal dignity and emphasise the saint's authority as a shepherd of the Church.

The geometric patterns of the mitre and the orphrey, like those of Saint Stephen's dalmatic, may originally have included silver, which has darkened over time. The rich decoration of the vestments balances that of Stephen's dalmatic on the inner panel, so that the two saints on the right side of the polyptych form a pair distinguished by their liturgical splendour, in contrast to the simpler dress of the Magdalene and James on the left.

The bishop's frontal pose and direct gaze give him an air of authority and dignity. Unlike Saint Stephen, who turns toward the centre, the bishop faces the viewer, raising his hand in blessing as if addressing the faithful before the altar. This frontal orientation balances that of the Magdalene on the outer left, who also gazes directly at the viewer, so that the two outer figures frame the polyptych with their direct address.

If the bishop is indeed Saint Nicholas, his inclusion in the polyptych would reflect the immense popularity of the saint in fourteenth-century Italy. Nicholas, bishop of Myra, whose relics had been translated to Bari in 1087, was venerated as a protector of children, sailors and the poor, and his cult was widespread in the Marche as well as in Tuscany. The absence of his usual attribute, however, makes it prudent to leave the identification open.

Stylistically, the figure of the bishop shows a slightly different character from the other saints. The lined face, the grey beard and the grave expression convey a sense of age and authority, and the modelling is more emphatic than in the youthful figures of Stephen and the Magdalene. The rich decoration of the vestments, however, is consistent with the rest of the polyptych, and the figure is fully integrated into its overall harmony.

The panel of the bishop saint completes the programme of the Philadelphia polyptych. The four saints, two on each side, form a heavenly court around the Virgin and Child. The Magdalene and James on the left, associated with the life of Christ, and Stephen and the bishop on the right, associated with the ministry of the Church, together represent the various forms of holiness: penitence, apostolic witness, martyrdom and pastoral care. Painted at the beginning of Allegretto's career, the polyptych is one of the most complete and harmonious works of his early Daddesque phase.

The attribution of the polyptych to Allegretto is a relatively recent achievement.
