When Berenson catalogued it in 1913, he gave it to "Giottino," the Florentine artistic personality to whom he assigned the frescoes of the Strozzi Chapel in Santa Maria Novella and of the chapter house of Santo Spirito in Florence, praising it as the work of a noble artist deeply influenced by the Lorenzetti. Berenson's error is instructive. The polyptych is so thoroughly Florentine in its forms, and so close to the Sienese-inflected painting of Florence in the 1340s, that it could be mistaken for the work of a native Florentine master. Its later recognition as a work by Allegretto has become one of the clearest demonstrations of how deeply the young painter from Fabriano had absorbed the art of Florence during his years there.

### Virgin and Child Enthroned with Saints Lucy, John the Baptist, Anthony Abbot and a Female Martyr, with the Angel of the Annunciation

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-07.jpg" alt="Small gabled panel by Allegretto Nuzi on gold ground: the Virgin in a dark mantle with gold ornament and a red gown, enthroned frontally on a tall red throne with gilded pinnacles, holding the standing Christ Child in a yellow tunic; behind the throne Saint Lucy holding a burning lamp and a female saint with a palm; in front Saint John the Baptist in a pink mantle with his hand on his breast and Saint Anthony Abbot in a black habit with staff and red book; the Archangel Gabriel in half-length in the gable">
  <figcaption>Virgin and Child Enthroned with Saints Lucy, John the Baptist and Anthony Abbot, a Female Martyr, and the Annunciate Angel, c. 1350, tempera and tooled gold on panel, 43.5 × 23.5 cm, Philadelphia Museum of Art, John G. Johnson Collection, 1917, inv. Cat. 2.</figcaption>
</figure>

This small panel, less than half a metre high and still in its original engaged frame, belongs to the type of portable devotional image that Allegretto produced throughout his career. Several features suggest that it was the left wing of a diptych. The half-length Archangel Gabriel in the gable turns to the right, toward a figure no longer present, and he would have been answered by the Annunciate Virgin in the gable of a companion panel, most probably a Crucifixion, as in the Berlin diptych. A small metal fitting on the right edge of the frame may be a remnant of the hinge that joined the two wings. The museum's dating to about 1350 places the panel in Allegretto's early phase, shortly after his Florentine sojourn, when the influence of Bernardo Daddi was still dominant in his work.

The composition is dominated by the Virgin, enthroned frontally at the centre on a tall, narrow throne whose back is covered by a cloth of red and orange. The throne rises into a pointed gable crowned by a gilded finial in the form of a flaming vase, and its uprights are topped by similar gilded ornaments shaped like flames or foliage. This vertical architecture, rising into the gable of the panel, gives the Virgin an imposing presence despite the small scale of the image. The throne also serves to separate the Virgin from the saints on either side, affirming her status as Queen of Heaven while situating her in the midst of her heavenly court.

The Virgin wears a dark mantle, strewn with gold scrolling ornament and fleurons, over a gown of red sprinkled with small gold motifs, and her head is covered by the mantle, beneath which a white veil frames her face. She looks directly at the viewer with a grave expression, her long face and narrow eyes recalling the Madonnas of Daddi's workshop. Across her lap the pale lining of the mantle sweeps in a broad curve, a motif Allegretto would repeat in larger works such as the Apiro polyptych. The Child stands on his Mother's lap, dressed in a yellow tunic tied at the waist with a blue sash. He reaches toward her breast and looks down, while the Virgin supports him with her hands. The standing Child, active and attentive, gives the solemn frontal group a note of animation.

Behind the throne, on either side, appear two young female saints, shown half-hidden by the throne and by the saints in front of them. On the left is Saint Lucy, identified by the burning lamp she holds, an attribute that alludes to her name, derived from the Latin *lux*, light. On the right is a young female martyr holding a palm, whose identity cannot be determined from this attribute alone, since the palm was common to all martyrs. Both wear their hair bound with a dark band and are dressed in pale garments that harmonise with the gold ground. Their placement in the second plane, behind the throne, suggests a heavenly court that extends beyond the visible figures, a device that Allegretto adapted from the larger Maestà compositions of Florentine and Sienese painting.

In the foreground stand two male saints. On the left, Saint John the Baptist, bearded and with long, dishevelled hair, wears a rose-pink mantle over his camel-hair garment, and his bare legs and feet recall his life in the wilderness. He places his right hand on his breast in a gesture of devotion, turning toward the Virgin. On the right, Saint Anthony Abbot, with a long grey beard, wears the black habit of the hermits and holds a staff and a red book. His dark figure, standing firmly beside the throne, balances the rose mantle of the Baptist, and the two saints frame the throne with their contrasting colours and characters: the prophet of the desert and the father of monasticism.

The choice of saints is revealing. Saint Anthony Abbot appears in several of Allegretto's works associated with Antonine patronage, including the Washington triptych of 1354 and the Macerata triptych of 1369, and his presence here may reflect the particular devotion of the owner. John the Baptist, as the last of the prophets and the forerunner of Christ, was often paired with the Virgin as an intercessor for humanity. Saint Lucy, whose lamp symbolised the light of faith, was invoked against diseases of the eyes and enjoyed a widespread cult in the Marche, where Allegretto himself represented her in the Apiro polyptych of 1366. The combination of these saints suggests a personal selection by the patron, whose name saints or protectors they may have been.

The Archangel in the gable and the fitting on the right edge of the frame both point to a missing second wing. Berenson had already reached this conclusion in 1913: he judged that the little pointed panel must have formed part of a diptych, on the top of whose other panel the Virgin Annunciate answered the Gabriel represented here. At that time he attributed the panel to Bernardo Daddi, an attribution that itself testifies to how closely Allegretto's early work followed the Florentine master. Whether the companion wing survives remains to be established; the museum's catalogue by [Strehlke](/scholars.html#strehlke-carl-brandon) (2004) may identify it.

The panel has suffered from the passage of time. A long vertical crack runs through the Virgin's figure and the throne, and there are losses of paint and wear to the gold, particularly in the lower part of the panel and on the frame. Despite this damage, the qualities of Allegretto's early style remain clearly legible: the graceful, softly modelled faces, the harmonious colour, the refined decoration of the Virgin's mantle, and the careful tooling of the haloes and borders. The panel shows how the young painter, fresh from his contact with Daddi's workshop, could condense the grandeur of a Florentine altarpiece into the intimate format of a private devotional image, creating a small but complete vision of the Virgin enthroned among her saints.

### Virgin and Child, c. 1360

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-08.jpg" alt="Small rectangular panel by Allegretto Nuzi on gold ground: the Virgin in half-length, her head inclined, wearing a dark mantle with fine gold lines and an embroidered border over a gold gown patterned with dark flowers, holds the Christ Child on her left arm; the Child, wrapped in a red and gold cloth, looks out at the viewer; both have finely punched haloes, and painted ornaments decorate the corners of the gold ground">
  <figcaption>Virgin and Child, c. 1360, tempera, silver and tooled gold on panel with horizontal grain, 43.5 × 34.9 × 3.8 cm (framed), Philadelphia Museum of Art, John G. Johnson Collection, 1917, inv. Cat. 119.</figcaption>
</figure>

This small panel presents the Virgin and Child in half-length, close to the viewer, in the intimate format of the private devotional image. The Virgin fills almost the entire height of the gold ground, her figure cut at the level of the hips by the lower edge of the panel, and the Child is held high on her left arm so that his head reaches the level of her shoulder. There are no saints, angels or architectural elements: the image is reduced to the essential relationship between Mother and Son, set against a luminous field of gold. The museum's dating to about 1360 places the panel in Allegretto's mature phase, between the Washington triptych of 1354 and the Vatican triptych of 1365, when his art was moving from the Daddesque grace of his early works toward the more decorative manner of his later years.

The Virgin inclines her head gently toward the Child, while her eyes, turned sideways, seem to look past him toward the viewer. Her face is long and oval, with a straight nose, narrow, almond-shaped eyes and a small, closed mouth, and its expression is one of quiet melancholy. The flesh tones have darkened considerably over time, and the greenish underlayer of the tempera now shows through, giving the faces a sombre cast that was not intended by the painter. Even so, the delicacy of the modelling and the gentle tilt of the head convey the tenderness and the foreboding that characterise Allegretto's Madonnas, in which maternal affection is always tinged with awareness of the Passion to come.

The Virgin's mantle, dark blue or black, covers her head and falls over her shoulders to the lower edge of the panel. Its surface is traversed by fine gold lines that follow the folds of the fabric, in a technique that recalls the chrysography of Byzantine icons, in which gold striations were used to model drapery and to suggest divine light. The mantle is edged with an embroidered border decorated with a pattern of crosses and lozenges. Beneath it, the Virgin wears a gown of gold brocade densely patterned with small dark flowers and stars, whose surface shimmers against the gold ground. The museum's technical description records the use of silver, which may have been employed in the decoration of the garments, although its exact location should be confirmed in the technical literature.

The Christ Child is seated on his Mother's left arm, wrapped in a cloth of red and gold brocade patterned with dark ornament, which leaves his chest and arms bare. He turns his face toward the viewer with a grave, almost adult expression, and raises one hand to his breast, where a small red pendant, perhaps a piece of coral, hangs from a cord around his neck. Coral, worn as an amulet by children, was also associated with the blood of Christ, so that the small ornament may carry an allusion to the Passion. The Virgin supports the Child with her hands, her long, slender fingers resting on his body in a gesture of protection and presentation.

The haloes of the Virgin and Child are among the most elaborately tooled elements of the panel. Each is composed of concentric bands of punched ornament, with rings of small circles and rosettes and incised lines radiating from the head. The gold ground around the figures, now much worn, reveals in many places the red bole beneath. In the upper corners and at the lower left, painted ornaments of dark foliate motifs decorate the gold ground, softening the rectangular format of the panel and suggesting a framing arch. These corner decorations, together with the rectangular format and the horizontal grain of the wood, raise the question of whether the panel preserves its original shape or was adapted at some point in its history.

The richness of the textiles, the gold striations of the mantle and the dense patterns of the gown and of the Child's cloth show Allegretto's growing interest in the decorative possibilities of gold and colour. At the same time, the figures retain the soft modelling and the gentle proportions of his Florentine training. The panel thus stands at a point of transition in the painter's career, between the Daddesque grace of the 1340s and 1350s and the flatter, more ornamental manner of his last decade. The icon-like character of the image, with its frontal presentation, its gold ground and its chrysographic mantle, also reflects the persistence of Byzantine devotional models in fourteenth-century Italian painting, particularly in images intended for private prayer.

The inscription on the reverse of the panel reveals an important chapter in the later history of the image. It records that anyone who came devoutly to see this sacred image of the Most Holy Name of Mary and recited any prayer before it would gain an indulgence of one hundred days, and that reciting the litanies would gain a further fifty days, under a concession of Pope [Pius VI](/endnotes.html#fn-pope-pius-vi). Since Pius VI reigned from 1775 to 1799, the inscription shows that more than four centuries after it was painted, the panel was still the object of public veneration, probably in a church or chapel, and that its cult was sanctioned by the papacy. The dedication to the Most Holy Name of Mary, a feast extended to the universal Church in the late seventeenth century, suggests that the image may have been associated with a confraternity or altar of that title.

The Philadelphia *Virgin and Child* is thus significant not only as a work of Allegretto's maturity but also as a document of the long devotional life of medieval images. Painted around 1360 for private or public devotion, it continued to attract prayer and veneration in the late eighteenth century, when its antiquity and its icon-like appearance may have enhanced its sacred aura. Its worn surface, darkened flesh tones and damaged gold bear witness to this long history of use. Despite its condition, the panel conveys the qualities that made Allegretto's devotional images so widely esteemed: the tenderness of the relationship between Mother and Child, the refinement of the decoration, and the luminous splendour of gold.

### Diptych with the Virgin and Child and the Man of Sorrows, c. 1366

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-09.jpg" alt="Small diptych by Allegretto Nuzi in gilded frames: on the left the Virgin in half-length, in a dark mantle with a gold border and a red gown, holding the Christ Child in a pink tunic who holds a goldfinch and turns toward her; on the right the dead Christ in half-length rising from a red tomb, with head bowed, eyes lowered and arms crossed over his breast, his hands and side bleeding and his body marked by the scourging; both on gold grounds with punched decorative borders">
  <figcaption>Virgin and Child; Man of Sorrows, c. 1366, tempera and tooled gold on panel, 39 × 50.8 cm (overall), Philadelphia Museum of Art, John G. Johnson Collection, 1917, inv. Cat. 118.</figcaption>
</figure>

#### Left Wing: The Virgin and Child

The Philadelphia diptych is one of the most moving examples of the small devotional images through which Allegretto reached the private piety of his patrons. Its two wings, still joined in their original engaged frames, confront the viewer with the two poles of the Christian story: on the left, the Mother holding her infant Son, and on the right, the same Son dead and risen from his tomb. The museum's dating to about 1366 places the diptych in the same years as the Apiro polyptych and the San Severino Madonna of Humility, at the height of Allegretto's maturity. The 2021 Fabriano exhibition included another diptych by Allegretto pairing the Madonna and Child with the *Imago Pietatis*, now divided between a private collection and the Musei Civici of Pesaro, which shows that the painter returned to this combination more than once.

The Virgin is shown in half-length, turned in three-quarter view toward the Child, who is held on her left arm. She wears a white veil that frames her face and falls over her shoulders, and above it a mantle of deep blue, now darkened almost to black, edged with a broad gold border embroidered with a lattice of crosses. On her right shoulder shines a large gold star with radiating rays, the emblem of Mary as *Stella Maris*. Beneath the mantle she wears a gown of brilliant vermilion, whose colour stands out with great force between the dark folds of the mantle. The restrained palette of dark blue, red and white, set against the gold, gives the image an intensity and clarity characteristic of Allegretto's mature devotional panels.

The Virgin's face is long and oval, with a straight, narrow nose, almond-shaped eyes and a small, closed mouth. She does not look at the Child but gazes out beyond the viewer with a grave, melancholy expression, as if absorbed in thoughts of the future. This withdrawn gaze, a constant of Allegretto's Madonnas, acquires particular significance in a diptych whose second wing shows the dead Christ. The Mother who holds her infant Son appears already to foresee the Passion displayed on the opposite panel, so that her sorrow becomes the link between the two images.

The Christ Child, dressed in a pink tunic girded with a blue sash, sits on his Mother's arm and turns toward her, raising his face to hers. With both hands he holds a small bird with a red face and black and yellow wings, a goldfinch, which he presses against the Virgin's breast. The museum's own description explains the symbolism: the bird, fond of eating thistles and thorns, was often shown as a reminder of Christ's future Passion and the crown of thorns. Like the Virgin's gaze, the goldfinch links the tender scene of the left wing to the image of suffering on the right, making the whole diptych a meditation on the unity of Incarnation and Redemption.

The Virgin raises her right hand before her breast, touching the Child's hands and the bird, while her left hand supports him from below. On the ring finger of her right hand she wears a small gold ring. The same detail appears in the San Severino Madonna of Humility of 1366, and it may be read as a sign of Mary's role as the bride of Christ, the *Sponsa* of the Song of Songs, in whom the Church was personified. The recurrence of the ring in two works of the same years suggests a deliberate iconographic choice on Allegretto's part, perhaps reflecting the devotional culture of the mendicant orders for which he frequently worked.

The Virgin's hands are painted with a delicacy and elegance that reveal the refinement of Allegretto's mature style. Her long, slender fingers are arranged in graceful gestures, one raised before her breast, the other supporting the Child, and their pale flesh stands out against the vermilion of the gown. The gesture of the raised hand, which both touches and presents the Child and the bird, draws the viewer's attention to the symbolic centre of the image, where the Mother's hand, the Son's hands and the bird of the Passion meet.

The gold ground behind the figures is framed by broad punched borders decorated with rows of small rosettes, ogival motifs and scalloped bands, and the haloes of the Virgin and Child are richly tooled with floral ornament. The density of the punchwork, which covers the whole border of the panel, testifies to Allegretto's extraordinary mastery of gold tooling, singled out by modern scholarship as one of the hallmarks of his art. In the small format of the diptych, the richly worked gold creates an effect of preciousness appropriate to an object intended for intimate, prolonged contemplation.

The left wing of the Philadelphia diptych thus presents the Virgin and Child as an image of tenderness shadowed by sorrow. The affectionate gesture of the Child turning to his Mother, the grave expression of the Virgin, the goldfinch and the ring together compose a dense web of meanings that the devout owner would have unfolded in prayer. Opened on a table or a small altar, the diptych offered its owner the image of the Mother presenting her Son, and at the same time invited him to turn toward the opposite wing, where the Son appears as the Man of Sorrows.

#### Right Wing: The Man of Sorrows

The right wing presents the Man of Sorrows, or *Imago Pietatis*, the image of the dead Christ rising in half-length from his tomb. The type, which originated in Byzantine art and spread through Italy from the late thirteenth century, did not illustrate a moment of the Gospel narrative. It condensed the Passion, death and Resurrection into a single timeless image, designed to arouse compassion and devotion. It became one of the most widespread subjects of private devotion in the fourteenth century, particularly in small diptychs such as this, where it was paired with the Virgin and Child.

Christ is shown frontally, rising from a sarcophagus whose red and pink front runs across the lower part of the panel. His head is bowed onto his right shoulder, his eyes are almost closed, and his long brown hair falls over his shoulders. His face, with its lowered lids, parted lips and short beard, expresses the stillness of death and at the same time a mysterious life, for the dead Christ stands upright in his tomb. This ambiguity between death and life lies at the heart of the *Imago Pietatis*, which presents Christ as dead and yet present, suffering and yet victorious.

Christ's arms are crossed over his breast, with the hands laid on the opposite shoulders. This pose, derived from Byzantine models, was among the most common in Italian representations of the subject, and it emphasises both the stillness of death and the display of the wounds. The wounds in his hands are clearly visible, with blood flowing from them in thin streams down the forearms, and the wound in his side, partly concealed by the crossed arms, also bleeds. The display of the wounds, the signs of Christ's sacrifice, invited the viewer to meditate on the suffering of the Passion and on the love that it revealed.

The body of Christ is covered with small red marks, the traces of the scourging and of the crown of thorns. These wounds are scattered across the chest, the arms and the abdomen, and small drops of blood run from the brow. Allegretto renders them with restraint, as fine red strokes on the pale flesh, avoiding any excess of violence. The effect is one of quiet pathos rather than horror, in keeping with the contemplative character of an image for private devotion.

The modelling of the body is one of the finest passages in Allegretto's work. The torso is rendered with a careful attention to anatomy, with the ribs, the muscles of the chest and the abdomen indicated by subtle gradations of tone. The flesh has a greenish cast, which results in part from the green underpainting, or *verdaccio*, of Trecento tempera technique becoming more visible over time as the upper layers have thinned, but which also expresses the pallor of death. The lower edge of the body is veiled by a transparent loincloth, visible just above the edge of the tomb.

Behind Christ's head is a cruciform halo, richly tooled and set with punched square motifs that suggest jewels. The cross inscribed within the halo, reserved for Christ, identifies him as the Son of God. The gold ground around him is framed by the same broad punched borders as the left wing, so that the two panels, when opened side by side, form a unified and harmonious ensemble. The consistency of the decoration confirms that the two wings were conceived together as a single object.

The pairing of the Virgin and Child with the Man of Sorrows had a profound devotional meaning. It set side by side the Incarnation and the Passion, the infant Christ in his Mother's arms and the dead Christ in his tomb, inviting the viewer to meditate on the continuity between the two. The Virgin's sorrowful gaze and the goldfinch on the left wing anticipate the suffering shown on the right, while the Man of Sorrows recalls the human body that the Virgin had given to her Son. In the practice of affective devotion promoted by the mendicant orders, such diptychs served as supports for prayer, in which the faithful were encouraged to share the grief of the Mother and to contemplate the wounds of the Son.

The Philadelphia diptych is one of the most refined and intense examples of Allegretto's devotional painting. The two wings, united by their gold grounds, their punched borders and their original frames, compose a coherent and moving meditation on the mystery of salvation. The tenderness of the left wing and the silent suffering of the right are expressed with a delicacy and restraint that reveal the painter at the height of his maturity. Painted around 1366, in the same years as the Apiro polyptych and the San Severino Madonna, the diptych shows how Allegretto could combine monumental dignity and intimate emotion within the smallest of formats.

### The Crucifixion with the Virgin, Saint John the Evangelist, Mary Magdalene and Two Angels, 1345–50

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-23.jpg" alt="Gabled panel by Allegretto Nuzi on gold ground in a frame with crockets: Christ dead on the Cross between two small mourning angels; Mary Magdalene in red kneels and embraces the foot of the Cross on a rocky ground; the Virgin in a dark mantle over a red gown stands on the left with hands joined, and Saint John in a red mantle over a green tunic stands on the right with head bowed; in the gable a pelican feeds its young in a green nest above the red titulus">
  <figcaption>Allegretto Nuzi (attr.), The Crucifixion, c. 1345–50, tempera and gold on panel, 58.42 × 23.5 cm (unframed), 77.47 × 40.64 cm (framed), Richmond, Virginia Museum of Fine Arts, Arthur and Margaret Glasgow Fund, inv. 59.4.</figcaption>
</figure>

This small Crucifixion, dated by the museum to about 1345–50, is a work whose authorship has been debated for almost a century. It is presented here under the name of Allegretto Nuzi, an attribution first proposed by Bernard Berenson in 1936 and maintained by him in 1968. More recently, the National Gallery of Art's catalogue has included the Richmond Crucifixion among the works on which the initial phase of Allegretto's career can be reconstructed, works in which the Florentine stylistic component, essentially derived from Maso di Banco, is fused with elements revealing his familiarity with the art of the Lorenzetti. If this attribution is accepted, the panel belongs to the years immediately surrounding Allegretto's documented presence in Florence in 1346. Its modest dimensions and its composition suggest that it was intended for private devotion, perhaps as the wing of a diptych, although the present frame, with its crockets and finial, gives it the appearance of an independent gabled panel.

The composition is organised around the tall Cross, which rises from the rocky ground of Golgotha to the gable of the panel. Christ hangs dead upon it, his arms stretched along the crossbar and his body sagging under its own weight, his head bowed onto his right shoulder. The slender body, modelled in pale, greenish flesh tones, is girded by a transparent loincloth, and the legs are bent, with the feet crossed and nailed to the upright. Above his head is a red titulus, on which no inscription is now legible. The figure belongs to the Tuscan type of the *Christus patiens*, and its gentle modelling and elegant proportions reflect the example of Bernardo Daddi and of the Florentine painters Allegretto encountered in the 1340s.

On either side of the Cross, in the upper part of the panel, appear two small angels in half-length, emerging from clouds against the gold ground. Dressed in pale garments, with spread wings, they join their hands and bow their heads in grief. The mourning angels, a motif introduced into Crucifixion scenes by Giotto in the [Arena Chapel](/endnotes.html#fn-arena-chapel) and widely adopted by his followers, extend the lament for Christ's death from the earthly to the heavenly sphere. Their small scale and their position in the upper corners of the field balance the composition and frame the body of Christ, directing the viewer's attention toward it.

At the foot of the Cross, Mary Magdalene kneels on the rocks, her figure wrapped in a mantle of brilliant vermilion lined with a paler colour, and embraces the wood with both arms. Her face is pressed against the Cross, close to Christ's nailed feet, and her long hair falls over her shoulders. Her gesture of intense, bodily devotion, repeated in several of Allegretto's Crucifixions, including the Berlin diptych and the Bern triptych, offered the viewer a model of affective prayer. The Magdalene, the repentant sinner who had anointed Christ's feet, appears here as the embodiment of penitence and of love for the crucified Lord.

On the left stands the Virgin, wrapped in a dark mantle that covers her head and falls to the ground, over a red gown. She stands upright, her hands joined before her, and her face, turned toward the Cross, expresses silent, contained grief. On the right stands Saint John the Evangelist, in a red mantle over a green tunic, with his head bowed and his hands clasped before him in a gesture of sorrow. The two figures, placed on either side of the Cross in symmetrical correspondence, follow the traditional composition of the Crucifixion with the Mother and the beloved disciple. Their broad, simple silhouettes, modelled with solid volumes, reflect the monumental conception of the figure that Allegretto learned from Maso di Banco.

In the gable, above the titulus, a pelican in a nest wreathed with green foliage pierces her breast to feed her young with her blood. The image, derived from the early Christian bestiary known as the *Physiologus*, was one of the most widespread symbols of Christ, who gives his blood for the redemption of humankind and nourishes the faithful in the Eucharist. The same motif crowns the Crucifixion of the Berlin diptych, where the pelican nests in a spray of leaves sprouting from the top of the Cross. The recurrence of the pelican in these two works shows how Allegretto developed a consistent iconographic formula for his devotional Crucifixions, in which the historical scene below is completed by a symbolic image of the sacrifice above.

The gold ground of the panel is framed by a punched border of floral ornament, which follows the outline of the gable, and the haloes of the figures are finely tooled. The frame, with its crockets and pointed finial, is richly carved and gilded, although whether it is original or a later addition would need to be verified in the museum's documentation. Crocketed frames of this kind were common in Tuscan panels of the period, but many surviving examples on small devotional panels have been restored or replaced.

Few works in Allegretto's catalogue have had so varied a critical history. In 1932 Berenson listed the panel under Jacopo di Cione, and in 1933 Lionello Venturi published it as the work of a collaborator of Giotto. When it entered the Minneapolis Institute of Arts with the Van Derlip bequest in 1935, it was first catalogued as Florentine School and then as Maso di Banco. In 1936 Berenson reassigned it to Allegretto Nuzi, and the Virginia Museum of Fine Arts, which acquired it with the Arthur and Margaret Glasgow Fund, published it in 1966 as Maso di Banco.

In his 1969 dissertation on Maso, [David Wilkins](/scholars.html#wilkins-david-g) rejected that attribution, while recognising the panel as a high-quality work of the second or third quarter of the Trecento. Fredericksen and [Zeri](/scholars.html#zeri-federico), in their 1972 census of Italian paintings in North American collections, catalogued it as studio of Maso di Banco. Meanwhile [Carlo Volpe](/scholars.html#volpe-carlo) in 1971, [Miklós Boskovits](/scholars.html#boskovits-miklos) in 1975 and Everett Fahy in 2001–2002 assigned it to the anonymous Florentine painter known as the Master of San Lucchese. The oscillation between Maso di Banco, his workshop, the Cione, the Master of San Lucchese and Allegretto is itself revealing. It shows how closely the panel is bound to the Florentine painting of the 1340s, and how difficult it is to separate the young Allegretto's work from that of the Florentine masters and workshops whose idiom he absorbed. It is particularly noteworthy that Boskovits, who in 1975 gave the panel to the Master of San Lucchese, is the author of the National Gallery of Art's systematic catalogue, which now lists it among Allegretto's early works. The attribution to Allegretto should therefore be regarded as plausible, supported by authoritative opinion, but not beyond dispute.

In its style, the Richmond Crucifixion stands at the heart of the Florentine painting of the 1340s. The monumental figures of the Virgin and Saint John, with their simple silhouettes and solid volumes, reflect the example of Maso di Banco, and it is no accident that the panel was long attributed to Maso himself or to his workshop. The tender expressions and graceful gestures, on the other hand, recall the Sienese art of the Lorenzetti. The composition is clear and balanced, and the colours, the red of the Magdalene and John, the dark blue of the Virgin and the green of John's tunic, stand out with brilliant clarity against the gold. The iconographic affinities with Allegretto's Berlin Crucifixion, in particular the pelican in the gable and the kneeling Magdalene embracing the Cross, lend some support to the attribution to him. Whether by Allegretto or by a Florentine contemporary, the panel offers a precious testimony to the artistic milieu in which the young painter from Fabriano was formed.

### Portable Tabernacle with the Madonna and Child Enthroned with Saints, the Nativity, the Crucifixion and the Annunciation

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-24.jpg" alt="Small Gothic tabernacle by Allegretto Nuzi in an elaborate gilded frame with pinnacles and twisted columns: in the centre the Virgin enthroned under a canopy with a starry vault, holding the Christ Child, flanked by two angels, Saint John the Baptist and a female saint; on the left wing the Nativity with the ox and ass, Joseph and a choir of angels, and above an angel announcing to a shepherd; on the right wing the Crucifixion with the fainting Virgin, holy women, Saint John and Mary Magdalene in red at the foot of the Cross; the Annunciation divided between the gables of the wings; coats of arms on the base">
  <figcaption>Madonna and Child with Saints, Crucifixion and Nativity, c. 1350–70, tempera and gold on panel, 77.5 × 53.3 × 12.7 cm (overall), Detroit Institute of Arts, Gift of James E. Scripps, inv. 89.19.</figcaption>
</figure>

#### Central Panel: The Madonna and Child Enthroned with Saints

The Detroit tabernacle is among the most complete surviving examples of the portable devotional altarpieces produced in Allegretto's workshop. It consists of a central panel with a pointed arch and two hinged wings, which could be closed over the centre to protect it and opened to reveal the whole programme: the Virgin and Child enthroned, the Nativity, the Crucifixion and the Annunciation. The Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani lists the portable tabernacle in Detroit, with the Madonna enthroned, the Nativity, the Crucifixion and the Annunciation, among the works of Allegretto's first Daddesque period, which it describes as graceful works aligned with the Sienese-Florentine compromise established by Daddi. The museum's broader dating to 1350–70 reflects the uncertainty that still surrounds the chronology of Allegretto's small devotional works.

At the centre of the panel, the Virgin sits enthroned beneath an elaborate Gothic canopy. The throne rises behind her in the form of a miniature architectural structure, with pointed gables, pinnacles and tracery rendered in delicate gold, and its vault, visible above her head, is painted deep blue and sprinkled with golden stars, an image of the heavenly firmament. This architectural throne, derived from the tabernacles and baldachins of Gothic church furnishings, transforms the Virgin's seat into a sacred shrine and affirms her status as Queen of Heaven. Its fine gold ornament, punched and incised into the gilded surface, demonstrates Allegretto's mastery of the goldsmith's techniques applied to panel painting.

The Virgin wears a gown of red, its folds highlighted with fine gold lines, and a mantle whose lower part, spread across her knees, is dark and densely patterned with gold ornament, lined with white. The rendering of this precious fabric, in which the pattern imitates the luxury silks imported and woven in fourteenth-century Italy, is sufficiently accomplished that the tabernacle was included in Brigitte Klesse's study of silk textiles in Italian painting of the Trecento (1967).

The Virgin's face, framed by a white veil and a finely tooled halo, is long and oval, with narrow eyes and a small mouth, and she looks out toward the viewer with a gentle, grave expression. The Child sits on her left arm, dressed in a blue tunic, and clings to her with one hand while turning toward the viewer. The motif attracted the attention of Dorothy Shorr, who reproduced a detail of the Child in her study of the Christ Child in Italian devotional images of the fourteenth century (1954). The intimacy of the embrace, set within the solemn frame of the throne, combines the tenderness of the Sienese tradition with the monumental order of Florentine painting.

Behind the throne, on either side, stand two angels in blue robes, with haloes and folded hands, turning toward the Virgin in adoration. Their pale faces and gentle expressions recall the angels of Daddi's small altarpieces, and their symmetrical placement frames the enthroned group. The angels represent the heavenly court, and their presence extends the throne's architecture into a vision of the celestial realm, within which the saints in the foreground stand as intercessors on behalf of the faithful.

In front of the throne, on the left, stands Saint John the Baptist, bearded and with dishevelled hair, in a rose-pink mantle over his camel-hair garment. He holds a slender cross-staff in one hand and raises the other in a gesture that points toward the Virgin and Child, as the forerunner who announced the coming of the Messiah. His bare feet rest on the ground before the throne. On the right stands a young female saint in golden-yellow robes, with a crown or diadem on her head, holding a small book and a palm. The palm identifies her as a virgin martyr, but her identity cannot be determined with certainty from these attributes alone.

The saints are painted on a slightly smaller scale than the Virgin, according to the hierarchical conventions of the period, and they are turned toward the enthroned group, participating in the sacred conversation. The Baptist's gesture of indication and the female saint's gentle inclination link them to the Virgin and Child and direct the viewer's attention toward the centre. Their selection presumably reflects the personal devotion of the patron for whom the tabernacle was painted, whose coats of arms may appear on the painted base.

The base of the tabernacle is decorated with a band of dark painted quatrefoils, some of which contain coats of arms, alternating with foliate ornament. If these heraldic devices are original, they would identify the family that commissioned the tabernacle, and they could provide valuable evidence for its provenance and date. The present frame, with its pinnacles, crockets, finial and twisted columns, is richly carved and gilded. The neo-Gothic character of some of its elements, however, suggests that it may have been restored or partly reconstructed. Since the Scripps catalogue already described the work as a triptych in 1889, any such intervention probably predates its arrival in Detroit. Attached to the wooden stand is a small brass plaque recording the gift of James E. Scripps and identifying the painter as "Allegretto Nuzi, Umbrian School, active 1346–1373," a label that reflects the state of scholarship in the early twentieth century rather than the work's original presentation.

The central panel thus presents a complete devotional image in miniature: the Virgin enthroned in a celestial shrine, adored by angels and flanked by saints who intercede for the faithful. The graceful figures, the delicate modelling, the refined gold decoration and the harmonious colour are characteristic of Allegretto's early work, when the influence of Bernardo Daddi was strongest. In its combination of monumental order and intimate tenderness, the panel shows how the young painter from Fabriano condensed the grandeur of a Florentine altarpiece into the portable format of a private tabernacle.

The tabernacle entered the Detroit Museum of Art with the collection of James E. Scripps, whose catalogue of 1889 described it as a triptych attributed to Giotto. The attribution was repeated in the periodical The Collector in 1891 and again in the museum's handbook of 1910, a characteristic example of the late nineteenth-century habit of giving the finest Trecento panels to the most celebrated name of the period. It was Bernard Berenson who, in his Central Italian Painters of the Renaissance of 1908, first ascribed the triptych to Allegretto Nuzi.

The small brass plaque still attached to the base, which describes the painter as belonging to the "Umbrian School," reflects the classification of the period, when Allegretto was grouped with the painters of central Italy rather than recognised as the founder of a distinct Fabrianese school. Berenson's attribution was soon confirmed by [Chandler R. Post](/scholars.html#post-chandler-rathfon), who devoted an article to the work in 1915, and it has been accepted by all subsequent scholarship, from Berenson's own studies of Allegretto's early works (1922 and 1930) and [Raimond van Marle](/scholars.html#van-marle-raimond)'s survey of 1925 to Lionello Venturi, [Wilhelm Valentiner](/scholars.html#valentiner-wilhelm-r), [Alessandro Marabottini](/scholars.html#marabottini-alessandro)'s monograph of 1951–1952, Federico Zeri and Burton Fredericksen's census of 1972, Miklós Boskovits in 1975 and Fabio Marcelli's monograph of 2004.

The date has been less settled: the museum's catalogue of 1944 proposed about 1365, whereas later scholarship has tended to place the tabernacle in Allegretto's early Daddesque phase. In 2008 the tabernacle was exhibited at the Uffizi in L'eredità di Giotto. Arte a Firenze 1340–1375, a recognition of its place in the Florentine painting of the mid-fourteenth century from which the young painter of Fabriano drew his formation.

#### Left Wing: The Nativity and the Annunciation to the Shepherds

The left wing presents the Nativity of Christ, combined with the Annunciation to the Shepherds, in a vertical composition that fills the narrow panel from top to bottom. In the gable above, a half-length figure, probably the Archangel Gabriel, turns toward the right, toward the Annunciate Virgin in the gable of the opposite wing. The narrative is organised in three superimposed zones, which lead the viewer's eye from the hillside of the shepherds at the top, through the choir of angels in the middle, to the manger at the bottom. This vertical arrangement, dictated by the tall, narrow format of the wing, is typical of the narrative panels of portable tabernacles.

In the upper zone, set in a rocky landscape of hills and trees, an angel swoops down from the sky toward a shepherd standing on the hillside, bringing him the tidings of the birth of Christ. Around the shepherd, sheep graze or rest on the slopes, and a dog lies among them. The episode follows the account of the Gospel of Luke, in which the angel appears to the shepherds keeping watch over their flock by night. The landscape, rendered in greens and browns with simplified rocky forms, belongs to the Giottesque tradition of pictorial space, which Allegretto adapted to the small scale of the tabernacle.

In the middle zone, above the roof of the stable, a choir of angels gathers in adoration, their haloed heads arranged in tiers, some with joined hands, some singing or playing. They fill the space between the landscape of the shepherds and the scene of the Nativity below, and they represent the heavenly host that, according to Luke, praised God and sang "Glory to God in the highest." The dense grouping of the angels, their gold haloes shining against the dark background, creates a rich decorative pattern that animates the centre of the composition.

In the lower zone, the Nativity takes place in the shelter of a rocky cave or stable. The Virgin, in a dark mantle over a red gown, kneels or bends over the manger, in which the Christ Child lies swaddled in white, while the ox and the ass, their heads visible above the manger, warm him with their breath. Rays of light descend from above onto the Child, marking the divine nature of the newborn. The Virgin's attitude of adoration, rather than of maternal care, reflects the devotional interpretation of the Nativity that spread in the fourteenth century, in which Mary kneels before her Son as the first of the faithful to worship him.

In the lower right corner sits Saint Joseph, his head resting on his hand in an attitude of meditation or sleep. His figure, in a blue and golden mantle, turned away from the central scene, follows the traditional iconography of the pensive Joseph, who contemplates the mystery of the Incarnation or rests after the journey to Bethlehem. His position, separated from the Virgin and Child, emphasises the divine origin of the Child and the unique role of Mary in the Incarnation.

The composition of the Nativity, with its superimposed zones and its combination of several episodes in a single panel, reflects the narrative traditions of Tuscan painting, which Allegretto had studied in Florence. The Giottesque model of the Nativity, familiar from the Arena Chapel and the Lower Church at Assisi, is adapted here to the tall format of the wing and enriched with details, such as the choir of angels, that heighten its devotional appeal. The small figures are painted with remarkable delicacy, and their gestures and expressions convey the joy and wonder of the event.

In the gable above, the half-length figure turned toward the right completes the Annunciation that spans the two wings. The figure is small and worn, and its identification as Gabriel rests on its position and orientation, since the angel traditionally occupied the left side of a divided Annunciation. When the tabernacle was open, the angel and the Virgin faced each other across the central panel, framing the whole object within the mystery of the Incarnation.

The left wing thus presents, in a single vertical composition, the beginning of Christ's earthly life: the announcement to the shepherds, the heavenly praise and the adoration of the newborn Child. It forms the counterpart to the Crucifixion on the right wing, which presents the end of that life. Together, the two wings frame the enthroned Virgin and Child of the central panel with the two decisive moments of the story of salvation, the Incarnation and the Redemption.

#### Right Wing: The Crucifixion

The right wing presents the Crucifixion, in a composition that fills the narrow panel with the tall Cross and the group of mourners at its foot. In the gable above, a half-length figure, probably the Annunciate Virgin, kneels with her hands crossed on her breast, completing the Annunciation begun in the gable of the left wing. The wing forms the counterpart to the Nativity, so that the tabernacle, when open, set the birth and death of Christ on either side of the enthroned Virgin and Child.

At the centre of the panel, the Cross rises against the gold ground, with a red titulus inscribed INRI above Christ's head. Christ hangs dead upon it, his arms stretched along the crossbar, his head bowed and his body sagging under its own weight. His slender body, modelled in pale flesh tones, is girded by a loincloth, and his feet are nailed to the upright. The figure, conceived in the Tuscan type of the *Christus patiens*, dominates the upper part of the panel, and its isolation against the gold ground conveys the solitude of death.

At the foot of the Cross, on the left, the Virgin swoons in grief, supported by two holy women who hold her up and gaze at her with sorrow. The motif of the fainting Virgin, the *spasimo*, had been introduced into Italian painting in the late thirteenth century and became widespread in the fourteenth, particularly in Tuscan Crucifixions. It expressed the Virgin's participation in the Passion of her Son, the *compassio* that made her the model of all Christian mourning.

Kneeling at the foot of the Cross, Mary Magdalene, in a brilliant vermilion gown, embraces the wood with both arms, raising her face toward the feet of Christ. The same motif appears in Allegretto's Crucifixions in Berlin, Bern and Richmond, and it offered the viewer a model of affective devotion and penitence. The intense red of the Magdalene's gown creates a focal point at the base of the composition, leading the viewer's eye up the Cross to the body of Christ.

On the right stands Saint John the Evangelist, in a rose-pink mantle over a blue tunic, looking up toward Christ with an expression of sorrow. His figure, standing apart from the group of women, balances the composition and completes the traditional group of mourners at the Cross. The contrast between the collapsing Virgin on the left and the upright John on the right creates a dynamic asymmetry that animates the scene.

The ground at the foot of the Cross is rendered as a rocky hill, the Golgotha of the Gospels, and the gold ground above is enriched by punched decoration along its edges. The figures are small and delicately painted, with graceful proportions and expressive gestures that convey the grief of the mourners. The narrative clarity of the composition, which presents the essential elements of the Crucifixion in a compact and legible form, suits the devotional function of the tabernacle.

The Crucifixion on the right wing thus completes the programme of the tabernacle. The Nativity and the Crucifixion, the beginning and the end of Christ's earthly life, frame the Virgin and Child enthroned in the centre, while the Annunciation in the gables embraces the whole object within the mystery of the Incarnation. For the owner of the tabernacle, it offered a portable image of the entire history of salvation, to be opened in private prayer and closed for protection or travel.

In its style, the Detroit tabernacle is fully consistent with Allegretto's early Daddesque manner. The graceful figures, the delicate modelling, the harmonious colour and the refined gold decoration recall the small portable altarpieces of Bernardo Daddi and his workshop, which were produced in great numbers for private devotion in Florence in the 1330s and 1340s. The tabernacle shows how Allegretto adopted this Florentine type and brought it to the Marche. It stands, together with the Bern triptych and the Berlin diptych, among the finest examples of his devotional painting on a small scale.

### Triptych of the Coronation of the Virgin with Saints, Prophets and Angels, c. 1360

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-25.jpg" alt="Gothic triptych by Allegretto Nuzi on gold ground: in the centre Christ in a blue tunic and golden mantle crowning the Virgin, who bows with hands crossed in a white patterned mantle, both seated on a throne draped in red with a green pattern, with four kneeling angels in blue and yellow playing instruments below; in the side panels crowds of haloed saints and prophets turned toward the centre, including Saint Francis in a brown habit on the left and Saint John the Baptist with a scroll and Saint Peter with keys and a book on the right; two small adoring angels in trefoils in the side gables">
  <figcaption>The Coronation of the Virgin, c. 1360, tempera and gold on panel, Southampton City Art Gallery, purchased in 1951 through the Frederick William Smith Bequest Fund, inv. SOTAG 1403.</figcaption>
</figure>

#### Central Panel: The Coronation of the Virgin

The Southampton triptych is one of Allegretto's most ambitious surviving altarpieces and, according to the gallery, by far the earliest work in its collection. The central panel presents the Coronation of the Virgin, the heavenly consummation of Mary's life, in which Christ crowns his Mother as Queen of Heaven. The subject, drawn not from Scripture but from theological reflection and liturgical tradition, became one of the most popular themes of Italian altarpieces in the fourteenth century, especially in Florence, where painters such as Bernardo Daddi and the Cione brothers gave it definitive form. Allegretto's version, painted around 1360, reflects this Florentine model, which he had come to know during his years in Tuscany.

Christ and the Virgin sit side by side on a single broad throne, whose back and seat are covered with a cloth of red patterned with small dark green trefoils. The throne rises into a pointed form beneath the cusped arch of the panel, and its red cloth fills the upper part of the composition, creating a warm, luminous field against which the two figures stand out. The sharing of the throne between Mother and Son expresses the theology of the Coronation: the Virgin, assumed into heaven body and soul, is raised to reign beside Christ as Queen of the celestial court.

Christ, on the right, is represented as a bearded man in the prime of life, with long brown hair and a grave, intent expression. He wears a tunic of intense blue and a mantle of golden yellow patterned with ornament, which falls over his knees in broad folds. With both hands he raises a jewelled crown and places it on his Mother's head, leaning toward her in a gesture that combines solemnity and tenderness. The deep blue of his tunic, set against the red of the throne and the gold of his mantle, gives his figure a commanding presence.

The Virgin, on the left, bows her head to receive the crown, her hands crossed on her breast in a gesture of humility and acceptance. She wears a white veil and a mantle of white brocade patterned with gold motifs over a gown of pale blue, also decorated with ornament. Her face, seen in profile, is delicate and serene, and her bowed posture contrasts with the upright figure of Christ. The white and gold of her garments, symbols of her purity and glory, distinguish her from the more intense colours of Christ's robes and create a harmonious contrast between the two figures.

In the lower part of the panel, four angels kneel before the throne, making music in honour of the Coronation. Two wear blue robes and two yellow-green robes, and all have wings of blue and white. The gallery notes that four angels provide the musical accompaniment. Among the instruments visible are a bowed instrument, a vielle, and a wind instrument. The music of the angels, evoking the eternal praise of the heavenly court, was an essential element of the iconography of the Coronation, and it gave the scene a sense of joyful celebration.

The arrangement of the angels, two on each side, facing each other in pairs, creates a symmetrical composition that balances the asymmetry of the throne above. Their kneeling postures and upturned faces direct the viewer's attention toward the central group, and their brightly coloured robes, the blue and yellow-green, form a luminous base for the composition. The figures are painted with the graceful proportions and soft modelling characteristic of Allegretto's mature style.

The panel is framed by a carved and gilded cusped arch, and the gable above contains a trefoil, now much worn, in which a half-length figure can still be discerned. The gold of the gable is decorated with foliate ornament. The richness of the decoration, the gold of the arch, the patterned textiles of the throne and the garments, and the brilliant colours of the angels' robes, gives the central panel an effect of great splendour, appropriate to the celebration of the Virgin's glory.

The central panel of the Southampton triptych shows Allegretto at the height of his powers as a painter of large altarpieces. The composition, derived from Florentine models, is clear and balanced, and the figures combine monumental dignity with grace and tenderness. The rich textiles and the brilliant colour reflect Allegretto's growing interest in decorative effects, which would become increasingly pronounced in his later works. The Coronation of the Virgin, the triumph of Mary in heaven, is presented here with a solemnity and splendour that make it one of the most impressive images of the Fabrianese school.

#### Left Panel: Saints and Old Testament Figures

The left panel presents a crowd of saints and biblical figures, arranged in tiers and turned toward the Coronation in the central panel. Beneath a cusped arch, against a gold ground, their haloed heads rise one above another to the top of the arch, creating the impression of a vast heavenly assembly of which only a part is visible. This arrangement, derived from Florentine Coronations in which the celestial court fills the lateral panels, gives the altarpiece the character of a vision of paradise, in which all the blessed participate in the glorification of the Virgin.

According to the gallery, the figures identifiable on the left panel include Saint Stephen with the stones of his martyrdom, King David with his harp, and Abraham with his knife and his son Isaac. Stephen, the first martyr, is recognisable in the upper part of the panel by the stones on his head. David, the royal ancestor of Christ and of the Virgin, and Abraham, the patriarch whose sacrifice of Isaac prefigured that of Christ, represent the Old Testament, whose promises were fulfilled in the New.

In the foreground stand two larger figures. On the right is Saint Francis of Assisi, in the brown habit of the Friars Minor, holding a red book and a slender cross, with the stigmata visible on his hands. His presence suggests a connection with the Franciscan order, perhaps as patrons of the altarpiece or as custodians of the church for which it was painted. On the left stands a bearded figure in a mantle of white brocade patterned with gold, holding a book and a knife. The knife could identify him as Abraham, as the gallery's account suggests, or as the apostle Bartholomew, whose attribute was also a knife. The identification should be verified.

Behind these foreground figures, the tiers of saints include figures of different ages and types: bearded elders, young men, a figure holding a large book, and figures in varied headgear. Their faces, turned toward the central panel, express devout attention, and their haloes, finely tooled, create a pattern of gold discs rising toward the top of the arch. The density of the crowd, in which heads overlap and bodies are partly concealed, conveys the multitude of the blessed gathered around the throne of the Virgin.

At the left edge of the panel, near its lower part, a fragment of an object cut by the edge of the panel is visible. According to the catalogue of the Museum of Fine Arts, Houston, part of the grill of Saint Lawrence, cut off on one of the Houston panels, continues onto the left lateral panel of the Southampton altarpiece. This fragment is the incontrovertible proof that the Houston panels once formed part of the same altarpiece. The Southampton triptych, in other words, is the central part of a larger polyptych, which originally extended further to the left, and probably to the right.

The colours of the left panel are rich and varied: the white brocade of the foreground figure, the brown of Saint Francis's habit, and the reds, blues and yellows of the saints behind. These colours echo those of the central panel, creating a chromatic unity across the altarpiece. The patterned textiles, in particular the white brocade of the foreground figure, correspond to the Virgin's mantle in the central panel and demonstrate Allegretto's attention to the depiction of precious fabrics.

In the gable above the arch, a trefoil contains a half-length figure of a youthful angel, dressed in blue and white, with hands crossed on the breast in a gesture of devotion. The figure is turned toward the centre of the altarpiece, and it corresponds exactly to the angel in the gable of the right panel. Together the two angels frame the central gable, whose trefoil, now much worn, once contained the principal image of the upper register. Their gesture of adoration extends the celestial homage of the Coronation to the summit of the altarpiece, so that the heavenly court, gathered around the throne in the main panels, is echoed by the angels in the gables.

The left panel of the Southampton triptych thus presents a portion of the heavenly court, in which saints of the New Testament and figures of the Old Testament are united in the celebration of the Virgin's glory. The combination of Old Testament patriarchs and kings with saints of the Christian era expresses the continuity of salvation history, which culminates in the Coronation of the Virgin. The panel, with its dense crowd of figures and its rich colours, is a remarkable example of Allegretto's ability to organise complex compositions within the narrow format of a lateral panel.

#### Right Panel: Saints and Prophets

The right panel corresponds to the left in composition and structure. Beneath a cusped arch, against a gold ground, a crowd of saints and biblical figures is arranged in tiers, turned toward the Coronation in the central panel. The heads of the figures rise one above another to the top of the arch, and their haloes create a rhythmic pattern of gold discs. The symmetry between the two lateral panels frames the central scene and gives the altarpiece a balanced and harmonious structure.

According to the gallery, the right panel includes Moses with his tablets of stone and Saint Peter with his keys and book. Moses is recognisable in the middle tiers by the horns that, according to a medieval tradition based on a translation of the Book of Exodus, radiated from his head when he descended from Mount Sinai. Saint Peter, the prince of the apostles, stands in the foreground on the right, in a blue tunic and a golden mantle, holding his keys and a book, and looking up toward the central panel.

In the foreground on the left stands Saint John the Baptist, in a mantle of pale blue lined with red over his camel-hair garment, holding a scroll on which part of the inscription ECCE AGNUS DEI, "Behold the Lamb of God," can be read. These words, spoken by John when he saw Jesus coming toward him, identify him as the forerunner who announced the coming of the Messiah. The gallery does not mention him, but his identification is secure from his costume and his scroll.

Behind the foreground figures, the tiers of saints include a Dominican saint in the black and white habit of the Order of Preachers, probably Saint Dominic, a bishop in a mitre, bearded elders, a figure holding an open book, and, at the top, young female saints with palms. The presence of a Dominican saint in the right panel, balancing Saint Francis in the left, brings together the founders of the two great mendicant orders, as in several of Allegretto's works. The identity of the other figures cannot always be established from their attributes.

The pairing of Saint John the Baptist and Saint Peter in the foreground of the right panel is significant. John, the last of the prophets, and Peter, the first of the apostles, represent the transition from the Old Covenant to the New, and they correspond to the Old Testament figures on the left panel. Together, the foreground saints of the two lateral panels form a company of intercessors who present the faithful to the Virgin and Christ.

The colours of the right panel echo those of the left and central panels: the pale blue and red of the Baptist's mantle, the gold and blue of Peter's garments, and the varied colours of the saints behind. The golden mantle of Saint Peter, in particular, corresponds to the golden mantle of Christ in the central panel, creating a visual link between the apostle and his Lord. The chromatic unity of the three panels contributes to the harmony of the altarpiece.

In the gable above the arch, a trefoil contains the corresponding half-length angel, in blue and white, with hands crossed on the breast, turned toward the centre. The symmetry between the two gable angels mirrors that of the lateral panels below and completes the balanced structure of the altarpiece. Their adoring attitude directs the viewer's gaze toward the central gable, reinforcing the upward movement of the whole composition, from the music-making angels at the foot of the throne, through the Coronation itself, to the summit of the altarpiece.

The Southampton triptych, completed by the two panels in Houston, originally formed a large polyptych, one of the most ambitious altarpieces of Allegretto's career. The Houston catalogue records that when Percy Straus acquired his two panels in 1922, they had the same moulding at the top as the Southampton panels, but they had been cut down by about one-sixth of their height. Neither the Southampton triptych nor the Houston panels has yet undergone dendrochronological testing or pigment analysis. In its original state, the altarpiece presented the Coronation at the centre, framed by a heavenly court of saints and Old Testament figures extending across five panels, from Saint Stephen, Saint Francis and the patriarchs on the left to Moses, Saint John the Baptist and Saint Peter on the right, all turned toward the throne. At the summit, the adoring angels in the side gables directed the gaze toward the central gable. The reconstruction gives an idea of the grandeur of Allegretto's conception, and it makes the Southampton triptych one of the most important documents of the painter's activity as a creator of large altarpieces.

### Two Panels with Saints and Angels from the Fabriano Pentaptych (wings of the Southampton Coronation of the Virgin)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-01.jpg" alt="Two gabled panels by Allegretto Nuzi on gold ground, each filled with rows of haloed saints: on the left Mary Magdalene in red with the palm and Saint Lawrence in a white dalmatic holding a palm and his iron grill, with the crowned Saint Elizabeth of Hungary carrying roses in her skirt, a Franciscan friar and female saints above them; on the right Saint Louis of Toulouse in a mitre and a blue mantle covered with gold fleurs-de-lis holding a crozier, Saint Catherine beside him, and above them Saint Paul, Saint Jerome in a red cardinal's hat, Saint Dominic and Saint Ursula; pairs of angels at the top of each panel">
  <figcaption>Saints and Angels, c. 1370, tempera and gold leaf on wood, 107.9 × 50.9 cm each, Houston, The Museum of Fine Arts, The Edith A. and Percy S. Straus Collection, inv. 44.568–.569.</figcaption>
</figure>

#### Left Panel: Mary Magdalene, Saint Lawrence and the Company of Female Saints

This panel and its companion are the outer wings of the great polyptych whose central Coronation of the Virgin and two inner panels are in the Southampton City Art Gallery. Their attribution to Allegretto Nuzi was first proposed by [F. Mason Perkins](/scholars.html#perkins-frederick-mason) in 1922, published by Lionello Venturi in 1931 and widely accepted since. Each panel is filled with four rows of saints and angels, arranged in groups of twos and threes on a gold ground, so that the eye moves upward through a dense assembly of haloed figures. Placed at the far left of the altarpiece, the panel formed the outer edge of a heavenly court that extended across five compartments toward the throne of the Virgin.

In the front row stand the two principal figures. On the left, Mary Magdalene, in a mantle of brilliant vermilion with a gold border, holds the palm of martyrdom and her ointment jar, the vessel with which she anointed Christ's feet and went to the tomb on Easter morning. Her face, seen in profile, is delicate and grave, and her rich red mantle, falling in ample folds, gives her figure a commanding presence at the edge of the composition. The Magdalene, the model of penitence and of contemplative love, was among the most widely venerated saints of the fourteenth century, and Allegretto represented her frequently, notably at the foot of the Cross in his Crucifixions.

Beside her stands Saint Lawrence, the deacon of the Roman Church martyred in 258, represented as a beardless youth in a white dalmatic with a broad orphrey of gold ornament and a golden cap. He holds the palm of martyrdom and, at his side, the iron grill on which, according to his legend, he was roasted alive. The grill is cut off at the right edge of the panel, and its continuation on the left lateral panel of the Southampton triptych is, as the Houston catalogue puts it, the incontrovertible proof of the original connection between the two groups of panels. This small overlap of a painted object across a join is a rare and valuable piece of material evidence for the reconstruction of a dismembered altarpiece.

In the centre of the row above them stands Saint Elizabeth of Hungary, or of Thuringia, crowned but wearing a nun's habit. She has gathered up her skirt to cradle red and white roses, evidence of her famous Miracle of the Roses, in which the bread she was carrying to the poor was turned into flowers when her husband demanded to see what she concealed. The combination of crown and habit expresses the essence of her legend: a princess who renounced her rank for a life of charity and penance. She was a member of the Third Order of Franciscans, and her presence is one of the indications of the Franciscan character of the whole altarpiece.

Elizabeth is flanked by an unidentified Franciscan friar, possibly Saint Anthony of Padua, in the brown habit of the Friars Minor, and by a female martyr carrying a palm frond, whose idealised female type recurs in the figures of the upper rows. Directly above Elizabeth, a young woman adorned with a large diadem looks out directly toward the viewer, the only figure in the panel to do so. Neither she nor the other female figures has been identified, although the habited woman beside her may be Saint Clare of Assisi, the founder of the Poor Clares and Elizabeth's Franciscan counterpart.

The uppermost row contains two young figures with large haloes but no attributes, who are believed to represent angels. Their idealised faces and simple garments distinguish them from the saints below, and they correspond to a similar pair at the top of the companion panel. Together with the music-making angels at the foot of the throne in the Southampton Coronation and the adoring angels in the gables, they complete the angelic presence that surrounds the Virgin's celestial court.

The composition of the panel is remarkable for its dense but legible arrangement. The figures are set in overlapping tiers, with the heads of the upper rows rising between the shoulders of those below, so that a crowd is suggested without confusion. Almost all the faces are shown in profile or three-quarter view, turned toward the right, that is, toward the centre of the altarpiece, and this uniform orientation creates a powerful sense of collective attention directed at the Coronation. The gold ground unifies the whole and the haloes, densely punched with floral ornament, form a shimmering pattern across the surface.

The stylistic hallmark of the panel, as the Houston catalogue observes, is its rich presentation, dominated by the gold ground and the heavy, intricately punched golden haloes, and extending to the sumptuous clothing, rendered with great attention to detail. This love for luxurious materials, the catalogue notes, predates the ornate style of Gentile da Fabriano, one of the outstanding representatives of the International Style. In the female saints of this panel, with their idealised faces and their delicate profiles, the Sienese element of Allegretto's formation is particularly evident, while the solidity of the figures recalls his Florentine training.

#### Right Panel: Saint Louis of Toulouse and the Company of Male Saints

The right panel, the outer wing at the opposite end of the altarpiece, corresponds to the left in size, structure and composition, but its figures are turned toward the left, again facing the centre. Its rows are dominated by male saints, whereas the left panel is dominated by female ones, so that the two wings framed the assembly in a balanced arrangement. The panel was cut down by about one-sixth of its height, as its companion was, probably on account of damage, and its original moulding at the top survives only in traces.

The most outstanding figure is a man draped in a blue mantle adorned with the golden fleurs-de-lis of the French king. His mitre and bishop's crook identify him as Louis of Anjou, bishop of Toulouse, who had a claim on the French crown but chose the church instead. Beneath the episcopal vestments he wears the brown habit of the Friars Minor, the sign of the Franciscan vocation for which he renounced his royal inheritance. He, like his royal counterpart Saint Elizabeth on the opposite panel, was closely associated with the Third Order of Franciscans, and the deliberate pairing of the two royal Franciscan saints at the outer edges of the altarpiece can hardly be accidental.

Beside Saint Louis stands Saint Catherine, in a pale gown with a golden mantle, holding a book and turning her face upward toward the centre. Her presence balances that of Mary Magdalene at the corresponding position on the left panel, so that each wing includes a prominent female saint in the front row. The two foreground figures of this panel, the royal bishop and the virgin martyr, are the most richly dressed in the whole ensemble, and their sumptuous garments, the fleur-de-lis mantle and the gold-lined robe, create a brilliant focus at the lower edge of the composition.

Directly above Saint Louis stands Saint Paul, recognisable by his high forehead and pointed beard, and beside him, possibly, Saint Anthony Abbot, holding a book. The row above contains three of the most readily identifiable figures of the panel: [Saint Jerome](/endnotes.html#fn-jerome), wearing a cardinal's hat and holding a pen, an anachronistic but standard attribute that presents the translator of the Vulgate as a prince of the Church; Saint Dominic, with a lily, the emblem of his purity and of the Order of Preachers; and Saint Ursula, with her banner. As in the left panel, the topmost pair of figures, with large haloes but no attributes, are believed to represent angels.

The arrangement of the saints follows the same principles as the left panel. The figures are set in overlapping tiers, their heads rising between the shoulders of those below, and almost all are shown in profile or three-quarter view, turned toward the centre. The variety of types is striking: the youthful bishop, the bearded apostles, the elderly Jerome, the Dominican in his black cappa, the young female saints. This variety, held within a rigorous compositional order, produces the impression of an entire community of the blessed, in which every age, rank and vocation is represented.

The presence of Saint Dominic on this panel, balancing the Franciscan friar on the left, brings together the two great mendicant orders, as in several of Allegretto's works. The prominence of the Franciscan saints, however, is unmistakable: Louis of Toulouse and Elizabeth of Hungary in the foreground of the two wings, the friar beside Elizabeth, and Saint Francis himself in the left lateral panel of the Southampton triptych. This Franciscan emphasis led Federico Zeri to argue that the pentaptych was most likely destined for the high altar of the church of San Francesco in Allegretto's native Fabriano. The church was demolished in 1864, and the reconstruction of its high altar remains theoretical.

The dating of the complex remains debated. Zeri argued for a date around 1370 and for a collaboration with Francescuccio Ghissi, whereas earlier scholars such as Alessandro Marabottini pointed to the panels' similarity with Bernardo Daddi and placed them in the early part of Allegretto's career. The Houston catalogue dates the two panels about 1370, while the Southampton gallery dates the Coronation to 1360, and the Houston comparative illustration gives it as about 1350. This range of nearly two decades reflects the difficulty of establishing a chronology for works that carry neither date nor signature.

The two Houston panels have a distinguished modern history. They passed through the hands of the dealers D'Atri in Paris, Ugo Jandolo in Rome and Enrico Testa in Florence before being acquired by Percy S. Straus in New York in November 1922, and they were bequeathed to the Museum of Fine Arts, Houston, in November 1944. They have been discussed by [Richard Offner](/scholars.html#offner-richard), Raimond van Marle, Bernard Berenson, Marabottini, Zeri and others, and their punchwork was included in [Erling Skaug](/scholars.html#skaug-erling)'s corpus of Trecento punch marks. Neither they nor the Southampton panels has yet undergone dendrochronological testing or pigment analysis, but, as the Houston catalogue states, the visual evidence for their original unity is indisputable.

### Pentaptych with the Madonna and Child and Saints Mary Magdalene, John the Evangelist, Bartholomew and Venantius

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-02.jpg" alt="Gothic pentaptych by Allegretto Nuzi with five half-length figures on gold ground under pointed arches: in the centre the Virgin in a dark mantle and a red gown with gold stars holding the Christ Child, who has his hand at his mouth; on the left Mary Magdalene in red with an ointment jar and the young Saint John the Evangelist holding an open book with Latin text; on the right the bearded Saint Bartholomew with a knife and a book and the young Saint Venantius with a white banner; in the gables a Crucifix in the centre and small roundels with the mourning Virgin, Saint John, Saint Anthony Abbot and a female saint with a palm">
  <figcaption>Madonna and Child with Saints Mary Magdalene, John the Evangelist, Bartholomew and Venantius, 1360s, tempera and gold on panel, c. 113–118 × 200–202 cm, Fabriano, Pinacoteca Civica "Bruno Molajoli" (property of the Curia vescovile of Fabriano-Matelica).</figcaption>
</figure>

#### Central Panel: The Madonna and Child

The Fabriano pentaptych is one of the most refined altarpieces that Allegretto painted for his native city, and one of the few that remain there. It belongs to the Curia vescovile of Fabriano-Matelica and is kept in the Pinacoteca Civica ""Bruno Molajoli"," where it was restored in 2014. The Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani places it among the principal works of Allegretto's mature phase, alongside the Apiro polyptych of 1366, the Macerata triptych of 1369 and the Urbino Madonna of 1372.
