In this phase the painter moved progressively away from the Florentine plastic vision and accentuated his links with Sienese art, above all with Ambrogio Lorenzetti. The composition follows the Florentine type of the half-length polyptych, with the Virgin and Child at the centre and four saints in the lateral compartments.

The Virgin is shown in half-length, turned slightly toward the Child whom she holds on her left arm. She wears a mantle of deep blue, now darkened almost to black, which covers her head and falls over her shoulders, with a gold star on her right shoulder. Beneath the mantle a red band frames her face, and she wears a gown of vermilion sprinkled with small gold stars, whose luminous pattern stands out against the dark mantle. The star on the shoulder and the stars on the gown allude to Mary's titles of *Stella Maris* and Queen of Heaven.

The Virgin's face is long and oval, with a straight nose, almond-shaped eyes and a small mouth, and it is modelled with soft transitions of tone that give it a gentle, luminous quality. She looks out toward the viewer with a grave, contemplative expression, and her head inclines slightly toward the Child. This melancholy gaze, a constant of Allegretto's Madonnas, suggests her foreknowledge of the Passion, which is made explicit by the Crucifixion in the gable directly above her head.

The Christ Child sits on his Mother's arm, dressed in a white tunic that leaves his legs bare. He turns toward the viewer and raises one hand to his mouth, with his fingers pressed against his lips, in a spontaneous childlike gesture that Allegretto also used in the Berlin diptych. With his other arm he reaches around his Mother's shoulder. The Virgin holds him with both hands, one supporting his body, the other holding his foot. The naturalism of the Child's pose and gesture, drawn from observation of real infants, gives the image an intimacy and warmth that temper the solemnity of the altarpiece.

The gold ground behind the figures is framed by a pointed arch, and the haloes of the Virgin and Child are richly tooled with floral ornament. The central panel is taller and broader than the lateral compartments, and its gable rises above theirs, affirming the pre-eminence of the Virgin and Child in the hierarchy of the altarpiece. The dark frame, with its engaged colonnettes and gables, appears to preserve the original carpentry of the polyptych, though this should be confirmed by the restoration report.

In the gable above the arch, against a gold field framed by spandrels of red decorated with gold foliate ornament, appears the crucified Christ. The Cross bears the titulus INRI, and the pale body of Christ hangs with bowed head. The image of the Crucifixion directly above the Virgin and Child establishes the vertical axis of the altarpiece, linking the Incarnation to the Passion. It is completed by the two roundels in the gables of the inner lateral panels, which show the mourning Virgin and Saint John. The Crucifixion is thus divided across three gables, in a manner that unifies the upper register of the polyptych.

The central panel displays the qualities that the Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani associates with Allegretto's mature style: the linear and decorative intentions visible in the rendering of fabrics and ornaments, and the accentuated relationship with Sienese art. The gown sprinkled with stars, the delicate modelling of the faces and the tender naturalism of the Child's gesture recall the art of Ambrogio Lorenzetti. At the same time, the monumental construction of the Virgin and the clarity of the composition reflect Allegretto's Florentine training.

The central panel thus establishes the tone of the whole altarpiece: intimate, tender and devotional, with a sense of quiet sorrow that anticipates the Passion. The combination of the Virgin's grave expression, the Child's spontaneous gesture and the Crucifixion above creates an image of great emotional depth. Painted for a Fabrianese patron, the panel shows Allegretto at the height of his maturity, combining the lessons of Florence and Siena in a language of great refinement.

#### Outer Left Panel: Saint Mary Magdalene

The outer left panel presents Mary Magdalene in half-length, beneath a pointed arch, against a gold ground. The Magdalene, the woman from whom Christ cast out seven devils, who stood at the foot of the Cross and who was the first witness of the Resurrection, was venerated as the model of the repentant sinner and of contemplative love. Her cult enjoyed extraordinary popularity in the fourteenth century, particularly among the mendicant orders, and she appears frequently in Allegretto's work.

The saint is shown turned in three-quarter view toward the central panel, her head inclined and her eyes lowered in an expression of quiet contemplation. She is wrapped entirely in red: a mantle of vermilion covers her head and falls over her shoulders, and beneath it she wears a gown of the same colour. Red was her traditional colour, symbolising the ardour of her love for Christ. The broad expanses of red, modelled with subtle gradations of tone, give the figure a strong and unified presence at the outer edge of the altarpiece.

In her hands the Magdalene holds her characteristic attribute, the jar of ointment with which she anointed Christ's feet. The jar, white and cylindrical, with a conical lid and decorated with incised ornament, is held delicately in both hands before her breast. Its precious appearance recalls the costly ointment of the Gospel account and alludes to the value of her devotion.

The Magdalene's face is oval and pale, with a long, straight nose, lowered eyes and a small mouth, and it is modelled with soft, luminous transitions of tone. The delicacy of the face and the gentle inclination of the head express the saint's humility and her inward devotion. The treatment of the face, with its delicate modelling and its tender expression, reflects the Sienese element of Allegretto's style, which the Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani identifies as dominant in this phase of his career.

The saint's halo, finely tooled with floral ornament, stands out against the gold ground, and the pointed arch of the panel frames her figure. The simplicity of the composition, a single figure against a gold ground, gives the panel a devotional intensity that invites contemplation. The red of the Magdalene's garments corresponds to the red of the Virgin's gown in the central panel, creating a chromatic link across the altarpiece.

In the roundel in the gable above the arch appears the half-length figure of Saint Anthony Abbot, bearded, in the dark habit of the hermits, holding a book. Saint Anthony, the father of monasticism, appears in several of Allegretto's works. His presence here, in the outer left gable, may reflect the devotion of the patron or the dedication of an altar. The small figure, painted against a gold ground within the roundel, is framed by red spandrels decorated with gold foliate ornament.

The placement of the Magdalene at the outer left, balanced by Saint Venantius at the outer right, frames the altarpiece with two figures of particular significance. The Magdalene represents the penitent sinner who became a saint through love, while Venantius, the patron saint of Fabriano, represents the civic devotion of the community for which the altarpiece was painted. The two figures together express the universal and the local dimensions of the altarpiece's devotional programme.

The Magdalene panel is one of the most refined in the pentaptych. The delicacy of the face, the harmony of the red garments and the elegance of the jar create an image of gentle, contemplative piety. It shows Allegretto's ability to create single figures of great devotional intensity, in which simplicity of composition is combined with refinement of execution.

#### Inner Left Panel: Saint John the Evangelist

The inner left panel presents Saint John the Evangelist, the beloved disciple of Christ, the author of the fourth Gospel and, according to tradition, of the Book of Revelation. He is represented as a beardless youth, in accordance with the tradition that he was the youngest of the apostles, and he stands at the Virgin's right hand, in the place of honour beside the central panel.

The saint is shown in half-length, turned in three-quarter view toward the viewer, with his long, curly golden hair falling to his shoulders. His face is youthful and grave, with wide-set eyes and a small mouth, and he gazes directly at the viewer with an expression of serious concentration. He wears a tunic of blue edged with gold and a mantle of pink, lined with red, which falls over his left shoulder and arm. The combination of blue and pink, one of the favourite colour harmonies of Florentine painting, gives the figure a luminous and elegant appearance.

In his hands John holds an open book, which he displays to the viewer, pointing with his right hand to the text. The pages bear the opening words of his Gospel, written in Gothic letters:

> In principio erat verbum et verbum erat apud Deum et Deus erat verbum. Hoc erat in principio,

"In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God. The same was in the beginning" (John 1:1–2). This text, which proclaims the divinity of Christ and the mystery of the Incarnation, is the theological key of the altarpiece. It is displayed immediately beside the Virgin holding the incarnate Word.

The juxtaposition of the Evangelist's text with the image of the Virgin and Child is highly significant. The Word that was in the beginning with God is the Child held in his Mother's arms in the central panel, and the Gospel text thus becomes a commentary on the image beside it. This intellectual and theological dimension distinguishes the pentaptych from simpler devotional altarpieces and suggests a patron of some learning, perhaps a cleric.

The saint's halo is finely tooled with floral ornament, and the pointed arch of the panel frames his figure against the gold ground. The treatment of the drapery, with its soft folds and its gold border, and the delicate modelling of the face show the refinement of Allegretto's mature style. The figure's direct gaze, addressed to the viewer, invites contemplation of the text he displays.

In the roundel in the gable above the arch appears the half-length figure of the Virgin, in a dark mantle over a red gown, raising both hands in a gesture of grief. She is one of the two mourners of the Crucifixion divided across the three central gables, facing the crucified Christ in the gable of the central panel. The presence of the mourning Virgin above the Evangelist is particularly apt, since John was the disciple to whom Christ entrusted his Mother from the Cross.

The placement of John at the Virgin's right hand, in the place of honour, reflects his special relationship with Christ and with Mary. As the beloved disciple and the adoptive son of the Virgin, he was the saint most closely associated with her, and his presence beside her in the altarpiece expresses this bond. His Gospel text, displayed beside the image of the incarnate Word, links him to the central mystery of the altarpiece.

The panel of Saint John is thus one of the most significant in the pentaptych, both for its theological content and for its artistic quality. The youthful figure, with its luminous colours and its grave expression, and the Gospel text, with its proclamation of the Incarnation, combine to create an image of great spiritual depth. It shows Allegretto's ability to combine devotional intensity with theological meaning in a single figure.

#### Inner Right Panel: Saint Bartholomew

The inner right panel presents Saint Bartholomew the Apostle, who according to tradition preached the Gospel in India and Armenia, where he was flayed alive and beheaded. He stands at the Virgin's left hand, beside the central panel, balancing Saint John on the opposite side. His cult was widespread in medieval Italy, and Allegretto represented him in several works, including the Berlin diptych.

The saint is shown in half-length, facing the viewer, with thick curly brown hair and a full, flowing beard. His face is strong and grave, with a straight nose, deep-set eyes and a firm mouth, and he gazes directly at the viewer with a penetrating expression. The strength and maturity of his features contrast with the youthful delicacy of Saint John on the other side of the Virgin, so that the two apostles flanking the central panel represent youth and maturity.

Bartholomew wears a tunic of golden yellow and a mantle of lilac, which falls over his left shoulder and is gathered across his body. In his right hand he holds a knife, the instrument of his martyrdom by flaying, raised before his breast. In his left hand he holds a large book bound in dark leather with metal fittings, the attribute of the apostles and of the Gospel they preached. The knife and the book together identify him as an apostle and a martyr.

The golden yellow of the tunic and the lilac of the mantle create a harmonious chromatic combination, which balances the blue and pink of Saint John on the opposite side. The folds of the mantle, modelled with soft gradations of tone, suggest the volume of the body beneath, and the gold border of the garments adds a touch of refinement. The figure has a solid, monumental presence that reflects Allegretto's Florentine training.

The saint's halo, finely tooled with floral ornament, stands out against the gold ground, and the pointed arch of the panel frames his figure. The directness of his gaze and the frontality of his pose give the figure an imposing authority, appropriate to an apostle and a martyr. His presence beside the Virgin and Child expresses his role as a witness to Christ and an intercessor for the faithful.

In the roundel in the gable above the arch appears the half-length figure of Saint John the Evangelist, in a pink mantle, with his hand raised to his face in a gesture of grief. He is the second mourner of the Crucifixion divided across the three central gables, facing the crucified Christ from the right, as the mourning Virgin faces him from the left. The Crucifixion group, spread across the three central gables, forms a unified narrative at the summit of the altarpiece.

The pairing of Saint Bartholomew with Saint John the Evangelist on either side of the Virgin is significant. Both were apostles, both were witnesses to Christ's life and teaching, and both were venerated as intercessors. Their contrast in age and character, the youthful John and the mature Bartholomew, creates a balanced composition and expresses the variety of the apostolic community.

The panel of Saint Bartholomew shows Allegretto's ability to create figures of strong character and monumental presence. The penetrating gaze, the full beard and the solid form give the saint an air of authority and dignity, while the harmonious colours and the refined decoration reflect the painter's mature style. The panel forms a worthy counterpart to Saint John on the opposite side of the Virgin.

#### Outer Right Panel: Saint Venantius

The outer right panel presents Saint Venantius, the youthful martyr of Camerino and patron saint of Fabriano. According to his legend, Venantius was a youth of noble family who, at the age of fifteen, was martyred under the emperor Decius after surviving a series of torments. His cult was deeply rooted in the Marche, and the cathedral of Fabriano bears his dedication. His presence in the pentaptych expresses the civic identity of the community for which the altarpiece was painted.

The saint is represented as a beardless youth, with short, curly golden hair, turned in three-quarter view toward the central panel. His face is delicate and serene, with a long nose, narrow eyes and a small mouth. He looks toward the Virgin and Child with an expression of quiet devotion, and his youthful beauty expresses the idealised innocence of the martyr who died for his faith as a boy.

Venantius wears a costume that marks his noble rank: a red tunic, visible at the arm and the lower edge, and over it a cloak of blue, fastened at the neck with a broad collar of dark fabric embroidered with gold ornament in the form of lozenges and crosses. The rich embroidery of the collar and the brilliant colours of the garments display Allegretto's love of precious textiles, which would become increasingly prominent in his later works.

In his right hand the saint holds a tall lance bearing a white banner, the standard of victory that was one of his traditional attributes. The banner, rising beside his head, is the emblem of the martyr's triumph over death and of the city that claimed him as protector. The same attribute appears in the Washington triptych of 1354, where Venantius also holds a banner, and it was characteristic of the saint's iconography in the Marche.

The saint's halo is finely tooled with floral ornament, and the pointed arch of the panel frames his figure against the gold ground. The combination of the youthful face, the rich costume and the banner creates an image of noble grace and civic dignity. The contrast between the brilliant blue and red of his garments and the gold ground gives the figure a luminous presence at the outer edge of the altarpiece.

In the roundel in the gable above the arch appears the half-length figure of a young female saint, with a palm and a diadem, turned toward the centre. She is a virgin martyr, but her identity cannot be determined from her attributes alone. Her presence in the outer right gable balances that of Saint Anthony Abbot in the outer left, so that the two outer roundels frame the Crucifixion group in the central gables.

The placement of Venantius at the outer right, corresponding to the Magdalene at the outer left, frames the altarpiece with two figures of different character. The Magdalene, the penitent sinner, and Venantius, the youthful martyr, represent two paths to holiness, penitence and martyrdom, and together they express the universal scope of the altarpiece's devotional programme. The civic dimension, embodied in Venantius, gives the altarpiece a particular meaning for the community of Fabriano.

The panel of Saint Venantius completes the programme of the Fabriano pentaptych. The Virgin and Child at the centre are flanked by four saints who represent the universal Church and the local community: the Magdalene and John on the left, Bartholomew and Venantius on the right. The Crucifixion group in the gables, with Saint Anthony Abbot and a female martyr, crowns the whole. Painted in Allegretto's maturity for a Fabrianese patron, the pentaptych is one of the most complete and harmonious expressions of his art, and it remains in the city for which it was made.

### Saint Augustine between Saint Nicholas of Tolentino and Saint Stephen (three lateral compartments of a dismembered polyptych)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-11.jpg" alt="Three Gothic panels by Allegretto Nuzi joined in one frame, with full-length saints on gold ground above a red floor: in the centre Saint Augustine, bearded, in a mitre and a blue cope with gold patterns over a black habit, holding a crozier and an open book; on the left Saint Nicholas of Tolentino in a black habit holding a lily and a red book; on the right the young deacon Saint Stephen in a white dalmatic decorated with blue and red motifs, holding a palm and a red book">
  <figcaption>Saint Augustine between Saint Nicholas of Tolentino and Saint Stephen, 1360s–early 1370s, tempera and gold on panel, c. 117–118 × 137–139 cm, Fabriano, Pinacoteca Civica "Bruno Molajoli", from the sacristy of Saint Augustine.</figcaption>
</figure>

#### Central Panel: Saint Augustine

The work now presented as a triptych is, according to the Pinacoteca of Fabriano, not originally a triptych at all but three lateral compartments of a polyptych, remounted in the form of a triptych. The three panels are of the same size, and none of them was designed as the centre of an altarpiece. Saint Augustine, who now occupies the middle, originally stood among the lateral saints flanking a central image that has been lost, presumably the Virgin and Child. The altarpiece has been connected with the church of Santa Maria Nova in Fabriano, which was served by the Augustinian community, and the Augustinian character of its saints supports this destination. The work was among those that yielded new discoveries on the occasion of the 2021 Fabriano exhibition.

Saint Augustine of Hippo, the Father of the Church whose Rule was followed by the Order of Hermits of Saint Augustine, stands frontally, gazing directly at the viewer. He is represented as a mature man with a short dark beard and a grave, penetrating expression. On his head he wears a tall mitre decorated with red, blue and gold ornament, and in his right hand he holds a crozier whose curved head rises above his shoulder. These are the insignia of his dignity as bishop of Hippo, the office he held for more than three decades in North Africa.

Beneath his episcopal vestments Augustine wears the black habit of the Augustinian friars, whose hood is visible at his neck. The combination is deliberate. The mendicant order that claimed Augustine as its founder presented him not only as a bishop and doctor of the Church but as a friar of their own, clothed in their habit, and this iconography was actively promoted by the Augustinians in the fourteenth century. The juxtaposition of the humble black habit and the magnificent episcopal vestments expresses the union of monastic poverty and pastoral authority that the order wished to embody.

Over the habit Augustine wears a cope of blue patterned with gold ornament, bordered with a broad orphrey in orange and gold decorated with a meander pattern, and fastened at the breast with a star-shaped morse. The richness of this vestment, with its dense gold patterns and its bold border, exemplifies the heightened decorativism that scholars have associated with Allegretto's last phase, with its increasingly abstract figures and garments quilted with gold. The contrast between the flat, patterned blue of the cope and the dark mass of the habit creates a striking effect against the gold ground.

With his left hand the saint holds an open book, which he presents to the viewer. Its two pages, written in Gothic minuscule with red initials and a red rubric, contain three texts. The first, at the top of the left page, is the opening of the Rule of Saint Augustine:

> Ante omnia, fratres carissimi, diligatur Deus, deinde proximus, quia ista praecepta sunt principaliter nobis data,

"Before all things, dearest brothers, let God be loved, and then your neighbour, because these are the chief commandments given to us."

The second, introduced by a red initial and continuing onto the right page, is a prayer circulated in the Middle Ages under Augustine's name:

> Totum cor meum flamma tui amoris accendat, et nihil in me relinquatur mihi, neque respiciam ad me ipsum, sed totus in te sim, cum totus in te ardeam, totus diligam te tamquam inflammatus a te. Confiteor tibi, Domine, in toto corde meo,

"May the flame of your love set my whole heart on fire, so that nothing in me is left to myself, nor do I look back upon myself, but that I may be wholly in you, burning wholly in you, loving you wholly as one set aflame by you. I confess to you, O Lord, with my whole heart."

The closing words echo the opening of Psalm 137 in the Vulgate numbering, and the red rubric beneath them, *Super psalmum CXXXVII*, presents the passage as drawn from Augustine's commentary on that psalm. The image of the heart burning with divine love evokes the flaming heart that was Augustine's emblem in medieval iconography, derived from the *Confessions*. The third text, opened by two further red initials and only partly legible, is a eulogy of the saint:

> Augustinus, lux doctorum, firma[mentum …] … malleus haereticorum … vas sac[rum] … fidelibus in gra[tia] Dei …,

"Augustine, light of the doctors, pillar [of the Church] … hammer of heretics … sacred vessel … to the faithful in the grace of God …"

These are the titles traditionally bestowed on Augustine as the greatest of the Latin Fathers and the adversary of the Manichaeans, Donatists and Pelagians, and the passage probably belongs to an antiphon or responsory for his feast. Taken together, the three texts present Augustine as the legislator of the order, as the doctor of divine love and as the champion of orthodoxy. They offered the friars who prayed before the altarpiece both the foundation of their common life and a model of ardent devotion.

The saint stands on a red ground decorated with a fine pattern, which continues across all three panels and gives them a common base line. The gold ground behind him is framed by a round-headed arch, and his halo is finely tooled. The full-length format, less common in Allegretto's work than the half-length figure, gives the saint a monumental presence, and his frontal pose and direct gaze confer on him an air of solemn authority.

The date of the panels is debated. The Pinacoteca's own materials and other accounts give about 1362, and one account of the 2021 exhibition dates them no later than 1362. Another report on the same exhibition dates the triptych about 1372, and the Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani regarded it as the artist's last work. A collaboration with Francescuccio Ghissi has also been proposed. The emphatic decoration of the vestments and the abstraction of the figures accord well with a late date, but the question cannot be settled without the evidence published in the 2021 catalogue.

The panel of Saint Augustine thus presents the founder of the order as bishop, doctor and legislator, clothed in the habit of his friars and displaying the Rule by which they lived. The magnificent cope and the rich decoration give the figure a splendour appropriate to his dignity, while the black habit expresses the humility of the mendicant life. Now placed at the centre of the ensemble, although originally a lateral figure, Augustine dominates the three panels as the spiritual father of the community that commissioned them.

#### Left Panel: Saint Nicholas of Tolentino

The left panel presents Saint Nicholas of Tolentino, the Augustinian friar who died at Tolentino, in the Marche, in 1305, and whose cult spread rapidly through the region after his death. He was formally canonised only in 1446, so that at the time the panel was painted he was venerated as *beatus* rather than as a canonised saint. His representation with a halo in the 1360s or early 1370s testifies to the strength of his local cult, which the Augustinians actively promoted.

The saint stands in the black habit of the Augustinian friars, with its hood gathered around his neck, and his head bears the tonsure. He is represented as a beardless man with a gentle, grave face, turned in three-quarter view toward the centre, that is, toward Saint Augustine. The simplicity of his dress and the gentleness of his expression convey the humility and the ascetic life for which he was renowned.

In his left hand Nicholas holds a lily, the emblem of his purity, and in his right hand a book bound in red, which the Pinacoteca identifies as the book of the Sacred Scriptures. The lily was his characteristic attribute from the earliest images of his cult, recalling the chastity and austerity of his life. The bright red of the book creates a vivid accent against the black of the habit.

The black habit, rendered as a broad, dark mass against the gold ground, gives the figure a severe and imposing silhouette. The folds of the habit, modelled with subtle gradations of tone, suggest the volume of the body beneath, while the dark colour isolates the figure against the luminous gold. The contrast between the dark habit and the gold ground, softened only by the red of the book and the white of the lily, gives the panel a sober dignity.

The presence of Saint Nicholas beside Saint Augustine reflects the Augustinian character of the altarpiece. Nicholas was the most celebrated saint of the order in the fourteenth century, and his inclusion alongside the order's founder expresses the order's pride in its recent holy men. For a community of Augustinian friars in Fabriano, not far from Tolentino, Nicholas would have been a particularly beloved and familiar figure.

The saint stands on the same red ground as Augustine and Stephen, and his halo is finely tooled. The round-headed arch of the panel frames his figure against the gold ground. The full-length format gives him the same monumental presence as the other two saints, so that the three figures form a unified group of equal importance.

The panel of Saint Nicholas is, together with the Saint Augustine, one of the most significant early images of the friar from Tolentino. It shows how the Augustinians used painting to promote the cult of their recent saints, presenting them with the attributes that would become standard in later iconography. The lily and the book, the black habit and the gentle expression, established a type that would be repeated throughout the following centuries.

The Saint Nicholas panel thus expresses the devotion of the Augustinian community to its most beloved saint. The simplicity and austerity of the figure, in contrast with the magnificence of Saint Augustine and Saint Stephen, express the humility of the friar. At the same time, his halo and his place beside the founder of the order affirm his holiness and his importance for the community.

#### Right Panel: Saint Stephen

The right panel presents Saint Stephen, the first martyr of the Church, one of the seven deacons appointed by the apostles, whose stoning is recounted in the Acts of the Apostles. His presence in an Augustinian altarpiece may reflect Augustine's own devotion to the protomartyr. Augustine described the miracles worked by Stephen's relics, which had been brought to North Africa, and preached on his feast. It may also reflect a particular dedication of the altar or the devotion of a patron.

Stephen is represented as a beardless young man with short brown hair, turned in three-quarter view toward the centre. His face is delicate and grave, with a long nose, narrow eyes and a small mouth. He looks toward Saint Augustine with an expression of quiet devotion, and his youthful appearance recalls the tradition that he was a young man at the time of his martyrdom.

The saint wears the dalmatic, the liturgical vestment of the deacon, which is the most richly decorated garment in the ensemble. It is of cream or white, covered with blue and red rosettes and cross-shaped motifs, and adorned with bands of orange and gold ornament in which lozenges and squares alternate. The rich decoration of the dalmatic, spread across the entire surface of the garment, is an outstanding example of the decorativism of Allegretto's late style.

In his right hand Stephen holds the palm of martyrdom, and in his left a book bound in red, which alludes to his role as a deacon, charged with the reading of the Gospel in the liturgy. The palm and the book together define Stephen as both martyr and minister of the Word. The red of the book echoes the red of Saint Nicholas's book on the opposite side, creating a chromatic symmetry across the ensemble.

The splendour of Stephen's dalmatic balances the magnificence of Augustine's cope, and together they contrast with the austere black habit of Saint Nicholas. This contrast between the ornate liturgical vestments of the bishop and the deacon and the simple habit of the friar expresses the diversity of vocations within the Church, from the pastoral and liturgical ministry to the mendicant life of poverty.

Stephen stands on the same red ground as the other two saints, and his halo is finely tooled. The round-headed arch of the panel frames his figure against the gold ground. The full-length format and the frontal pose give him a monumental presence, and his delicate face and elegant dalmatic give him an air of youthful grace.

The treatment of the dalmatic, with its dense patterns of rosettes and lozenges, shows Allegretto's mastery of the representation of precious textiles. The pattern is spread across the surface of the garment with little regard for its folds, so that the dalmatic reads less as a modelled garment than as a flat field of ornament. This tendency toward the flat, decorative treatment of textiles is characteristic of the painter's late works and anticipates the courtly art of Gentile da Fabriano.

The panel of Saint Stephen completes the ensemble of the three saints. Augustine, the founder of the order, stands at the centre, flanked by Nicholas of Tolentino, the order's most celebrated recent saint, and Stephen, the first martyr, whom Augustine himself venerated. Although they are no longer arranged as the painter intended, having originally flanked a lost central image, the three panels together form a coherent expression of Augustinian devotion. They show Allegretto at the most decorative phase of his art.

### Saint Bartholomew the Apostle (attr.)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-17.jpg" alt="Half-length panel of Saint Bartholomew, bearded with dark hair, glancing to the right, holding a flaying knife and a red book, wearing a yellow tunic and violet mantle against a gold ground with a punched halo; attributed to Allegretto Nuzi, c. 1350, Orvieto, Museo dell'Opera del Duomo">
  <figcaption>Saint Bartholomew the Apostle, c. 1350, tempera and gold on panel, Orvieto, Museo dell'Opera del Duomo.</figcaption>
</figure>

This small half-length panel of Saint Bartholomew, preserved in the Museo dell'Opera del Duomo at Orvieto and attributed to Allegretto Nuzi, is a fragment from the mid-fourteenth century, when Tuscan painting was being carried into Umbria and the Marche. The apostle appears against a gold ground, his torso cut by the lower edge of the panel, holding the flaying knife of his martyrdom and a red-bound book. Despite substantial losses to the gilding and abrasion of the flesh tones, the figure keeps a physical presence and psychological intensity that place it firmly in the post-Giottesque idiom. The generally proposed date of around 1350 locates the work at a pivotal point in the painter's career, between his Florentine training and his mature activity in his native Fabriano.

The panel's present format is almost certainly not its original one. The trimmed lower edge, the band of bare wood along the top, and the rows of punched rosettes that follow the upper margins of the gold ground all suggest that the image once sat beneath an arched or cusped frame. It was probably one lateral compartment of a polyptych or part of an upper register of half-length saints. The direction of the apostle's gaze supports this reading. His head is turned slightly and his eyes look sharply towards the viewer's right, a device commonly used to connect lateral saints with a central image, usually the Virgin and Child. Bartholomew would therefore have stood to the left of the central panel, and his glance would have directed the devotional attention of the beholder towards the principal subject of the altarpiece. The original ensemble, its commission, and the route by which the fragment reached the Orvieto collections need confirmation from documentary sources and the museum's records.

The iconography follows the conventions well established for Bartholomew by the fourteenth century. According to the tradition collected in [Jacobus de Voragine](/endnotes.html#fn-jacopo-da-varagine)'s *Legenda aurea*, the apostle preached in India and Armenia and was flayed alive before being beheaded. The knife he grasps is therefore both an instrument of torture and a badge of triumph over death. Here it is shown with a broad, dark, slightly curved blade and a riveted handle ending in a round black pommel, a realistic detail that makes the attribute concrete and even menacing. The book, bound in vermilion with gilt studs and a lozenge ornament, with its page edges rendered as fine white striations, identifies him as an apostle and bearer of the Gospel. Holding the two attributes together, the implement of his passion and the Word he died for, sums up the saint's identity in a single, compact gesture.

The treatment of the head shows the painter's grasp of Florentine physiognomic types. The dark, thick hair and full, flowing beard recall the description of Bartholomew in the *Legenda aurea*, where he is given black curling hair, large eyes, and a long beard. The painter departs from that source in leaving the ear exposed, where the text describes it as covered by the hair. The face is built with firm plastic logic: a long, straight nose, a strongly modelled brow, cheekbones catching the light, and a small, slightly parted red mouth set within the mass of the beard. Above all it is the sideways glance, with the whites of the eyes clearly visible, that gives the figure its alert, almost apprehensive intensity. This is no impassive icon but a saint who seems aware of his own fate, and that inward animation is a hallmark of the best Florentine painting of the 1340s.

The drapery and colour show a sophisticated balance between Tuscan structure and a more lyrical chromatic taste. The saint wears a golden-yellow tunic whose broad, soft folds define the shoulders and chest, and over it a mantle of pale violet shading into deeper lilac. The mantle is drawn around the right shoulder and falls in long vertical folds along the right side of the panel. Gold borders trace the edges of both garments and catch the light of the ground, tying the figure to the surrounding field. The complementary relation between yellow and violet, punctuated by the intense red of the book, produces a harmony that is refined yet sonorous. The yellow is especially notable. Blending into the gold background, it gives the figure an almost luminous quality, as though the saint were partly made of the same substance as the celestial field behind him.

The technical features of the panel repay close attention. The halo has an inner field worked with fine granulation, scattered with six-petalled rosette punches and bordered by rows of circular punch marks. This is a vocabulary of ornament closely comparable to that used in Florentine workshops around the middle of the century. Punch tools of this kind are a useful instrument for attribution and dating, since they moved between workshops with painters and assistants. The condition of the work, meanwhile, shows its material stratigraphy. Large areas of lost gilding expose the grey preparation beneath, and the network of craquelure across the gold registers the ageing of the gesso layer. The flesh tones appear dark and olive, probably because the upper pink layers have been abraded and the green-earth underpainting (*verdaccio*) shows through, a phenomenon common in Trecento panels painted according to the Giottesque method. This deterioration now accentuates the shadowed, brooding character of the face, but it should not be mistaken for the painter's original intention.

The attribution to Allegretto Nuzi rests on stylistic comparison and should be treated with appropriate caution, as the "attributed" qualification indicates. The monumentality of the figure, the plastic construction of the head, and the Daddesque character of the ornament are consistent with Nuzi's Florentine formation. The warm palette and the concentrated emotional tone anticipate his later works in the Marche. Other painters in the Florentine orbit of the 1340s and 1350s have similar traits, so an exact attribution will depend on closer analysis of the punch tools, the panel construction, and any identification of companion fragments. Whatever its final attribution, the Orvieto Bartholomew is an eloquent witness to the transmission of Florentine art into central Italy around 1350. Although damaged and removed from its original context, it keeps the dignity and inner life that the best painters of the generation after Giotto gave to the saints of their altarpieces.

### The Crucifixion with the Holy Women, Saint John the Evangelist, Mary Magdalene and Angels

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-04.jpg" alt="Small gabled panel by Allegretto Nuzi on a worn gold ground showing red underlayer: Christ on the Cross with four dark-winged angels around him holding bowls to catch his blood; Mary Magdalene in red kneels and embraces the foot of the Cross; on the left the Virgin supported by holy women; on the right Saint John in a pink mantle over a green tunic looking up; at the top a pelican feeding its young in a nest above a green tree growing from the Cross">
  <figcaption>The Crucifixion, 1355–60, tempera and gold leaf on panel, 43.5 × 20 cm, New York, The Metropolitan Museum of Art, Robert Lehman Collection, 1975, inv. 1975.1.106.</figcaption>
</figure>

This small gabled panel, less than half a metre high, belongs to the group of devotional Crucifixions that Allegretto painted for private patrons, alongside those in Berlin, Bern and Richmond. The Metropolitan Museum attributes it to Allegretto di Nuzio and dates it to about 1355–60, placing it in the years following the Washington triptych of 1354, in the painter's mature phase. Its format and composition suggest that it was the right wing of a diptych whose left wing, now lost or unidentified, probably showed the Virgin and Child. The panel entered the Metropolitan Museum in 1975 with the Robert Lehman Collection, which Philip and Carrie Lehman had begun around 1905 and which their son Robert bequeathed to the museum. It is catalogued in the first volume of the collection's catalogue, devoted to Italian paintings, by [John Pope-Hennessy](/scholars.html#pope-hennessy-john).

The composition is dominated by the tall Cross, which rises from the rocky ground of Golgotha to the gable. Christ hangs dead upon it, his arms stretched along the crossbar, his head bowed onto his right shoulder, his body slender and softly modelled in pale, greenish tones. A transparent loincloth is knotted at his hips, and his crossed feet are fixed by a single nail. The wound in his side bleeds, and the blood from his feet runs down the upright of the Cross. The figure belongs to the Tuscan type of the *Christus patiens*, and its restrained pathos and graceful proportions are characteristic of Allegretto's devotional painting.

The most distinctive feature of the panel is the group of four angels surrounding the crucified Christ. Two half-length angels emerge from the upper corners of the field, at the level of the crossbar, each holding a bowl or chalice toward Christ's hands. Two further angels, with dark, outspread wings, hover beside his body at the level of his chest. The one on the left holds a bowl to catch the blood from the wound in his side, while the one on the right extends a hand toward him. The motif of angels gathering Christ's blood in chalices, which spread through Italian painting after Giotto's Crucifixion in the Arena Chapel, gives the scene an explicitly Eucharistic meaning: the blood shed on the Cross is the same blood offered in the chalice of the Mass.

At the foot of the Cross, Mary Magdalene kneels on the ground in a gown of brilliant vermilion, her long golden hair falling down her back, and embraces the wood with both hands, raising her face toward Christ. On the left, the Virgin, in a dark mantle, sinks in grief and turns her face upward, supported by a holy woman in a red mantle who holds her from behind. Beside them, a bearded male figure with a halo, wrapped in an olive-gold mantle, bends tenderly over the Virgin. He cannot be Saint John the Evangelist, who is represented beardless and standing on the right, and his identity remains uncertain. He may be one of the disciples who, according to the Gospels, witnessed the Crucifixion from afar, or Joseph of Arimathea or Nicodemus, who would later take Christ's body down from the Cross.

On the right, Saint John the Evangelist stands in a rose-pink mantle over a green tunic, gazing up at Christ with an expression of sorrow. The composition thus brings together the mourners of the Crucifixion, and, as in Allegretto's other Crucifixions, the Magdalene embracing the Cross offers the viewer a model of affective prayer.

In the gable, above the Cross, a pelican nests in a spray of green foliage, piercing her breast to feed her young with her blood. The foliage grows from the top of the Cross itself, transforming the instrument of death into a living tree, the *lignum vitae*. The same combination of the pelican and the flowering Cross crowns the Crucifixion of the Berlin diptych, and the pelican also appears in the Richmond Crucifixion. The recurrence of these motifs shows that Allegretto developed a consistent symbolic formula for his devotional Crucifixions. In the Metropolitan panel the formula is enriched by the angels with chalices, so that the theme of the saving blood runs from the summit of the panel, where the pelican feeds her young, to the Cross, where the angels gather the blood of Christ.

The gold ground of the panel has suffered severe abrasion, and over large areas the red bole beneath the gold is now exposed, giving the background its present reddish appearance. The original effect would have been one of uniform, luminous gold, against which the figures stood out with brilliant clarity. The punched border along the edges of the panel and the haloes of the figures, tooled with rings of small circles, survive better and still show the refinement of the original decoration. The panel also shows vertical splits in the wood and losses of paint, particularly in the gable, where the pelican and the foliage are much worn.

Despite its condition, the panel displays the qualities of Allegretto's devotional painting. The figures are graceful and softly modelled, their gestures eloquent and restrained, and the composition, although crowded, remains clear and legible. The dark-winged angels, the vermilion of the Magdalene, the pink and green of Saint John and the dark and red mantles of the holy women create a rich chromatic harmony. The expressive intensity of the mourners, in particular the fainting Virgin and the upturned face of the Magdalene, reflects the Sienese element of Allegretto's style, derived from his study of the Lorenzetti.

The Metropolitan Crucifixion thus takes its place within a coherent group of works in which Allegretto developed a distinctive devotional formula for the Passion. The pelican and the leafy Cross, the Magdalene embracing the Cross and the fainting Virgin recur, with variations, across these works, while the angels gathering the blood in chalices give this panel its particular Eucharistic emphasis. Together, these Crucifixions document the importance of the small devotional image in Allegretto's workshop and the refinement with which he adapted Florentine and Sienese models to the needs of private piety.

### Five Apostles in Half-Length (fragment of a predella)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-05.jpg" alt="Long horizontal panel by Allegretto Nuzi with five half-length apostles on gold ground, each under a round gilded arch on twisted columns: from left, a bald bearded apostle in an ochre mantle holding a red book; a young apostle in a red mantle raising his hand and holding a green book; a bearded apostle holding a large knife and a red book, in a patterned tunic and lilac mantle; a young apostle in a blue tunic and red mantle holding a dark book; and Saint Peter, grey-bearded and tonsured, holding a large key and a book, with a white band marked with black crosses over an orange mantle">
  <figcaption>Five Apostles in Half-Length, fragment of a predella, c. 1360, tempera and gold leaf on panel, 35.3 × 135.5 cm, Strasbourg, Musée des Beaux-Arts, inv. MBA 168; 44.R.20.</figcaption>
</figure>

#### First Compartment: An Elderly Apostle

The Strasbourg panel is a fragment of the predella of a large altarpiece, the horizontal base on which the main panels of a polyptych rested. It contains five apostles in half-length, each set beneath a round arch of an arcade carved in relief and gilded, supported by twisted colonnettes with foliate capitals. The sequence of arches continues beyond the present edges of the panel: at the left, part of a sixth arch is visible, and the damaged ends of the moulding show that the predella has been cut. The complete predella probably contained the twelve apostles, arranged on either side of a central image, perhaps Christ or the Man of Sorrows. Such a programme was common in Tuscan and Marchigian altarpieces of the fourteenth century.

The first apostle, at the left, is an elderly man with a high, bald forehead, short grey-brown hair and a full beard. He is shown in three-quarter view, turned toward the right, that is, toward the centre of the predella, with a grave and concentrated expression. His features, with their strong nose and deep-set eyes, are modelled with the soft greenish underlayer and warm highlights of Trecento tempera technique.

The apostle wears a blue tunic and over it a mantle of golden ochre, which falls over his shoulders and is drawn across his body. In his hands he holds a book bound in red, raised before his breast, the attribute common to all the apostles as preachers of the Gospel. The book is the only attribute visible, and it is not sufficient to identify the apostle with certainty. His type, with the bald forehead and the beard, has sometimes been associated with Saint Paul or Saint Andrew, but without a distinguishing attribute such as Paul's sword or Andrew's cross the identification must remain open.

The ochre of the mantle, close in tone to the gold ground, gives the figure a luminous but subdued presence, while the blue of the tunic and the red of the book provide chromatic accents. The folds of the mantle are modelled with soft gradations of tone, and they suggest the volume of the body beneath with restraint.

The apostle's halo, finely tooled with punched ornament, stands out against the gold ground, and the round arch above frames his head. The carved arcade, with its moulded arches and foliate spandrels, creates a series of niches that isolate each apostle while uniting them in a continuous rhythm across the predella. The left edge of this compartment is damaged, and part of the adjoining arch has been lost.

The figure shows the qualities of Allegretto's mature style: the solid construction of the half-length figure, derived from his Florentine training, and the gentle modelling of the face, reflecting his study of Sienese painting. The gravity of the expression and the dignity of the pose give the figure an authority appropriate to an apostle.

The orientation of the apostle toward the right indicates his place in the original predella. Since the figures on this fragment face toward the right, and Saint Peter stands at the right end, it is likely that the fragment formed the left half of the predella, and that the centre of the altarpiece lay beyond Peter. On the other side of the centre, the remaining apostles would have faced toward the left, in a symmetrical arrangement.

The first compartment thus opens the sequence of apostles with a figure of dignity and gravity. Although the apostle's identity cannot be determined, his presence in the predella contributes to the programme of the altarpiece, in which the apostles, as the foundation of the Church, supported the image of the Virgin and saints above. The predella's function as the base of the altarpiece gave this programme a theological meaning: the Church is built on the foundation of the apostles.

#### Second Compartment: A Youthful Apostle with Raised Hand

The second apostle is represented as a beardless youth, with short, curly auburn hair, turned in three-quarter view toward the viewer. His face is gentle and serious, with wide-set eyes and a small mouth, and he looks out of the panel with a direct and calm gaze. His youthful appearance distinguishes him from the bearded apostles around him.

He wears a pink tunic edged with gold at the neck and a mantle of brilliant red, which falls over his left shoulder and is drawn across his body. The combination of pink and red, set against the gold ground, gives the figure a warm and luminous presence. The folds of the mantle are modelled with gradations of red and deep crimson, suggesting the volume of the body beneath.

The apostle raises his right hand, with the palm open, in a gesture of speech or witness, and in his left he holds a book bound in green. The gesture of the raised hand, which recalls the rhetoric of preaching, gives the figure an animation that distinguishes it from the more static poses of the other apostles. It may be read as an allusion to the apostolic mission of proclaiming the Gospel.

The youthful type could identify the apostle as Saint John the Evangelist, traditionally represented as the youngest of the apostles. However, the fourth apostle of the panel is also beardless and youthful, and without a specific attribute neither can be identified with certainty as John. Other apostles, such as Philip or Thomas, were also sometimes represented as young men.

The halo is finely tooled, and the round arch above frames the head. The figure fills the compartment from the base of the arcade to the arch, and the raised hand extends into the space of the adjoining twisted colonnette, creating a sense of movement across the arcade.

The treatment of the face, with its soft modelling and gentle expression, and the harmony of the warm colours show the refinement of Allegretto's mature style. The raised hand, rendered with delicate fingers, and the elegant fall of the mantle give the figure a grace that recalls the Sienese component of his art.

The pairing of this youthful apostle with the elderly apostle on his left creates a contrast of age and character that animates the sequence. This alternation of bearded and beardless figures, which continues across the panel, gives the predella a rhythmic variety that avoids monotony and suggests the diversity of the apostolic college.

The second compartment thus presents an apostle who, with his raised hand and his book, embodies the mission of preaching. His youthful beauty and his luminous colours give the figure an attractive presence, and his gesture introduces a note of movement into the solemn sequence of the predella.

#### Third Compartment: Saint Bartholomew (?)

The third apostle, at the centre of the fragment, is a man in the prime of life, with thick, curly dark hair and a full beard. He is shown frontally, looking directly at the viewer with a grave and intense expression. His strong features, with a straight nose and deep-set eyes, give him an air of authority that makes him the visual focus of the fragment.

In his right hand he holds a large knife with a broad blade, raised along his side, and in his left a book bound in red. The knife is the instrument of the martyrdom of Saint Bartholomew, who according to tradition was flayed alive in Armenia, and it is his characteristic attribute. The combination of the knife and the book identifies the apostle as Bartholomew with a high degree of probability, since the knife was rarely used as an attribute for other apostles.

Bartholomew wears a tunic of cream decorated with a pattern of small dark motifs and gold, and over it a mantle of lilac and violet, which falls over his shoulders and is drawn across his body. The patterned tunic, rendered with gold and small coloured motifs, is the most richly decorated garment in the panel, and it demonstrates Allegretto's love of precious textiles, which would become increasingly prominent in his later works.

The frontal pose and direct gaze of Bartholomew distinguish him from the other apostles, who are turned toward the right. This frontality gives him a central, almost iconic role within the fragment. Whether this reflects his position in the complete predella or simply the painter's desire for variety cannot be determined without the rest of the sequence.

The halo is finely tooled, and the round arch above frames the head. The figure fills the compartment from the base of the arcade to the arch, and his imposing presence, with the raised knife and the book, gives the centre of the fragment a strong emphasis.

Allegretto represented Bartholomew in several of his works, including the Berlin diptych and the Fabriano pentaptych, where the saint also holds a knife and a book. The comparison with these works confirms the identification and shows the consistency of Allegretto's iconography. The type of the saint, with the dark curly hair and the full beard, is also similar in these works.

The treatment of the figure shows the qualities of Allegretto's mature style: the solid construction and the strong characterisation of the face, derived from his Florentine training, and the decorative richness of the tunic, reflecting his growing interest in ornament. The contrast between the grave face and the delicate pattern of the tunic is characteristic of his art in the 1360s.

The third compartment thus presents the most clearly identifiable apostle of the fragment after Saint Peter. With his knife, his book and his richly decorated tunic, Bartholomew embodies both the apostle's mission and the martyr's witness, and his central, frontal position gives him a particular prominence in the fragment.

#### Fourth Compartment: A Youthful Apostle with a Book

The fourth apostle is represented, like the second, as a beardless youth, with short, straight auburn hair, turned in three-quarter view toward the right. His face is gentle and serene, with a straight nose and a small mouth, and his gaze is directed toward the right, toward Saint Peter and the centre of the original predella.

He wears a tunic of intense blue, edged with gold at the neck, and over it a mantle of red and coral, which falls over his shoulders and is drawn across his body. The contrast between the blue tunic and the red mantle creates a vivid chromatic accent. The combination of colours is an inversion of that of the first apostle, whose blue tunic was covered by an ochre mantle.

In his hands the apostle holds a large book bound in dark green or black with metal fittings, held open or closed before his body. The book is his only attribute, and, as with the second apostle, it does not permit a secure identification. His youthful type could suggest Saint John the Evangelist, but, since the second apostle is also youthful, one of the two may be another apostle, such as Philip, Thomas or James the Less.

The figure's pose, with the body turned toward the right and the book held before him, is calm and contemplative, and it contrasts with the animated gesture of the second apostle. This variation of poses and gestures across the sequence gives the predella a rhythmic diversity.

The halo is finely tooled, and the round arch above frames the head. The figure fills the compartment, and the book, with its dark binding, creates a strong accent at the lower right, balancing the red of the mantle.

The gentle modelling of the face and the harmony of the colours show the refinement of Allegretto's mature style. The figure's calm expression and its orientation toward the right reinforce the movement of the sequence toward the centre of the original predella, where the principal image would have been placed.

The juxtaposition of this youthful apostle with Saint Peter on his right creates a contrast between youth and age, between the unidentified apostle and the prince of the apostles. This contrast mirrors that between the first and second apostles on the left side of the fragment, and it gives the whole sequence a balanced rhythm.

The fourth compartment thus presents an apostle of gentle and contemplative character, whose identity cannot be determined but whose presence contributes to the harmony of the predella. His luminous colours and calm pose prepare the viewer for the imposing figure of Saint Peter, who closes the sequence on the right.

#### Fifth Compartment: Saint Peter

The fifth apostle, at the right end of the fragment, is Saint Peter, the prince of the apostles and the first bishop of Rome. He is represented according to his traditional type: an elderly man with short grey hair, tonsured, and a short, curly grey beard. He is shown frontally, looking directly at the viewer with a grave and dignified expression. His strong, square features give him an air of authority appropriate to his role as the head of the apostolic college.

In his right hand Peter holds a large golden key, rendered with an elaborately decorated bit, and in his left a book bound in dark green. The key is his characteristic attribute, recalling Christ's words: "I will give unto thee the keys of the kingdom of heaven" (Matthew 16:19). It symbolised the authority of Peter and his successors, the popes, to bind and loose. The book, as with the other apostles, alludes to the Gospel.

Peter wears a blue tunic, visible at the neck, and over it a mantle of orange, which falls over his shoulders and is drawn across his body. Over the mantle, around his shoulders and falling down his front, he wears a white band marked with black crosses, which resembles the pallium, the liturgical vestment of the pope and of archbishops. This detail presents Peter not only as an apostle but as the first pope, the head of the Church. It gives his figure a particular dignity within the sequence of apostles.

The frontal pose and direct gaze of Peter, like those of Bartholomew at the centre, distinguish him from the apostles turned toward the right. His position at the right end of the fragment, adjacent to the probable centre of the original predella, would have placed him in the place of honour, immediately beside the principal image. This position corresponds to his rank as the prince of the apostles.

The halo is finely tooled, and the round arch above frames the head. The figure fills the compartment, and the golden key, with its elaborate bit, creates a strong accent at the left of the compartment, balancing the dark book on the right.

The treatment of the figure shows the qualities of Allegretto's mature style: the strong characterisation of the face, the solid construction of the figure and the rich decoration of the garments. The white band with its black crosses and the elaborate key show his attention to decorative detail, which would become increasingly prominent in his later works.

The identification of Saint Peter is secure, and it provides a fixed point for the reconstruction of the original predella. If Peter stood immediately beside the centre, as his position at the right end of the fragment suggests, Saint Paul would probably have stood on the other side of the centre. The remaining apostles would then have continued the sequence to the right, facing toward the left.

The fifth compartment thus closes the fragment with the most important apostle, the prince of the apostles and the first pope. His imposing presence, with the key, the book and the pallium, gives the fragment a strong conclusion. It also suggests the grandeur of the original altarpiece, whose predella, with its twelve apostles, formed the foundation of a large and ambitious composition.

### Allegretto Nuzi and workshop, Madonna and Child (left wing of a diptych with the Man of Sorrows), c. 1365

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-14.jpg" alt="Small panel by Allegretto Nuzi on gold ground with a punched floral border: the Virgin in half-length, in a white veil and a dark mantle with a gold border and a large radiant gold star on the shoulder, over a red gown, looks out at the viewer with her right hand raised before her breast; on her left arm she holds the Christ Child in a pale tunic with a dark sash, who reaches toward her neckline and turns his head toward the viewer">
  <figcaption>Allegretto Nuzi and workshop, Madonna and Child, c. 1365, tempera and gold on panel, 34.6 × 19.9 cm, Vatican City, Pinacoteca Vaticana, inv. MV.40208.0.0.</figcaption>
</figure>

This small panel, just over a third of a metre high, is one wing of a devotional diptych whose other wing, also in the Pinacoteca Vaticana, represents the dead Christ as the Man of Sorrows. The Vatican Museums catalogue the panel as the work of Allegretto Nuzi and his workshop, a formulation that recognises the participation of assistants in its execution.

The attribution is characteristic of the small devotional diptychs produced in Allegretto's workshop, which were made in considerable numbers for private patrons and in which the master's designs were often carried out wholly or partly by his collaborators. The museums date the diptych about 1365 and see in the pairing of the two wings a testimony to the particular devotional attention given both to the Incarnation and to the sufferings Christ endured until his death. The date places the work in the same years as the great triptych of 1365 in the same collection, at the height of Allegretto's maturity. The combination of the Virgin and Child with the Vir dolorum recurs in the Philadelphia diptych and in the diptych divided between a private collection and Pesaro, and it was evidently one of the formulas most in demand in Allegretto's workshop.

The Virgin is shown in half-length, filling almost the entire height of the panel, so that the image offered its owner the intimacy of a close encounter with the Mother of God. She wears a white veil that frames her face and falls over her shoulders, and above it a mantle of deep blue, now darkened almost to black, edged with a band of gold embroidered with dots. On her right shoulder shines a large gold star with radiating rays, the emblem of Mary as *Stella Maris*, the star of the sea that guides the faithful. Beneath the mantle she wears a gown of vermilion, whose neckline is decorated with a band of gold embroidery with scrolling motifs. The intense red of the gown, framed by the dark mantle, is the chromatic focus of the image.

The Virgin's face is long and oval, with a straight, narrow nose, almond-shaped eyes and a small, closed mouth, and it is modelled with soft gradations of tone over a greenish underlayer. She inclines her head slightly and looks out toward the viewer with a grave, melancholy gaze. This detachment from the Child, a constant of Allegretto's Madonnas, acquires a precise meaning in a diptych whose other wing shows the dead Christ. The Mother appears already to contemplate the Passion displayed on the opposite panel, so that her silent sorrow becomes the link between the two images.

The Virgin raises her right hand before her breast, with her long, slender fingers gently extended, while with her left hand she supports the Child. The gesture of the raised hand, which appears in almost identical form in the Philadelphia diptych, has an ambivalent meaning: it both presents the Child to the viewer and seems to express the Mother's inward emotion. In the context of the diptych, it may also be read as directing the viewer's attention across to the opposite wing, where the Son whom she presents appears as the Man of Sorrows.

The Christ Child sits on his Mother's left arm, dressed in a pale tunic tied at the waist with a dark sash, which leaves his feet bare. He turns his body toward her and reaches with one hand toward the embroidered neckline of her gown, a spontaneous gesture of affection that echoes the tender motifs of Sienese painting. At the same time he turns his head toward the viewer, with a grave, attentive expression that seems older than his years. The combination of the childlike gesture and the solemn gaze expresses the double nature of the Child, at once a human infant and the incarnate Son of God.

The gold ground behind the figures is framed by a broad punched border decorated with floral motifs, and the haloes of the Virgin and Child are richly tooled with rings of ornament. In the small format of the diptych, this dense punchwork creates an effect of preciousness appropriate to an object intended for intimate and prolonged contemplation. The same technique frames the gold ground of the Philadelphia diptych, and it testifies to Allegretto's mastery of gold tooling, which modern scholarship has recognised as one of the hallmarks of his art.

The companion wing presents the Man of Sorrows, the dead Christ shown in half-length, standing, stripped of his garments, with his hands bound, his eyes closed and his face inclined in the sleep of death. The pairing of the two wings set side by side the Incarnation and the Passion: the infant Christ in his Mother's arms and the dead Christ displaying his suffering. When the diptych was opened, the owner was invited to meditate on the continuity between the two, sharing the sorrow of the Mother and contemplating the wounds of the Son, in the practice of affective devotion promoted by the mendicant orders.

Despite the wear of its surface and the craquelure that marks the paint and the gold, the panel preserves the essential qualities of Allegretto's mature devotional painting: the monumental construction of the Virgin, the harmony of the dark blue, red and white, and the refinement of the punched gold. The close correspondence with the Madonna of the Philadelphia diptych, in the pose of the Virgin, the raised hand and the star on the shoulder, shows how a successful composition devised by the master was repeated for different patrons. It is precisely this practice of repetition that explains the Vatican's attribution to Allegretto and his workshop. The design and the general conception belong to the master, while the execution may reflect the hand of assistants, perhaps in the somewhat simplified modelling of the drapery and the more summary treatment of certain details. The panel thus documents not only Allegretto's devotional art but also the organisation of his workshop, which allowed the painter to meet the demand for small images of private devotion that his reputation had created in Fabriano and the Marche.

### Allegretto Nuzi and workshop, Man of Sorrows (right wing of a diptych with the Madonna and Child), c. 1365

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-16.jpg" alt="Small panel by Allegretto Nuzi and workshop on gold ground with a punched floral border: the dead Christ in half-length, naked to below the waist, with head bowed, eyes closed and long dark hair falling onto his shoulders; his arms are crossed over his breast, and red streams of blood flow from the wounds in his hands and side; a punched halo surrounds his head">
  <figcaption>Allegretto Nuzi and workshop, Man of Sorrows (Vir dolorum), c. 1365, tempera and gold on panel, 34.8 × 20.3 cm, Vatican City, Pinacoteca Vaticana, inv. MV.40210.0.0.</figcaption>
</figure>

This small panel, companion to the Madonna and Child in the same collection, formed the right wing of a devotional diptych catalogued by the Vatican Museums as the work of Allegretto Nuzi and his workshop and dated about 1365. The two wings are almost identical in size and share the same gold ground framed by a punched floral border, which confirms that they were conceived together. The Vatican Museums, presenting this panel, observe that in the image of the *Vir dolorum* the sorrow of Christ's Passion and death appears almost crystallised, and they see in the diptych as a whole a testimony to the particular devotional attention given to the Incarnation and to the sufferings Christ endured until his death.

The Man of Sorrows, or *Imago Pietatis*, was not an illustration of a moment in the Gospel narrative but a timeless devotional image, which condensed the Passion, death and Resurrection into a single figure. Originating in Byzantine art, the type spread through Italy from the late thirteenth century and became one of the most widespread subjects of private devotion in the fourteenth. It was frequently paired with the Virgin and Child in small diptychs, as here and in the Philadelphia diptych, where the same pairing appears in a composition closely related to the Vatican work.

Christ is shown frontally, in half-length, his body cut just below the navel by the lower edge of the panel. Unlike the Philadelphia version, in which he rises from a sarcophagus, here no tomb is visible, and the figure emerges directly from the lower edge of the panel against the gold ground. The effect is one of immediate presence: the dead Christ appears before the viewer at close range, as if offered for contemplation in the intimacy of private prayer. The absence of any narrative setting concentrates the whole meaning of the image on the body of Christ and its wounds.

His head is bowed onto his right shoulder, his eyes are closed and his long, wavy brown hair falls over his shoulders and breast. His face, with its lowered lids, straight nose, parted lips and short beard, expresses the stillness of death, and a few drops of blood run from his brow, recalling the crown of thorns. At the same time the upright figure possesses a mysterious life, for the dead Christ stands before the viewer as if still present. This ambiguity between death and life lies at the heart of the *Imago Pietatis*, which presents Christ as dead and yet living, suffering and yet victorious over death.

Christ's arms are crossed over his breast, with his hands laid on the opposite shoulders. This pose, derived from Byzantine models, emphasises the stillness of death and at the same time displays the wounds. Blood flows in thin red streams from the wounds in his hands down the forearms, and the wound in his side, partly covered by the crossed arms, also bleeds in vertical streams. Allegretto renders the wounds with restraint, as fine red lines against the pale, greenish flesh. The effect is one of quiet pathos, which invites the viewer to meditate on the suffering of the Passion rather than to recoil from it.

The modelling of the body, with the muscles of the chest and arms and the navel indicated by subtle gradations of tone, is soft and restrained. The flesh has a pronounced greenish cast, which results in part from the green underpainting, or *verdaccio*, of Trecento tempera technique, now more visible as the upper layers have thinned. It also expresses, however, the pallor of death. The treatment of the body is somewhat simpler than in the Philadelphia version, a difference that may reflect the participation of the workshop recognised by the Vatican catalogue.

Behind Christ's head is a cruciform halo, richly tooled with punched ornament, whose inscribed cross, reserved for Christ alone, identifies him as the Son of God even in death. The gold ground is framed by a punched border of floral motifs identical to that of the companion wing. When the diptych was opened, the two panels formed a unified ensemble. On the left, the Virgin in her dark mantle, with the star on her shoulder, presented the infant Christ, gazing out with a melancholy expression. On the right, the same Christ appeared dead, displaying the wounds of the Passion. The Virgin's sorrowful gaze and her raised hand directed the viewer's attention across the diptych to the image of her suffering Son.

The Vatican diptych thus offered its owner a concentrated meditation on the mystery of salvation, setting side by side the Incarnation and the Passion, the Mother holding her Child and the dead Christ displaying his wounds. In the practice of affective devotion promoted by the mendicant orders, such images served as supports for prayer, in which the faithful were encouraged to share the grief of the Mother and to contemplate the wounds of the Son. Although the execution may reflect the participation of assistants, the conception of the diptych belongs to Allegretto. It shows how his workshop met the demand for small devotional images, repeating and varying compositions devised by the master for patrons in Fabriano and the Marche.

### School of Allegretto Nuzi, Madonna and Child Enthroned with Two Angels between Saints Catherine of Alexandria and John the Baptist, c. 1380–90

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-15.jpg" alt="Small Gothic panel on gold ground with a painted foliate border: the Virgin in a dark blue mantle and red gown enthroned under a pointed canopy with a starry blue vault, holding the Christ Child in a pink tunic; two angels in blue above the throne; on the left Saint Catherine, crowned, in a dark mantle, holding a palm and a large wheel; on the right Saint John the Baptist in a camel skin and pink mantle, barefoot, pointing and holding an inscribed scroll">
  <figcaption>School of Allegretto Nuzi, Madonna and Child Enthroned with Two Angels between Saints Catherine of Alexandria and John the Baptist, c. 1380–90, tempera and gold on panel, 36.4 × 28.7 cm, Vatican City, Pinacoteca Vaticana, inv. MV.40209.0.0.</figcaption>
</figure>

This small devotional panel, catalogued by the Vatican Museums as an *altarolo*, a portable altarpiece for private prayer, is attributed not to Allegretto himself but to his school, and dated 1380–90. The date places it in the decade or more after the painter's death in 1373, when his models continued to be repeated by the painters of the Fabrianese school formed in his workshop. The panel is therefore a document of Allegretto's afterlife: it shows how his compositions, his figure types and his decorative vocabulary survived him and continued to satisfy the demand for devotional images in Fabriano and the Marche in the last decades of the century.

The composition is centred on the Virgin, enthroned beneath an elaborate Gothic canopy. The throne rises in the form of a miniature architectural structure, with a pointed gable, pinnacles and twisted colonnettes, and its vault, visible above the Virgin's head, is painted deep blue and spangled with gold stars, an image of the heavenly firmament. This type of throne closely recalls that of the central panel of the Detroit tabernacle, painted by Allegretto himself, where the Virgin also sits beneath a Gothic canopy with a starry vault. The similarity suggests that the painter of the Vatican panel drew directly on compositions devised by the master, which circulated in his workshop and among his followers.

The Virgin wears a white veil and a mantle of deep blue, now darkened, over a gown of vermilion. She sits frontally, with her head slightly inclined, and looks out toward the viewer with a gentle, grave expression. Her face, with its long nose, narrow eyes and small mouth, follows the type of Allegretto's Madonnas, although the modelling is simpler and less refined than in the master's autograph works. The Child sits on her knee, dressed in a pink tunic, holding a small object before his breast, perhaps a bird, and turning toward the viewer. The Virgin supports him with both hands, in a pose that echoes the enthroned Madonnas of Allegretto's small altarpieces.

Above the throne, on either side of its pinnacled gable, appear two angels in half-length, dressed in blue and with dark wings, their haloes finely tooled. Each appears to hold a small open book, perhaps a book of chant, as if singing the praises of the Virgin. Their symmetrical placement frames the throne and extends the heavenly court into the upper part of the panel, in a manner that recalls the angels behind the throne in the Detroit tabernacle.
