On the left stands Saint Catherine of Alexandria, crowned as a royal princess, in a pale pink gown and a dark blue mantle. In one hand she holds the palm of martyrdom, and beside her stands the great spiked wheel on which she was to be broken, rendered with its spokes and felloe in brown. The large circular form of the wheel fills the lower left corner of the panel and balances the vertical figure of the saint. Catherine's gentle, pale face and her elegant, slightly swaying pose follow the type of the virgin martyrs in Allegretto's altarpieces, such as the Saint Catherine of the Apiro polyptych.

On the right stands Saint John the Baptist, bearded and with long, dishevelled hair, wearing his camel-skin garment beneath a rose-pink mantle, with his legs and feet bare. With his right hand he points toward the Virgin and Child, and in his left he holds an unrolled scroll inscribed with lines of text. Traditionally, this inscription reads *Ecce Agnus Dei*, "Behold the Lamb of God," the words with which John announced the coming of Christ. The pairing of the Baptist with a crowned female saint on either side of the enthroned Virgin repeats the arrangement of the central panel of the Detroit tabernacle, in which the Baptist and a female martyr also flank the throne.

The gold ground is framed by a painted border of dark foliate scrolls, which runs around the edges of the panel, and the haloes of all the figures are richly tooled. The decorative repertory, with the starry vault of the canopy, the tooled haloes and the foliate border, belongs to the tradition established by Allegretto. The execution, however, is somewhat drier and more schematic than in his autograph works: the draperies fall in simpler folds, and the figures have a certain stiffness that distinguishes them from the graceful forms of the master. A small area at the upper left of the photograph is obscured, and the panel shows some wear to the gold and the paint.

The Vatican panel thus illustrates the persistence of Allegretto's art after his death. The composition, derived from models such as the Detroit tabernacle, the figure types of the Virgin and the saints, and the decorative vocabulary of gold and colour were transmitted through his workshop to the painters of the following generation. Francescuccio Ghissi, active until 1395, is the best known of them, but many others remain anonymous. Works such as this show that the Fabrianese school founded by Allegretto remained a living tradition into the last decades of the fourteenth century, preparing the ground from which Gentile da Fabriano would emerge.

### The Crucifixion with the Virgin, Saint John the Evangelist and Mary Magdalene, c. 1365

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-26.jpg" alt="Small rectangular panel by Allegretto Nuzi on a worn gold ground showing red underlayer: Christ dead on the Cross above a dark mound; Mary Magdalene in orange-red kneels, embraces the Cross and presses her face to his feet; on the left the Virgin in a dark mantle with a gold star looks up with her hand at her throat; on the right Saint John in a pink mantle over a dark green tunic stands with hands clasped and head bowed">
  <figcaption>Crucifixion with the Virgin Mary, Saint John the Evangelist and Saint Mary Magdalene, c. 1365, tempera and gold on panel, painted surface 23.3 × 15.7 cm, frame 34.9 × 27.3 × 7.3 cm, Birmingham (Alabama), Birmingham Museum of Art, Gift of the Samuel H. Kress Foundation, inv. 1961.113.</figcaption>
</figure>

This small panel, whose painted surface measures little more than twenty centimetres in height, is the most intimate of the Crucifixions attributed to Allegretto. It came to the Birmingham Museum of Art in 1961 as a gift of the Samuel H. Kress Foundation, which distributed its vast collection of Italian paintings among American museums in the middle of the twentieth century. The museum dates it to about 1365, in the same years as the Vatican triptych and the Vatican and Philadelphia diptychs. According to the museum, the panel probably formed the right wing of a diptych, whose left wing would most likely have represented the Madonna and Child. Its diminutive size suggests that the diptych was portable, intended for use in a domestic or private devotional setting. The pairing of the Crucifixion with the Madonna and Child, the Passion with the Incarnation, is the same as in Allegretto's diptychs in Berlin, Philadelphia and the Vatican, and it confirms how central this devotional formula was to the production of his workshop.

Compared with Allegretto's other Crucifixions, the Birmingham panel is remarkable for its concentration. There are no angels gathering the blood of Christ, as in the Metropolitan panel and the Richmond Crucifixion, no pelican in the gable, as in the Berlin diptych, and no holy women supporting the fainting Virgin. The composition is reduced to its essential elements: the Cross, the dead Christ and the three mourners who, according to tradition, stood closest to him at the moment of his death. This reduction gives the image a severe and silent intensity, appropriate to an object intended for personal meditation.

The Cross rises from a dark mound, the hill of Golgotha, to the upper edge of the panel, and its crossbar extends almost to the edges of the frame. Christ hangs dead upon it, his arms stretched slightly upward along the crossbar, his head bowed onto his right shoulder and his eyes closed. His slender body, modelled in pale, greenish flesh tones with delicate indications of the ribs and the muscles of the chest and abdomen, is girded by a transparent white loincloth that clings to his thighs. His legs are bent, and his crossed feet are nailed to a small support at the base of the upright. The figure belongs to the Tuscan type of the *Christus patiens*, and its elegant proportions and restrained modelling reflect Allegretto's Florentine formation.

At the foot of the Cross, Mary Magdalene kneels in a mantle of orange-red, which spreads across the ground in broad, heavy folds. She embraces the upright of the Cross with both arms and presses her face against Christ's nailed feet, in an attitude of complete abandonment to grief and love. Her long golden hair falls down her back beneath her mantle. The motif of the Magdalene embracing the Cross and kissing Christ's feet, which recurs in almost all of Allegretto's Crucifixions, offered the viewer a model of penitence and affective devotion. Here the Magdalene's face, almost hidden against the wood, conveys an especially intense intimacy.

On the left stands the Virgin, wrapped from head to foot in a mantle of deep blue, now darkened almost to black, marked on the head with a small gold star, the emblem of Mary as *Stella Maris*. She raises her face toward her dead Son with an expression of anguish, and brings one hand to her throat in a gesture of grief that seems to stifle a cry. The Virgin's figure, a tall, dark column rising from the ground to the level of Christ's knees, has a monumental simplicity that gives it great expressive power. The restrained gesture and the upturned face convey her sorrow with more intensity than the fainting Virgin of Allegretto's larger compositions.

On the right stands Saint John the Evangelist, youthful and beardless, in a dark green tunic and a rose-pink mantle that falls from his shoulder in broad folds to the ground. He clasps his hands at his waist and inclines his head to one side, looking down with an expression of quiet sorrow. His pose, turned inward and absorbed in grief, contrasts with the Virgin's upward gaze, and the two standing figures frame the Cross in a balanced composition. The pink and green of John's garments, a colour harmony favoured by Florentine painters, balance the dark blue of the Virgin's mantle and the orange-red of the Magdalene's.

The gold ground of the panel has suffered severe abrasion, and over large areas the red bole beneath the gold is now exposed, giving the background its present reddish, mottled appearance. As with the Metropolitan Crucifixion, the original effect would have been one of uniform, luminous gold. The punched border that runs along the edges of the panel and the haloes of the Virgin and Saint John, tooled with rings of circles, survive better and still show the refinement of the original decoration. The figures themselves are relatively well preserved, and their colours retain much of their original intensity.

The Birmingham Crucifixion thus represents the most concentrated version of the devotional formula that Allegretto developed for the Passion. Stripped of the symbolic and narrative elements that enrich his other Crucifixions, it presents the essential drama of Christ's death and the grief of those who loved him most, in a small format designed for close, personal contemplation. The graceful figures, the soft modelling, the expressive gestures and the harmonious colours show the qualities of Allegretto's mature art. The panel demonstrates his ability to convey profound emotion with the simplest means.

### Allegretto Nuzi and Francescuccio Ghissi, Saints Catherine of Alexandria and Bartholomew

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-27.jpg" alt="Arched gold-ground panel with two standing saints on green ground, named on black tablets above: on the left Saint Catherine, crowned, in a pale pink gown with small patterns, holding a red book and resting her hand on a large spiked wheel; on the right Saint Bartholomew, bearded, in a light blue tunic and a white mantle with rosettes and red lining, holding a black book and a large knife; both turn toward the left">
  <figcaption>Allegretto Nuzi and Francescuccio Ghissi, Saints Catherine and Bartholomew, c. 1350, tempera on wood (probably poplar), 83.2 × 51 cm, London, The National Gallery, bequeathed by Herbert Charles Coleman, 1949, inv. NG5930.</figcaption>
</figure>

This panel, representing Saints Catherine of Alexandria and Bartholomew standing side by side, is one of the most instructive works associated with Allegretto Nuzi, because it raises directly the question of how the Fabrianese workshop functioned. The National Gallery attributes it jointly to Allegretto Nuzi and Francescuccio Ghissi and explains the reasoning behind this attribution. The painting has been connected with Allegretto's workshop on the basis of the punch marks in the gold leaf, visible in the haloes and the border, which are also found in other pictures by the artist. The style of painting and the lettering of the inscriptions, however, resemble the works of Francescuccio Ghissi. The gallery therefore suggests that Allegretto may have designed the picture and that Ghissi painted it, using his master's tools. The panel is thus an exemplary case of the use of punchwork analysis to associate an unsigned work with a particular workshop.

According to the National Gallery, the panel probably formed the right-hand side of a triptych. Both saints turn toward the left, that is, toward the centre of the lost altarpiece, where the principal image would have stood. The gallery, following [Dillian Gordon](/scholars.html#gordon-dillian-rosalind)'s 2011 catalogue, notes that a roundel with Christ blessing, now in the Museum of Art of Bowdoin College in Brunswick, Maine, has been connected with this panel on the basis of its condition, and may therefore have crowned the lost central panel. This connection has been challenged by a more recent proposal.

In 2022, [Emanuele Zappasodi](/scholars.html#zappasodi-emanuele) assigned the Bowdoin roundel to a small polyptych centred on the Crucifixion now in the church of the Ursuline nuns in Fribourg, together with two panels of Saint Agnes and a female martyr. If Zappasodi is right, the roundel did not belong to the altarpiece of the London panel, unless the London panel was itself one of the further compartments that his reconstruction requires, a hypothesis that remains to be tested. The gallery also observes that Catherine and Bartholomew both had churches dedicated to them in Allegretto's hometown of Fabriano, where he collaborated on several works with Ghissi after his return from Florence, and that the panel was therefore probably made there.

Above the heads of the saints, two black tablets bear their names in Gothic capitals: ·CHATERINA· and BARTOLOMEU·. The spelling Chaterina, with ch in place of the Latin c, and the vernacular form Bartolomeu reflect the fluid orthography of the fourteenth century and the mingling of Latin and Italian forms in the inscriptions of the period. According to the National Gallery, the lettering of these inscriptions is one of the elements that point to the hand of Ghissi rather than that of Allegretto. The inscribed tablets, set against the gold ground, give the panel a clarity of identification suited to an altarpiece viewed from a distance.

On the left stands Saint Catherine of Alexandria, crowned as a royal princess. Her gold crown is set with red stones and flanked by fleurons, and her long golden hair falls over her shoulders. She inclines her head gently and looks down toward the left, with a delicate, pensive expression. She wears a long gown of pale pink, patterned with small motifs in brown and gold and edged with embroidered borders at the neck, the sleeves and the hem. In her left hand she holds a book bound in red, the emblem of her learning, since according to her legend she confuted the pagan philosophers summoned by the emperor. Her right hand rests on the rim of the great wheel studded with metal spikes, the instrument on which she was to be tortured for her faith, which stands at the left edge of the panel.

On the right stands Saint Bartholomew, a man in the prime of life with thick dark hair and a full beard, turning his head toward the left with a grave expression. He wears a tunic of light blue and over it a white mantle patterned with rosettes and crosses in gold, lined with brilliant red, which falls from his shoulder in broad, heavy folds. In his right hand he holds a large book bound in black with metal clasps, the attribute of the apostles, and in his left a large knife, the symbol of his martyrdom, since he was flayed alive. The contrast between the pale blue of the tunic, the white of the mantle and the red of its lining gives the figure a luminous and refined presence.

The pairing of Catherine and Bartholomew recurs in Allegretto's work: in the Berlin diptych, the enthroned Virgin is flanked by exactly the same two saints, Bartholomew on the left and Catherine on the right. This repetition suggests a particular devotion to the two saints in Fabriano, where, as the National Gallery notes, both had churches dedicated to them, and it strengthens the connection of the London panel with Allegretto's workshop. The type of Bartholomew, with his dark beard and his knife, is also similar to the figures of the saint in the Berlin diptych, the Fabriano pentaptych and the Strasbourg predella.

The panel displays the qualities that characterise the collaboration between Allegretto and Ghissi. The figures are tall and elegant, with graceful proportions and gentle expressions, and the draperies fall in broad, rhythmic folds. The patterned textiles of the gown and the mantle, rendered with small repeated motifs, reflect the love of precious fabrics that runs through the Fabrianese school. The softness of the modelling, the delicacy of Catherine's face and the somewhat linear treatment of the folds are consistent with Ghissi's manner, which, while closely following his master's models, tends toward a gentler and more decorative style. The punched border that runs around the arched top of the panel and the richly tooled haloes testify to the shared workshop tools.

The London panel thus documents, with unusual clarity, the collaborative practice of Allegretto's workshop. The master's design and tools, identified through the punch marks, combine with the hand of his closest collaborator, identified through style and lettering, to produce a work that belongs to both. The panel was purchased in 1920 from or through Alphonse Kann in Paris by Herbert Charles Coleman, who bequeathed it to the National Gallery in 1949. It is fully catalogued in Dillian Gordon's volume on the gallery's Italian paintings before 1400 (2011). It demonstrates how modern technical study, alongside stylistic analysis, has refined our understanding of the relationship between Allegretto and Ghissi, one of the central problems of Fabrianese painting.

### Christ Blessing (roundel from the pinnacle of a dismembered altarpiece)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-28.jpg" alt="Small round panel by Allegretto Nuzi on gold ground, set in a square gilded surround on red velvet: Christ in half-length facing the viewer, with long light-brown hair, a short beard and a punched cross halo, wearing a blue-green tunic and a red mantle, raising his right hand in blessing and holding a closed green book in his left">
  <figcaption>Christ Blessing, c. 1360, tempera and gold on panel, 18.7 × 18.1 cm, Brunswick (Maine), Bowdoin College Museum of Art, Gift of the Samuel H. Kress Foundation, inv. 1961.100.4.</figcaption>
</figure>

This small roundel, less than twenty centimetres in diameter, originally crowned the central pinnacle of an altarpiece. Images of Christ blessing, God the Father or the Redeemer set in roundels or trefoils at the summit of the central gable were a common feature of fourteenth-century polyptychs. They completed the programme of the altarpiece by placing the divine authority above the Virgin and saints of the main register. Like many such elements, the roundel was separated from its altarpiece at an unknown date and entered the art market as an independent image. It came to the Bowdoin College Museum of Art in 1961 as a gift of the Samuel H. Kress Foundation, which distributed its collection of Italian paintings among American museums in the middle of the twentieth century.

The identification of the altarpiece from which the roundel came has been the subject of two different proposals. The National Gallery in London, following Dillian Gordon's catalogue of its Italian paintings before 1400 (2011), connects it on the basis of its condition with the panel of Saints Catherine and Bartholomew attributed to Allegretto Nuzi and Francescuccio Ghissi. That panel probably formed the right-hand side of a triptych, whose lost central panel the roundel would have crowned. In 2022, on the occasion of the exhibition in Fabriano of the recently restored Crucifixion from the church of the Ursuline nuns in Fribourg, Emanuele Zappasodi proposed a different reconstruction.

He associated the Crucifixion with two panels of Saint Agnes and a female martyr recovered by the Carabinieri's cultural heritage unit. In his view, the three panels came from a polyptych of modest dimensions, with the Crucifixion at the centre, surmounted in its pinnacle by the Bowdoin roundel, and with at least one further compartment beside the two saints. As published, the two proposals are incompatible. They could be reconciled only if the London panel proved to be one of the missing compartments of the Fribourg polyptych, a possibility that neither source raises and that only a comparison of the dimensions, punchwork and condition of the panels could test. The original context of the roundel therefore remains an open question.

Christ is shown in half-length, facing the viewer frontally, his figure filling the roundel from the halo, which touches its upper edge, to the lower edge, where his body is cut at the waist. His long, light-brown hair falls in soft waves to his shoulders, parted in the middle, and he has a short beard and moustache. His face, with its straight nose, wide-set eyes and small mouth, gazes directly out at the viewer with a calm and solemn expression. The frontal presentation and the direct gaze are appropriate to an image placed at the summit of an altarpiece, from which Christ would have looked down upon the faithful as the Judge and Saviour of the world.

Christ raises his right hand in blessing, with the index and middle fingers extended and the others folded against the palm, in the traditional gesture of the Latin benediction. In his left hand he holds a closed book with a green binding, which he rests against his body. The book signifies Christ as the Word of God and the source of the Gospel, and the blessing expresses his role as the giver of grace. Together, the gesture and the book define the image as that of Christ the Redeemer, or Salvator Mundi, the Saviour of the world who blesses and teaches.

He wears a tunic of blue-green, edged at the neck with a band of gold decorated with small dots and with gold lines at the cuff, and over it a mantle of red, which falls from his left shoulder and is drawn across his body. The combination of the blue-green tunic and the red mantle, one of the traditional colour schemes for Christ, symbolises his two natures, divine and human. The broad, simple folds of the mantle are modelled with soft gradations of tone, and they give the small figure a monumental presence despite its reduced size.

Behind Christ's head is a halo richly tooled with punched ornament, in which the arms of the cross, reserved for Christ, can be made out through the patterns of punched motifs. The gold ground of the roundel shows a network of craquelure, and the modelling of the flesh reveals in places the greenish underlayer of the tempera technique. The roundel is set within a square gilded surround whose spandrels are decorated with punched motifs, and the whole is mounted on red velvet in a later frame. Whether the square surround is part of the original carpentry or a later adaptation for independent display would need to be established by technical examination.

In its style, the roundel shows the qualities of Allegretto's mature art. The frontal construction of the figure, the solid volumes of the body beneath the drapery and the clarity of the composition reflect the painter's Florentine training. The gentle modelling of the face and the harmony of the colours recall the Sienese component of his art. The type of Christ, with the long parted hair, the short beard and the calm gaze, corresponds to the figures of Christ in Allegretto's other works, such as the blessing Christ in the gable of the Bern triptych.

The Bowdoin roundel thus provides a striking example of the dismemberment of fourteenth-century altarpieces, whose elements were dispersed among collections in Europe and America and which modern scholarship has sought to reconstruct. The two competing proposals for its original context, the triptych to which the London Saints Catherine and Bartholomew belonged and the Fribourg Crucifixion polyptych, show both the possibilities and the limits of such reconstructions. They also illustrate the importance of physical evidence, such as the condition of the panels, their dimensions and their punched decoration, alongside stylistic analysis. Whatever its original context, the roundel preserves, in its small format, an image of Christ of great dignity and serenity. In its original position, it would have crowned an altarpiece of Allegretto's workshop.

### The Fribourg Polyptych: The Crucifixion between Saint Agnes and a Female Martyr (recomposed)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-29.jpg" alt="Three Gothic panels by Allegretto Nuzi on gold ground: in the centre the Crucifixion, with Christ on the Cross, two lamenting angels and a pelican in a nest on a tree at the top, the fainting Virgin in a dark mantle supported by holy women, Mary Magdalene in red kneeling at the foot of the Cross, Saint John in a pink mantle and soldiers with spears and a man in a turban on the right; on the left panel Saint Agnes in a pink-orange gown holding a lamb; on the right panel a female saint in a dark green gown and red mantle">
  <figcaption>The Crucifixion between Saint Agnes and a Female Martyr, tempera and gold on panel. Central panel: Fribourg (Switzerland), Church of the Ursuline Nuns; lateral panels: recovered by the Carabinieri's cultural heritage unit. The three panels were recomposed and exhibited together in Fabriano, Pinacoteca Civica ""Bruno Molajoli"," from 3 September to 16 October 2022..</figcaption>
</figure>

The three panels reunited in Fabriano in 2022 offer a rare opportunity to see a dismembered altarpiece by Allegretto Nuzi in something close to its original form. The central Crucifixion, preserved in the church of the Ursuline nuns in Fribourg, in Switzerland, but of unknown provenance, had long been known to scholarship. The two lateral panels, representing Saint Agnes and an unidentified female martyr, emerged only recently, when the Carabinieri's cultural heritage unit recovered them in a seizure. Later studies attributed them to the Fabrianese painter. They were associated with the Crucifixion by Emanuele Zappasodi, who proposed that the three panels came from a polyptych of modest dimensions. The exhibition Allegretto Nuzi e la Crocifissione di Friburgo. Oro e colore ritrovati thus presented, for the first time, the recomposed central section of this altarpiece.

The recovery of the Crucifixion's original quality was the achievement of the restoration carried out by Lucia Biondi, funded by the Ursuline convent. The restorer first examined the panel in October 2021, during the installation of the monographic exhibition devoted to Allegretto in Fabriano. She was immediately struck by the quality and state of preservation of the painting, even though its pigments were obscured by a thick layer of dark, opaque materials connected with its conservation history. During the restoration, a meticulous cleaning removed the deposits of lamp-black and yellowed varnish. In the restorer's words, it recovered the painting typical of Allegretto, with its exquisite colour harmonies and fine brushstrokes that blend softly into one another.

The central panel, beneath a pointed arch, presents the Crucifixion as a crowded narrative scene, far richer than the devotional Crucifixions of Allegretto's diptychs. Christ hangs dead on the Cross at the centre, his head bowed and his body slender and pale, girded by a white loincloth. Blood drips in regular drops from his hands and spurts from the wound in his side. Above the red titulus inscribed INRI, two angels hover in lament on either side, and at the summit of the Cross, where the upright sprouts into a small leafy tree, a pelican feeds her young with her own blood in her nest. The combination of the pelican and the living Cross, which recurs in the Berlin and Richmond Crucifixions and in the Metropolitan panel, confirms that the Fribourg panel belongs to the same family of works.

On the left, a compact group of mourners surrounds the Virgin. Wrapped in a dark mantle marked with a gold star, she sinks in grief, supported by the holy women in mantles of yellow, red and green, one of whom raises her arms in lament. At the foot of the Cross, Mary Magdalene kneels in a vermilion mantle, embracing the upright and pressing her face against Christ's feet, in the gesture of affective devotion that Allegretto repeats in almost all his Crucifixions. The emotional intensity of this group, with its interlocking gestures and grieving faces, reflects the Sienese component of Allegretto's art, derived from his study of the Lorenzetti.

On the right, the scene opens onto the world of the executioners and witnesses. In the foreground, Saint John the Evangelist, in a rose-pink mantle over a dark green tunic, stands with his head bowed and his hands clasped, absorbed in silent grief. Behind him are gathered the soldiers, with lances and pennants rising against the gold ground, a soldier in armour with a shield and a bearded figure in a striped turban. One figure raises his face toward Christ, in a gesture that may identify him as the centurion who, according to the Gospels, recognised Christ at the moment of his death as the Son of God. The inclusion of the soldiers, absent from Allegretto's smaller devotional Crucifixions, gives the Fribourg panel a narrative breadth that recalls the great Florentine Crucifixions of the mid-fourteenth century.

On the left lateral panel, beneath a pointed arch, stands Saint Agnes, the young Roman virgin martyred under Diocletian. She wears a long gown of pink and orange and a dark mantle, and she holds in her arms her attribute, a small white lamb, whose name in Latin, agnus, evokes her own and which symbolises her purity and her sacrifice. On the right lateral panel stands a female martyr, whose identity has not been established, in a dark green gown and a red mantle, holding a slender palm. Both saints are shown frontally, standing on a narrow strip of ground against the gold, with a calm and solemn dignity that contrasts with the animated drama of the central Crucifixion.

Zappasodi's reconstruction places Saint Agnes on the left and the female martyr on the right of the Crucifixion, with the roundel of Christ blessing now at Bowdoin College in the pinnacle above the central panel. Each of the two saints, in his view, would in turn have been flanked by at least one further compartment. The model, as Zappasodi observes, is the type of polyptych that enjoyed wide success in fourteenth-century Florence. The attribution of the Bowdoin roundel to this ensemble, however, conflicts with the National Gallery's connection of the same roundel with its panel of Saints Catherine and Bartholomew. That conflict remains to be resolved by a comparison of the dimensions, punchwork and condition of all the panels concerned.

The Fribourg polyptych, even in its incomplete state, demonstrates the quality of Allegretto's narrative painting on a moderate scale. The combination of the crowded central Crucifixion with the solemn standing saints in the lateral panels, the refined colour harmonies revealed by the restoration and the delicate modelling of the faces show the painter at his best. It is appropriate that, as the exhibition's presentation of the artist emphasised, Allegretto should be remembered for his delicate, plastic images clothed in golden embroidery, and for his close relationship with the Florentine heirs of Giotto, such as Bernardo Daddi, Maso di Banco and Andrea Orcagna, and above all with Puccio di Simone. The recomposition of the Fribourg polyptych is a significant contribution to the reconstruction of his oeuvre, and a reminder of how much of it may still await rediscovery.

### Madonna and Child Enthroned with Angels and Saints, between Saints Anthony Abbot and Julian (The Macerata Triptych), 1369

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-10.jpg" alt="Gothic triptych on gold ground by Allegretto Nuzi. In the centre the Virgin, crowned and wearing a gold-patterned mantle, is enthroned with the Christ Child, surrounded by tiers of haloed angels and four standing saints, with an inscription on the base of the throne. In the left panel Saint Anthony Abbot, bearded and in a brown hooded habit, holds a tau-shaped staff and raises his right hand. In the right panel the young Saint Julian, in a gold brocade tunic and dark cloak, holds a lance with a red banner. The gables show the Crucified Christ in a quatrefoil between two half-length figures, the Angel Gabriel on the left and the Virgin Annunciate on the right">
  <figcaption>Madonna and Child Enthroned with Angels and Saints, between Saints Anthony Abbot and Julian, tempera and gold on panel, 1369, Macerata, Duomo di San Giuliano.</figcaption>
</figure>

#### Central Panel: The Virgin and Child Enthroned with Angels and Saints

The central panel is the documentary heart of the altarpiece, because the base of the throne carries the inscription that records both the commission and the authorship. As transcribed in the literature, it reads:

> ISTAM TABULAM FECIT FIERI FRATER IOHANNES CLERICUS PRECEPTOR TOLENTINI: ALEGRITTUS DE FABRIANO ME PINXIT [...] MCCCLXVIIII.

The painting thus belongs to the small and precious group of Nuzi's signed and dated works, which provide the scaffolding of his chronology. In the sequence of dated works it follows the Vatican triptych of 1365 and the Apiro polyptych and San Severino Madonna of Humility, both of 1366, and precedes the Madonna and Child Enthroned in Urbino of 1372. Produced four years before the painter's death in 1373, the Macerata triptych is therefore a document of Nuzi's mature and late manner, painted at a moment when his workshop dominated panel painting in the southern Marche.

The work cannot be understood apart from its prototype. Miklós Boskovits, in his catalogue of the National Gallery of Art, observed that the Washington triptych is exceptional for the disparity between the numerous small figures in the centre and the large figures in the laterals, which are even larger in scale than the enthroned Madonna, and for the rare repetition of Saint Anthony Abbot, who appears both in the central panel and again in the left lateral. He further noted that, uniquely, a practically identical, slightly larger version exists in the Duomo of Macerata, with a provenance from the church of Sant'Antonio Abate in that town. In the Macerata panel, then, Nuzi does not invent a composition but re-performs one: the Florentine design that Puccio di Simone had executed in Fabriano in 1354 is here translated into the idiom of the Marchigian painter who had himself contributed the left wing of the earlier work.

Compositionally, the panel is organised around a strong vertical axis that runs from the crucifix in the gable, through the crowned head of the Virgin, down to the inscribed base of the throne. The throne itself is almost entirely concealed beneath the sweep of the Virgin's mantle, so that the Mother of God seems to rise from a pedestal of drapery rather than to sit upon a piece of furniture. Around her, the angels are stacked in superimposed tiers on either side, their haloes forming overlapping discs of gold that read as a continuous, shimmering screen rather than as a space inhabited by bodies. The result is a deliberate tension between the Giottesque solidity of the central figure and the archaic, hieratic crowding of the celestial court, a tension already present in the Washington prototype and here accentuated by Nuzi's preference for surface pattern over spatial recession.

The Virgin is characterised by the elongated oval face, the narrow almond-shaped eyes and the small, closed mouth that recur throughout Nuzi's Madonnas of the 1360s. Her gaze is directed outward, towards the beholder, while the Child, seated upright on her left arm, turns towards her in a gesture that softens the frontal formality of the image. The most striking feature, however, is the mantle, whose entire surface is covered by a dense gold pattern worked over a dark ground in the manner of a luxury silk. This treatment, which Nuzi shares with contemporary Sienese and Florentine panel painters, transforms the garment into a precious object in its own right and aligns the Virgin with the imagery of the Queen of Heaven, an association reinforced by the crown resting upon her veil.

In the lower register, flanking the throne and standing in front of the angelic tiers, are four saints. The *Enciclopedia dell'Arte Medievale* identifies them as Saints Anthony Abbot, Rose, Benedict and Catherine of Alexandria, and the same reading is repeated in local guides. The crowned female saint in red on the right is readily recognisable as Catherine, and the dark-habited figure beside her is consistent with Benedict; the identification of the female saint on the left as Rose, however, cannot be confirmed from the reproduction and deserves verification in front of the panel. The presence of Anthony Abbot among these saints, in addition to his monumental figure in the left wing, is the most eloquent sign of the Antonine destination of the altarpiece, for it doubles the titular saint of the commissioning order within the heavenly court itself.

The inscription on the base of the throne deserves attention not only as a signature but as a statement of institutional identity. The donor, Frater Iohannes, styles himself *clericus* and *preceptor Tolentini*. Since the houses of the Antonine order were called preceptories, it has been surmised that Johannes was the superior of the Antonine house in Tolentino and perhaps the promoter of its daughter-house in Macerata. Luigi Serra, in 1929, conjectured that the same person had commissioned both the Macerata altarpiece and its Washington prototype, a hypothesis that would explain the otherwise puzzling decision to replicate a fifteen-year-old composition almost exactly. On this reading, the design functioned as a kind of corporate image of the Antonine communities in the Marche, repeated from house to house as an emblem of the order's devotional identity.

The central gable completes the vertical programme. At its apex, within a gilded quatrefoil, is the Crucified Christ, while two half-length figures in blue and rose garments lean towards the cross from the spandrels of the gable. The *Enciclopedia dell'Arte Medievale* describes the scene as the Crucifix between Elijah and Moses; this reading, unusual for a Crucifixion, would align the image with the Transfiguration typology, and it should be checked against the panel itself, since the mourning Virgin and Saint John would be the more customary pairing. Whatever the identity of the flanking figures, the placement of the Crucifixion directly above the Virgin and Child establishes a eucharistic axis familiar from Trecento altarpieces: the Incarnation presented below finds its sacrificial fulfilment above, immediately over the altar on which the Mass was celebrated.

Technically, the panel displays the full range of Nuzi's goldsmith-like surface treatment: incised and punched haloes, the pierced and cusped trefoil arch that frames the Virgin, and the sgraffito brocade of her mantle. Erling S. Skaug's systematic study of punch marks has made Nuzi's repertory of tools one of the best-documented in fourteenth-century central Italy, and the Macerata triptych, as a fully autograph and dated work, constitutes an essential reference point for that repertory. Stylistically, the panel shows how thoroughly Nuzi had absorbed the lessons of his Florentine sojourn and of his collaboration with Puccio di Simone, while filtering them through a Sienese-inflected taste for line, decorative richness and gentle facial types. The effect is less the dramatic realism of Puccio's 1354 centre than a serene, courtly and luminous devotional image.

#### Left Panel: Saint Anthony Abbot

The left wing is occupied by a single monumental figure of Saint Anthony Abbot, the Egyptian hermit venerated as the father of Christian monasticism and the patron of the Hospital Brothers of Saint Anthony. The Antonines, whose mother house was at Saint-Antoine-en-Viennois in the Dauphiné, maintained a network of preceptories and hospitals across Europe, where they cared above all for victims of ergotism, the disease known as "Saint Anthony's fire". The prominence of the saint in this altarpiece, and his repetition in the central panel, is fully explained by the provenance of the triptych from the church of Sant'Antonio Abate in Macerata, a foundation dependent, as the inscription suggests, on the Antonine preceptory of Tolentino.

The most immediately striking aspect of the figure is its scale. Anthony stands on a red patterned floor that projects forward like a carpet, and his body fills almost the entire height of the arched field, so that he appears physically larger than the enthroned Virgin in the adjoining panel. This inversion of the usual hierarchy of scale, inherited from the Washington prototype, is not a lapse of proportion but a deliberate strategy: the laterals present the saints as near and accessible intercessors, standing on the same ground as the viewer, while the central panel presents the celestial court as a distant, jewelled vision.

The saint's head is conceived with considerable psychological gravity. The long grey beard, the drawn cheeks and the heavy-lidded eyes convey age and ascetic discipline, in keeping with the hagiographic image of the desert father who lived to more than a hundred years. Nuzi models the face with soft transitions from the greenish underlayer to the warmer flesh tones, a technique rooted in the Tuscan tradition, yet he avoids the incisive naturalism that Puccio di Simone had brought to the figures of the 1354 triptych. The expression is meditative rather than dramatic, turned slightly towards the centre, as if the saint were addressing the Virgin on behalf of the faithful.

The habit is rendered as a heavy, dark brown garment with a hood drawn up over the head, falling in long vertical folds to the floor. The drapery is modelled with broad planes of light and shadow that confer weight and volume on the figure, recalling the Giottesque conception of the body as a solid mass beneath cloth. At the same time, the silhouette is simplified and elongated, and the lower edge of the habit spreads slightly on the floor, anchoring the figure. The sobriety of this monastic garment contrasts pointedly with the gold brocades of the Virgin's mantle and of Saint Julian's tunic, making visible within the altarpiece the ascetic ideal that the Antonine order claimed as its origin.

Anthony holds a tau-shaped staff in his left hand, the crutch-like emblem of the order, which the Antonines also wore as a blue tau on their habits. His right hand is raised, open, with the palm turned outward, in a gesture that may be read as speech, benediction or intercession; the same gesture appears in other figures of the saint by Nuzi, including the panel of 1372 now in the Museo Diocesano of Fabriano. At the lower left, near the saint's feet, a dark form is visible that is consistent with the pig, his traditional attribute, which alluded to the Antonines' privilege of letting their pigs roam freely in towns to support their hospitals; its legibility should be confirmed on the original.

The architectural frame defines the figure's space with a pointed arch whose intrados is filled with pierced, gilded cusps, and with twisted colonnettes running along the lateral edges. Small lozenge-shaped inserts decorate the piers between the panels. This carpentry, with its crocketed gables and pinnacled buttresses, belongs to the vocabulary of Gothic micro-architecture that transformed the altarpiece into an image of a church façade. Whether all elements of the present frame are original, or partly the result of later restoration and regilding, is a question that the photograph cannot resolve and that should be verified in the conservation literature.

In the gable above the saint, within a gilded quatrefoil set against a red field, appears the Angel Gabriel, winged and half-length, turning towards the centre. His counterpart, the Virgin Annunciate, occupies the corresponding quatrefoil above the right wing, so that the Annunciation is distributed across the two lateral gables, separated by the Crucifixion in the centre. The *Enciclopedia dell'Arte Medievale* records the Virgin Annunciate and the Archangel Gabriel within quatrefoils in the lateral gables. This arrangement, common in Trecento triptychs, frames the entire altarpiece between the moment of the Incarnation and the moment of the Redemption.

The doubling of Anthony Abbot, present both here and in the central panel, invites reflection on the function of the altarpiece. In the centre, Anthony belongs to the eternal court of heaven, one saint among many around the Virgin; in the wing, he stands alone, at human scale, as the patron and founder figure of the community that served at the altar. The two images thus articulate two modes of sanctity, the celestial and the institutional, and they made the triptych a mirror in which the Antonine brothers of Macerata could see both the object of their devotion and the model of their religious life.

#### Right Panel: Saint Julian the Hospitaller

The right wing presents Saint Julian, the patron saint of Macerata, whose cult was so central to the city that the cathedral itself is dedicated to him; the church of San Giuliano, known since the tenth century, served as the cathedral of the diocese of Macerata until 6 February 2023. The choice of Julian here signals the adaptation of the Washington scheme to a new civic context. In the 1354 prototype the right wing was occupied by Saint Venantius, the patron of Fabriano; in Macerata, Nuzi substituted the patron of the new host city, preserving the formula of an Antonine founder on the left paired with a local civic protector on the right.

The Saint Julian venerated in Macerata is identified with Julian the Hospitaller, whose legend, popularised by Jacobus de Voragine's *Golden Legend*, tells of a young nobleman who, deceived by a prophecy he had tried to escape, unwittingly killed his own parents, and who atoned for the crime by founding a hospice by a river where he ferried and sheltered travellers and the poor. The legend combines aristocratic identity, tragic guilt and redemptive charity, and it made Julian the patron of travellers, boatmen, innkeepers and hospitals. It is precisely this last dimension that makes him an especially apt companion for Anthony Abbot in an altarpiece commissioned by a hospital order.

Nuzi presents Julian as an idealised youth: the face is smooth and oval, framed by blond hair falling in soft curls, the eyes gently lowered, the expression composed and serene. The contrast with the aged, bearded Anthony across the altarpiece is complete, and it is surely intentional. Youth and age, courtly nobility and desert asceticism, the lay knight and the monastic founder are set in balance on either side of the Virgin, giving the triptych an internal rhetoric of complementary forms of holiness.

The saint's costume is among the most sumptuous passages in the whole altarpiece. He wears a knee-length tunic covered with a dense gold brocade pattern, similar in technique to the Virgin's mantle, and over it a dark cloak lined in a contrasting colour, which he gathers with his left hand across his body. The costume corresponds to the fashionable dress of the fourteenth-century nobility and presents Julian in his earthly identity as a young knight. The rich surface of the brocade, executed in sgraffito over gold, is a technical *tour de force* that links the lateral figure to the precious textures of the central panel and unifies the altarpiece decoratively.

In his right hand Julian holds a lance bearing a red pennant, the attribute of the knightly saint, which rises almost to the arch of the frame and establishes a strong vertical that mirrors Anthony's tau staff on the opposite wing. The banner also carries civic connotations, since the saint was venerated as the protector of Macerata, and the image of the armed patron bearing a standard would have resonated with the city's communal self-representation. Like Anthony, Julian stands on a red patterned floor, and his feet, turned outward, give the figure a stable, almost sculptural stance.

The pairing of Anthony and Julian gives the triptych its conceptual coherence. Both saints are, in different ways, figures of hospitality: Anthony as the patron of the Antonine hospitals, Julian as the penitent who founded a hospice for the poor and for travellers. For an order whose raison d'être was the care of the sick, and for a church in a city under Julian's protection, the two figures together articulated a theology of charitable service under the intercession of the Virgin. This convergence of order, city and devotion is one of the most interesting aspects of the Macerata replica compared with its Washington model.

In the right gable, within a quatrefoil on a red ground, the Virgin Annunciate appears in half-length, her head inclined and her hands crossed on her breast in the traditional gesture of acceptance. She answers the Angel Gabriel in the opposite gable, completing the Annunciation across the width of the altarpiece. Placed above the knightly Julian, the Virgin's humility offers a counterpoint to the saint's worldly splendour, and it frames the entire lower programme within the mystery of the Incarnation, which the central panel then presents in its fulfilled form, with the Child enthroned on his mother's lap.

The provenance history requires some caution. Boskovits records the altarpiece's provenance from the church of Sant'Antonio Abate in Macerata, a church now destroyed, whereas the *Enciclopedia dell'Arte Medievale* lists the triptych in Macerata, Pinacoteca e Museo Comunale, with a provenance from Monte Cassiano. Current sources locate the work in the Duomo, on the right wall of the presbytery, though others place it in the canons' hall of the sacristy. These discrepancies do not affect the attribution, which is secured by the signature, but they should be resolved before publication. As a fully autograph, signed and dated work of 1369, the Macerata triptych remains a key monument for understanding the late Nuzi, his relationship with Florentine painting, and the role of the Antonine order as a patron of art in the Trecento Marche.

## Iconography and Devotion: The Madonna of Humility, the Coronation, and the Saints

Allegretto Nuzi was not merely a transmitter of Tuscan forms; he was, in matters of iconography, a genuine innovator within his region, and his choice of subjects illuminates the devotional culture of the fourteenth-century Marche. The theme most closely associated with his name is the Madonna of Humility — the image of the Virgin seated not upon a throne but low upon the ground, or upon a cushion laid on the ground, nursing or holding the Child. The iconography, which emerged in the early fourteenth century, embodied a theology of humility (from the Latin humus, earth) closely bound up with the ideals of the mendicant orders, who wished to present the Church, symbolised by the Virgin, as humble and at the level of the common people, in deliberate contrast to the regal Maestà with its enthroned Queen of Heaven. Allegretto was among the principal agents of the diffusion of this type in the Adriatic region, producing multiple redactions of it — the signed and dated Madonna of Humility of 1366 at San Severino Marche foremost among them — and passing the model on to his pupil Francescuccio Ghissi, whose own numerous Madonnas of Humility descend directly from it. That an intimate, tender, devotionally accessible image should have flourished in Allegretto's hands is entirely consonant with his Sienese-inflected sensibility and with the mendicant patronage that sustained so much of his work.

The Coronation of the Virgin, by contrast, gave Allegretto occasion for the grand, hierarchical, ceremonial mode: the Southampton triptych shows Christ crowning his mother as Queen of Heaven to the accompaniment of music-making angels, with saints and biblical figures — Saint Stephen with his stones among them — gathered in the wings. Between these two poles, the humble and the celestial, ranges the rest of his Marian imagery: the enthroned Madonnas of Apiro, Macerata and Urbino, and the many small Madonnas for private devotion.

His repertory of saints is likewise revealing of local cult and confraternal patronage. Saint Venantius (Venanzio), the martyr-patron of Fabriano, recurs prominently — in the Washington triptych, in the Fabriano pentaptych, and elsewhere — a reflection of civic devotion to the town's protector. Saint Anthony Abbot, patron of a Fabriano church and of the sick, appears repeatedly. Saint Ursula, with her banner and palm, is the subject not only of the Vatican triptych's lateral panel but of an entire fresco cycle. Mary Magdalene, Saint Augustine, Saint Nicholas of Tolentino (the great Augustinian saint), Saint Michael the Archangel, Saint Lawrence, Saint Catherine of Alexandria with her spiked wheel, Saint Bartholomew — the roster maps precisely onto the mendicant houses and confraternities of Fabriano and its neighbours: the Dominicans of San Domenico, the Augustinians of Sant'Agostino, the various lay brotherhoods for whom Allegretto and his shop supplied altarpieces and frescoes. His art was, in the deepest sense, an art of and for its community, its subjects dictated by the devotional needs of the churches, orders and confraternities that commissioned it.

## Technique, Materials and the Poetry of Gold

If Allegretto Nuzi's compositions were learned in Tuscany, his surfaces were his own glory, and it is in his handling of gold and colour that his originality as a craftsman is most apparent. The reputation of his gold grounds — their quality and their decorative richness — was, the sources insist, extraordinary from the very beginning and grew still greater in later centuries, so that his works were contested by connoisseurs and collectors and dispersed into the great museums of Italy and abroad. To understand this achievement one must understand the techniques of Trecento panel painting, in which the painter worked in egg tempera on a gessoed wooden panel and applied gold leaf to a bole ground, which was then burnished to a mirror-like brilliance and worked with a repertory of decorative techniques.

Allegretto was a master, and something of an experimenter, in all of these. The scholarship describes his surfaces as achieved "with the granulation and punching of the metal leaf and with sgraffito" — that is, with the full arsenal of gold-tooling techniques. The first of these is punchwork: the stamping of small motifs into the burnished gold with iron punches, used to decorate haloes, borders and backgrounds. The second is granatura, the stippling of the gold surface with a small rounded punch to make it, in the celebrated phrase of the contemporary handbook of Cennino Cennini, "glitter like grains of millet." The third is sgraffito (or sgraffio), the technique by which a painter lays colour over gold and then scratches away the pigment to reveal the metal beneath, used above all for the rendering of gold-brocaded textiles. Allegretto combined these with great freedom, and he delighted in the depiction of sumptuous "operated" fabrics patterned with fantastical motifs — birds, tortoises and the like — that lend his mature and late panels their air of courtly luxury and look forward to the textile splendours of Gentile da Fabriano.

Punchwork is of more than aesthetic interest, for it has become one of the most powerful tools of modern connoisseurship. Because each workshop possessed its own set of iron punches, the individual punch-marks left in the gold can serve as a kind of fingerprint, allowing scholars to associate unsigned works with a particular shop and to trace relationships between workshops. The foundational study in this field is the monumental corpus of Trecento Tuscan punch-marks compiled by the Norwegian scholar Erling Skaug, whose catalogue of punches "from Giotto to Fra Angelico" (published in two volumes at Oslo in 1994) has become the indispensable reference for the attribution and dating of early Italian paintings. The method has been applied directly to Allegretto and his circle: a panel of Saints Catherine and Bartholomew in the National Gallery, London, unsigned, has been connected to the workshop of Allegretto Nuzi precisely on the basis of its punch-marks, which recur in other works by the artist, even as the style of painting and the lettering of the inscriptions point rather to the hand of Francescuccio Ghissi — a discrepancy resolved by the hypothesis that Nuzi designed the picture while Ghissi executed it in the shared workshop using the shared tools. The point is a general one: in a period when iron punches were precious objects passed down and shared between painters, the study of gold tooling offers an avenue into the collaborative realities of workshop practice that stylistic analysis alone cannot reach. Allegretto's punches have accordingly been catalogued and studied as part of the wider effort to map the material culture of central-Italian painting.

Colour, finally, completes the account. Allegretto's palette — the lapis-blue mantles, the brick reds and peach and azure of the saints' robes, the orange tunics patterned with blue flowers, all set against and enriched by gold — is deployed with a decorative logic that grows stronger as his career advances. In his frescoes, the sources note a particular sensitivity in the use of colour that would find a wide echo in the later "courtly" painting of the Marche; in his panels, colour and tooled gold together produce that fusion of gravity and preciousness which is the signature of his art.

## The Frescoes of Fabriano

Although Allegretto's fame today rests largely on his panel paintings, dispersed across the world's museums, his contemporaries would have known him equally as a fresco painter, and it is in the churches of Fabriano that his monumental art survives — hidden, in several cases, in the interstices left between older apses and the newer choirs constructed in later rebuildings, so that the cycles have acquired the character of "secret chapels."

Three churches preserve his mural work: the cathedral of San Venanzio, the church of Sant'Agostino, and the church of San Domenico (later Santa Lucia Novella).

In the cathedral of San Venanzio, hidden behind the wooden stalls of the choir and reached by a narrow passage, lies the chapel of San Lorenzo, whose walls Allegretto frescoed with Stories of the life of Saint Lawrence, together with a Virgin enthroned with saints. The dating of this cycle has been debated: a document of 1365 records that work was still being carried out on this part of the church, which supports the belief that Allegretto was at work there around that time, and the stylistic proximity of the frescoes to the Vatican triptych of 1365 reinforces a dating to the middle of the decade; against this, some scholarship, relying on old manuscript guides, has proposed a date as early as around 1345, while others favour simply "the 1360s," when the artist had reached full maturity. The tribune of San Venanzio, erected in the 1360s, has been described as an unrecognised summit of central-Italian Gothic architecture, and it is within it that these frescoes are preserved.

In San Domenico, Allegretto and his workshop frescoed the chapel of Sant'Orsola (Saint Ursula) with an extensive cycle of the Stories of Saint Ursula, together with a Martyrdom of Saint Stephen and other scenes, and decorated the chapter house (sala capitolare) and the sacristy. These works, slightly later than the cathedral cycle, are the product of a broad collaboration between the master and his assistants: at least two hands besides Allegretto's own have been identified, with the master himself recognisable in the figure of the Archangel Michael and in certain scenes of the saint's life, while a Saint Dominic in the sacristy, only recently brought to light, has also been given to him. It is these Ursula frescoes that have sometimes been singled out as his masterpiece in the fresco medium.

### Allegretto Nuzi and Workshop, The Voyage of Saint Ursula and Pope Cyriacus, c. 1365

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-30.jpg" alt="Fresco by Allegretto Nuzi and workshop in a pointed lunette framed by a band of red, white and green geometric ornament. A dark boat sails across a grey-green sea between two ochre rocky shores. On the left, Saint Ursula's red-haired companions stand in a close row with hands joined in prayer; at the centre Ursula, haloed and in a pale gown with red dots, crosses her hands on her breast. On the right stand a cardinal in a red hat, Pope Cyriacus in a tall white tiara, and a bearded helmsman in red working the steering oar. A white sail and a white banner with a red cross rise above the passengers. Fragments of other scenes, an armoured leg and flames, are visible above the frame">
  <figcaption>Allegretto Nuzi and Workshop, The Voyage of Saint Ursula and Pope Cyriacus, c. 1365, fresco, Fabriano, church of San Domenico (formerly Santa Lucia Novella), Chapel of Saint Ursula (also known as the Chapel of Saint Michael).</figcaption>
</figure>

The fresco belongs to the cycle that decorates the chapel opening to the left of the presbytery in the church of San Domenico at Fabriano, formerly known as Santa Lucia Novella. The chapel is dedicated jointly to Saint Michael and Saint Ursula. Its programme combines a monumental Archangel Michael weighing souls, which fills an entire wall, with narrative cycles of Saint Ursula and Saint Nicholas on the facing walls. The date of 1365 commonly attached to the scene should be treated as an approximation rather than a documented fact. Claudia Ghisalberti's entry in the *Enciclopedia dell'Arte Medievale* places the Ursula cycle shortly after the frescoes of the Chapel of San Lorenzo in Fabriano Cathedral. The dating of that cycle is itself disputed: Miklós Boskovits proposed c. 1345, Alessandro Marabottini c. 1365, and Giampiero Donnini the 1360s. A date in the mid-to-late 1360s is therefore the most defensible, placing the frescoes in the same years as the signed Vatican triptych of 1365.

The attribution requires nuance. Donnini observed in 1986 that the Ursula stories resulted from extensive collaboration between the painter and his assistants. He identified at least two hands besides Nuzi's own, which he recognised in the Archangel Michael and in some scenes from the saint's life. Other sources are more cautious still: the Touring Club Italiano guide describes the chapel's frescoes simply as school of Allegretto Nuzi. The formula "Allegretto Nuzi and workshop" is therefore the most accurate label for this scene. It acknowledges both the unity of the design, which clearly depends on the master, and the plurality of execution typical of large fresco campaigns in the Trecento.

The episode derives from the legend of Ursula as fixed in Jacobus de Voragine's *Golden Legend*. In that account the British princess, betrothed against her will to a pagan prince, obtains a delay to undertake a pilgrimage to Rome with eleven thousand virgin companions. On the return journey the fleet is joined by Pope Cyriacus, who renounces the papacy to share their fate, and by several prelates. The company sails up the Rhine to Cologne, where they are massacred by the besieging Huns. The legend also explains that Cyriacus's name was struck from the list of popes because he had abandoned his office. Nuzi's scene represents this final voyage, the moment of serene confidence before the martyrdom, when the virgins and the churchmen travel together towards a destiny the viewer already knows.

The composition is governed by the pointed arch of the field. The dark hull of the boat curves upward at both ends, echoing the arch and forming a crescent that cradles the passengers. Two ochre rocky banks, drawn in the stylised, faceted manner inherited from Giotto and the Sienese, close the scene at left and right. The river thus becomes a narrow channel between them, appropriate to the Rhine of the legend. The water is rendered as a grey-green surface animated by white, rhythmic wave lines, a decorative convention rather than an observed sea. The whole field is enclosed by a broad ornamental border of red, white and green geometric motifs with small roundels, which asserts the flat, tapestry-like character of the wall surface.

On the left, Ursula's companions are packed into the boat in a dense row of overlapping heads, turned three-quarters and nearly identical in type. Their red-gold hair is bound with fillets, and their pale gowns and hands are joined in prayer. The repetition is deliberate. It conveys the idea of a multitude, the eleven thousand of the legend, within the few figures the space allows, and it gives the group the quality of a choir moving in unison. Ursula stands at the centre, distinguished by her gold halo and by a pale gown enlivened with small red dots. Her hands are crossed on her breast in a gesture of humility and acceptance, and her gaze is directed calmly outward. The oval faces, long noses and small closed mouths correspond closely to the female type of Nuzi's panel paintings of the 1360s.

The right half of the boat belongs to the churchmen, and here the painting shows its greatest variety of characterisation. Pope Cyriacus wears a tall white tiara, and beside him stands a cardinal in the broad red hat of his rank. This is an anachronism, typical of medieval narrative painting, that translates the legend into the ecclesiastical world familiar to fourteenth-century viewers. In the stern a bearded helmsman in a red garment works the long steering oar, which cuts diagonally across the hull towards the lower right. His furrowed, individualised features contrast with the idealised faces of the virgins. The contrast between the anonymous serenity of the maidens and the more incisive heads on the right may reflect the different hands Donnini identified within the cycle, though this can only be judged in front of the wall.

Above the passengers rise the white sail and a white banner bearing a red cross. The banner is both the vexillum of the Resurrection and Ursula's personal attribute, and it announces the triumph of martyrdom within a scene of apparent calm. The motif links the fresco to Nuzi's panel painting. In the Vatican triptych of 1365, the lateral panels show Saint Michael in armour with a sword and Saint Ursula with her banner and palm of martyrdom. [Enrica Neri Lusanna](/scholars.html#neri-lusanna-enrica) suggested that the triptych was originally painted for the church of Santa Lucia, which is San Domenico itself. If so, the triptych and the chapel shared the same pair of dedicatees, and the fresco cycle can be read as the narrative and monumental counterpart of the devotional panel, and together they testify to a cult of Michael and Ursula in this Dominican church. The church's patrons were the Chiavelli, lords of Fabriano, whose fourteenth-century coat of arms survives on the exterior. Whether they sponsored the chapel itself remains to be established.

The fresco exemplifies the qualities for which Nuzi's wall painting has been admired: the ability to combine a simplified grandeur of the whole with the narrative immediacy of detail. The decorative clarity of the design, the luminous palette of pale flesh, white, red and grey-green, and the courtly elegance of the figures made the cycle an important source for the later "courtly" painting of the Marche, culminating a generation later in Gentile da Fabriano. Fragments of adjoining scenes, visible above the border, show that the lunette was only one element of a continuous decorative system: an armoured leg at upper left and tongues of flame at upper right. Access to the chapel has been restricted since the earthquake of 2016. The 2021–2022 exhibition *Allegretto Nuzi e il Trecento a Fabriano* at the Pinacoteca Molajoli documented the cycle in its catalogue, but visitors could not reach the frescoes themselves. The scene remains one of the key surviving witnesses of Nuzi's activity as a fresco painter and of the collaborative practice of his Fabriano workshop.

### Allegretto Nuzi (or Francescuccio Ghissi?), Babylon the Great Seated on the Seven-Headed Beast

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-31.jpg" alt="Fresco lunette on a dark blue-black ground. A young woman with long loose reddish hair, wearing a pale off-the-shoulder gown with long hanging sleeves, sits on red patterned cushions on the back of a scaly grey-green dragon. She holds a long staff in her right hand and raises a golden chalice in her left, and one bare foot rests on stylised blue and white waves. The dragon's long tail curls behind her, and seven horned heads with open, fanged mouths rise on serpentine necks to the right, each labelled with a vice: SUPERBIA, INVIDIA, ACCIDIA, IRA, AVARITIA, GULA, LUXURIA. A Latin inscription at the top names the figure as Babylon the Great. The lunette is framed by red bands, with green and red foliage on the vault above">
  <figcaption>Allegretto Nuzi (or Francescuccio Ghissi?), Babylon the Great Seated on the Seven-Headed Beast, mid-1360s, fresco, Fabriano, church of San Domenico (formerly Santa Lucia Novella), sacristy, probably the former chapter hall of the Dominican convent.</figcaption>
</figure>

The fresco decorates a lunette of the room that now serves as the sacristy of San Domenico at Fabriano, the former church of Santa Lucia Novella. The room was probably the chapter hall of the Dominican convent before it became a sacristy. Its walls were painted in the second half of the Trecento by the leading masters of the Fabriano school. The attribution of this particular scene is not settled. Popular accounts divide the room between Allegretto Nuzi and Francescuccio Ghissi, assigning the Woman of Babylon, the Dormition of the Virgin and Saint Arsenius with his companions to Ghissi, and the Crucifixion, the Saint Dominic and the Madonna and Child to Nuzi. The photographic archive of the Fondazione Zeri, by contrast, files the image of "the Whore on the seven-headed Hydra" among Nuzi's frescoes. Ghisalberti's entry in the *Enciclopedia dell'Arte Medievale* credits Nuzi only with the Saint Dominic in the sacristy, following Donnini. The prudent label is therefore "Allegretto Nuzi or Francescuccio Ghissi", or "Fabriano school, circle of Allegretto Nuzi". The dating to the mid-1360s depends on the stylistic chronology of Nuzi's frescoes, not on any document.

The subject derives from the seventeenth chapter of the Apocalypse. An angel shows John the great harlot who sits upon many waters. She is seated on a scarlet beast with seven heads and ten horns, dressed in purple and scarlet, adorned with gold, pearls and precious stones, and holding a golden cup full of abominations. On her forehead is written a name:

> Babylon magna, mater fornicationum et abominationum terrae* (Rev. 17:5).

The painted inscription at the top of the lunette reproduces this title in Gothic capitals, apparently in shortened form:

> BABILON MAGNA MATE[R] HABOMI/NATIONUM TERRE.

It seems to omit *fornicationum* and spells *abominationum* with the initial H common in medieval Latin. The reading should be checked on the wall, since the letters are abbreviated and partly abraded.

The composition exploits the wide, low shape of the lunette with notable skill. The beast's body stretches horizontally across the field. Its tail rises in a long curve on the left, following the arch, while the seven necks spread in a fan on the right, filling the opposite curve of the frame. The woman sits at the centre-left in rigorous frontality, forming a strong vertical axis against the horizontal mass of the dragon. The deep blue-black ground, now darkened, isolates the figures like an emblem on a heraldic field. Along the lower edge, stylised blue and white waves render the "many waters" upon which Babylon sits. According to the angel's own explanation (Rev. 17:15), these waters signify peoples, multitudes, nations and tongues. Her single bare foot resting on the waves is a small but eloquent detail: the power of Babylon rests on the unstable multitude of the world.

The figure of the woman departs from the biblical text in revealing ways. Instead of the purple and scarlet of Revelation, she wears a pale, close-fitting gown cut low across the shoulders, with long hanging sleeves in the fashion of the mid-fourteenth century. Her long reddish hair falls loose over her shoulders, held only by a thin fillet. Loose hair was the conventional sign of the unmarried woman and, in a moral context, of the courtesan. Her face is youthful, oval and impassive, with the narrow eyes and small mouth of the Fabriano female type. Seduction is thus expressed not through overt sensuality but through worldly elegance and composure, in line with the preaching tradition that presented vice as attractive before revealing it as deadly. In her left hand she raises the golden chalice of abominations, in her right a long staff ending in a knob with a red tassel, a sceptre that marks her dominion over the kings of the earth.

The beast is the most inventive part of the image. Rather than the scarlet monster of the text, it is a grey-green dragon covered in overlapping scales, with clawed feet and a ridged, segmented tail. Seven serpentine necks issue from its body, each ending in a horned, wolf-like head with gaping, fanged jaws and a red forked tongue. Each head is labelled in white capitals with one of the seven capital vices: SUPERBIA, INVIDIA, ACCIDIA, IRA, AVARITIA, GULA and LUXURIA. The sequence opens with pride, root of all sins, at the top, and closes with lust at the bottom, closest to the woman. This substitution of the vices for the seven kings or seven hills of the angel's interpretation (Rev. 17:9–10) is a fully medieval, moralising exegesis. It transforms an eschatological and political symbol into a catalogue of sins that every Christian could recognise within himself.

This moral reading acquires its full meaning in the Dominican setting. The Order of Preachers had made the systematic analysis of vices and virtues a cornerstone of its pastoral mission; the Dominican William Peraldus's *Summa de vitiis* was among the most widely used preaching manuals of the later Middle Ages. If the room was indeed the chapter hall, the image also acquires a liturgical-disciplinary function. In the daily chapter, the friars gathered to hear a reading from the Rule and to accuse themselves publicly of their faults. For a community examining its own failings, the harlot enthroned on the vices offered a permanent visual *speculum peccatorum*: a mirror of the sins to be confessed and resisted, and a reminder of the eternal ruin that awaits those who drink from Babylon's cup.

Stylistically, the fresco combines the incisive linear drawing and decorative clarity of the Fabriano school with a vivid gift for fantastic invention. The rhythmic repetition of scales, the calligraphic curves of the necks and the careful variation of the heads, each turned at a different angle, show a painter accustomed to transforming nature into ornament. Two readings are possible. The attribution to Nuzi would stress the continuity with the courtly elegance of the Ursula cycle in the nearby chapel. The attribution to Ghissi, Nuzi's closest follower and the specialist of his workshop's formulas, would explain the somewhat simplified modelling of the woman's face and drapery. The surface has suffered considerably: large areas of plaster are lost along the left side, a long vertical crack runs through the beast's necks and the woman's gown, and the dark ground has probably altered. The vault above preserves fragments of green and red foliate scrolls, a reminder of the original decorative richness of the room.

Within the programme of the chapter hall, the Babylon lunette was not an isolated image but part of a dialectic of salvation and damnation. The Crucifixion, the Dormition, the Madonna and Child and the solemn figure of Saint Dominic presented the models of redemption, holiness and the Order's own identity. Babylon, the "mother of abominations", stood as their dark antitype: the false mother opposed to the Mother of God, the cup of abominations opposed to the Eucharistic chalice, and the worldly city opposed to the heavenly Jerusalem. The fresco is one of the most original apocalyptic images of fourteenth-century Marchigian painting. It shows how the painters of the Nuzi circle could adapt a complex biblical vision to the concrete needs of a religious community, turning prophecy into a tool of moral instruction.

### Allegretto Nuzi (attr.), The Four Evangelists

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-32.jpg" alt="Gothic cross vault decorated with fresco, seen from below. Four diagonal ribs painted with bands of red, blue and white quatrefoil and lozenge ornament meet at a small carved boss. In the lower web, best preserved, the young Saint John the Evangelist, haloed, in a green tunic and ochre mantle, sits on a pink Gothic throne with a pointed canopy, writing at a slanting desk and raising his right hand towards his head. The throne stands on a platform with an orange patterned carpet set against a blue-green ground. The other three webs are largely lost, showing only fragments of thrones, draperies and desks on blue. Circular medallions stand at the corners of the webs; one shows a winged ox, the others are faded or empty">
  <figcaption>Allegretto Nuzi (attr.), The Four Evangelists, third quarter of the 14th century, fresco, Perugia, basilica of San Domenico, Cappella di San Nicolò (now Cappella del Nome di Gesù), vault.</figcaption>
</figure>

The vault covers the inner apsidal chapel on the left of the Gothic choir of San Domenico in Perugia. The chapel was originally dedicated to Saint Nicholas and is now known as the Cappella del Nome di Gesù. It is documented in 1382, when Alberto di Nino di Lello Guidalotti wished to be buried there, and it had probably belonged to his family from the start. The later history of the chapel is tied to the same lineage: Fra Angelico's Guidalotti Polyptych was painted for its altar in 1447–1449. The frescoes of the vault are traditionally attributed to Allegretto Nuzi. The attribution goes back to Miklós Boskovits, who in 1973 assigned to Nuzi the frescoes of San Domenico in Perugia, some of which are now detached and kept in the Galleria Nazionale dell'Umbria. No document secures either the authorship or the date. Since the chapel belongs to the crossing and choir built in the mid-fourteenth century, the decoration can only be placed broadly in the third quarter of the century.

The theme follows a long-established tradition in Italian Gothic vault decoration. The four Evangelists occupy the four webs of the vault, which symbolise the four quarters of the world to which the Gospel was carried. Their writing becomes a kind of celestial architecture above the altar. The most influential precedent was [Cimabue](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Cimabue.html)'s vault in the crossing of the Upper Church at Assisi, where the Evangelists are shown writing, each with his symbol and a view of the region he evangelised. From Assisi the scheme spread throughout central Italy. The same subject appears in Nuzi's own work in Fabriano: in the Chapel of San Lorenzo in the cathedral, the vault was decorated with the Evangelists, of whom Saint John is also partly visible. The coincidence of theme and of state of preservation makes the comparison between the two vaults particularly instructive for the attribution.

The decorative framework is organised with great clarity. The four diagonal ribs are painted as broad bands of repeating ornament, quatrefoils and lozenges in red, blue, white and ochre, which convert the structural members into precious, jewelled borders. They converge on a small carved boss at the centre of the vault. The webs are painted on a ground of deep blue, now largely abraded. Where the blue has survived it gives the impression of a night sky. Where it has fallen, it reveals a greenish or greyish underlayer, as in the lower web. This loss pattern is typical of azurite, which was applied *a secco* over the dry plaster and therefore tends to flake away.
