Circular medallions near the springing of each web held the symbols of the Evangelists. A frieze of triangular and scalloped motifs runs along the base of the wall arch, completing the frame.

Saint John, in the lower web, is the only figure that has survived almost intact, and he allows the quality of the lost cycle to be gauged. He is represented as a beardless youth with soft curling hair, following the Western convention that distinguishes him as the youngest of the apostles. His gold halo is set off against the blue. He wears a green-blue tunic under an ochre mantle that falls in broad folds over his knees. With his left hand he writes on a slanting desk that projects towards the viewer, while his right hand is raised towards his head. The gesture evokes the moment of inspiration, the Evangelist listening to the divine voice before setting the words on the page. The face, with its oval contour and gentle, attentive expression, corresponds to the youthful male type found in Nuzi's panel paintings.

The throne is a small masterpiece of painted architecture. It is a Gothic seat of pink stone, crowned by a pointed canopy with crockets and pinnacles, and its side walls are pierced by openings that suggest depth. It stands on a stepped platform with a green base, and in front of it a large orange carpet decorated with a lozenge pattern is laid out in steep perspective. The painter has adapted this box-like space to the awkward triangular shape of the web. The throne is placed on the axis of the triangle, and the platform and carpet spread outward to fill its lower width. This perspectival construction reflects the Florentine lesson of Giotto and his followers, in particular Maso di Banco, whose influence on Nuzi's early formation is widely recognised. At the same time, the patterned surfaces and the pastel architecture show the painter's taste for decorative refinement.

The other three webs have suffered severe losses, and their figures can only be reconstructed from fragments. In the left web, part of a throne with a red drapery, the edge of a writing desk and some architectural elements survive. In the upper web, little remains except the lower edge of a desk covered with a triangular patterned cloth and a small round object that may be an inkwell. In the right web, a violet drapery and part of a throne can be distinguished. Of the medallions, the one at lower right still shows a winged ox, the symbol of Luke, while the others are faded or empty. The loss of the other figures prevents any secure reconstruction of the order in which the Evangelists were arranged, but the placement of the ox suggests that Luke occupied the right web.

Stylistically, the surviving figure of John supports the traditional attribution. The ample, well-structured drapery, the solid seated pose and the perspectival throne recall Nuzi's Florentine experience. The soft modelling of the face, the elongated proportions and the decorative colour, with its combination of green, ochre, pink and orange, correspond to his Marchigian manner. The painted architecture of the throne can be compared with the Gothic seats of his panel Madonnas. Given the scarcity of surviving material and the absence of documents, however, the attribution should be presented as traditional rather than proven. A comparison with the Evangelist vault of the Chapel of San Lorenzo in Fabriano would be the most direct way to test it.

The Perugia vault is important for understanding Nuzi's career beyond the Marche. If the attribution is correct, it documents the activity of the Fabriano painter in the Dominican church of the Umbrian capital. This is consistent with the dense artistic exchanges between Umbria and the Marche in the fourteenth century, and with Nuzi's connections with the Dominican order, attested at Fabriano by the frescoes in San Domenico (Santa Lucia Novella). The vault also shows how a small funerary chapel, later linked to one of Perugia's leading families, could be given a monumental decoration of cosmic and theological significance. The figure of Saint John, rescued from the general ruin of the cycle, remains a precious witness of the refinement of Nuzi's painting in fresco and of its dissemination along the routes between Fabriano and Perugia.

### Allegretto Nuzi (attr.), Saint John the Evangelist

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-33.jpg" alt="Fragmentary fresco of Saint John the Evangelist, shown from the knees up against a dark blue-black ground with extensive paint losses. The youthful, beardless saint has short curling auburn hair and a halo incised with radiating lines. He looks out at the viewer with a calm, slightly serious expression. He wears a pale tunic with a round neckline and long sleeves, and a red mantle falls from his left shoulder and wraps around his lower body in broad folds. With both hands he holds a closed book with a greenish cover against his chest. The upper part of the wall above the figure is covered by plain plaster">
  <figcaption>Allegretto Nuzi (attr.), Saint John the Evangelist, traditionally dated 1336 (?), fresco, Fabriano, church of Santa Maria Maddalena (property of the Pia Università dei Cartai), right wall.</figcaption>
</figure>

The fresco decorates the right wall of the small church of Santa Maria Maddalena at Fabriano. The church stands in the medieval Borgo del Piano, outside the city walls, in the quarter where Fabriano's celebrated paper industry developed. It belongs to the Pia Università dei Cartai, the historic papermakers' guild, and its first documentary mention dates to 1326. Three crosses on the exterior link the church to a hospital dependent on the Arcispedale di Santo Spirito in Sassia in Rome, so it originally served a charitable as well as devotional function. Two fourteenth-century frescoes survive on the right wall: this Saint John the Evangelist, dated 1336 and attributed to Allegretto Nuzi, and a Crucifixion with the Virgin and Saint John painted by the Master of Campodonico. The high altar holds Orazio Gentileschi's *Penitent Magdalen*, painted during his Fabriano period (1612–1615), a reminder that the Magdalen was the patron saint of the papermakers.

The chronological position of the work requires careful consideration. The date of 1336 would make the fresco by far the earliest known work by Nuzi. Ghisalberti's entry in the *Enciclopedia dell'Arte Medievale* gives his birth at around 1320. His first secure documentation is in 1346 in Florence, where he enrolled in the Compagnia di San Luca, and his first securely dated work is the left wing of the Washington triptych of 1354. A fresco of 1336 would therefore have been painted when he was barely an adult, a decade before any documented activity. This is not impossible, but the source of the date is not clear from the available literature: it may derive from a lost or damaged inscription, or from local tradition.

The juxtaposition with the Crucifixion by the Master of Campodonico is of considerable historical interest. Nuzi was probably trained in Fabriano by local masters, including the so-called Master of Campodonico, before encountering the work of Giotto and his followers in Florence. The Master of Campodonico was himself the principal channel through which the art of Ambrogio Lorenzetti reached Fabriano, even if indirectly. The two frescoes, side by side on the same wall, thus offer a rare opportunity to compare the art of the master and that of his presumed pupil. Should the early date be confirmed, the Saint John would document the very beginnings of Nuzi's career within the workshop culture of his native town.

The saint is represented as a youthful, beardless figure standing at full height, now visible from the knees up against a dark ground. This is the conventional type that distinguishes John as the youngest of the apostles and the beloved disciple. His head is framed by short, curling auburn hair and by a halo whose surface is incised with fine radiating lines, a technique that imitates in fresco the tooled gold haloes of panel painting. The face is broad and oval, with a straight nose, a small, firmly closed mouth and large almond-shaped eyes that look directly out at the viewer. The expression is calm and slightly grave, conveying the contemplative character traditionally associated with the author of the Fourth Gospel.

The costume and its drapery are the most accomplished part of the figure. The saint wears a pale tunic with a simple round neckline and long sleeves, gathered at the wrist. Over it a red mantle falls from the left shoulder and wraps around the lower body, gathered into a broad curve at the hip that falls in deep, sweeping folds to the lower edge of the surviving fragment. The folds are modelled with a controlled gradation from pale pink to deep red and articulated by incisive brown lines, particularly on the sleeve of the tunic. The effect is one of solidity and weight, rooted in the Giottesque tradition, and it reveals a painter already concerned with the plastic presence of the body beneath the cloth.

John holds his Gospel with both hands against his chest: the right hand, with its long, delicately drawn fingers, rests on the front cover, while the left supports the book from below. The cover, now greenish and abraded, was probably originally decorated or partly painted *a secco*. The gesture is at once protective and demonstrative, presenting the sacred text to the viewer as the saint's defining attribute. In the context of a hospital church, the figure of the Evangelist, author of the Gospel of love and of the Book of Revelation, would have had a clear devotional function as an intercessor for the sick and the poor sheltered by the institution.

Stylistically, the fresco combines a solid, sculptural conception of the figure with a still somewhat archaic simplification of forms. The broad planes of the face, the strong outlines and the clear distinction of colour areas reflect the Giottesque culture of the Adriatic regions, transmitted to Fabriano partly through the Riminese school and partly through local masters such as the Master of Campodonico. The monumental conception of the drapery is close to the manner that Nuzi would develop more fully after his Florentine experience, when he absorbed the art of Bernardo Daddi and Maso di Banco. The fresco can therefore be read either as an early work that already anticipates those developments, or as a work of the late 1340s. In the second case, the date of 1336 would need to be reconsidered.

The condition of the fresco is precarious. The dark ground, probably originally blue applied *a secco* over a dark underlayer, has largely flaked away, leaving numerous losses; the plaster surface is marked by cracks and abrasions; and the upper part of the wall has been covered with later plaster. Nevertheless, the head, the hands and much of the drapery survive in legible condition. Despite its fragmentary state, the Saint John in Santa Maria Maddalena is an important document of Trecento painting in Fabriano. It bears witness to the relationship between Nuzi and the Master of Campodonico, and it is closely tied to the history of the papermakers' guild, whose church preserves it to this day.

### The Virgin Interceding with Christ Enthroned

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-34.jpg" alt="Detached fresco, heavily damaged, mounted on canvas within a dark frame; large areas of loss are filled with neutral grey plaster. On the right, Christ sits on a green architectural throne, bearded, with long reddish-blond hair and a halo. He wears a pale greenish-blue tunic with a small rectangular gold ornament on the breast and a rose-red mantle draped over his shoulders and knees. His right hand is raised, open, towards the Virgin, and his left rests on his lap. On the left, the Virgin kneels in profile, haloed, wearing a dark mantle edged with gold over a red dress, her hands crossed on her breast and her face lifted towards Christ. Fragments of a painted frame survive along the left and lower edges">
  <figcaption>The Virgin Interceding with Christ Enthroned, 1360-70, detached fresco transferred to canvas, 144 × 84 cm, Fabriano, Pinacoteca Civica "Bruno Molajoli". It comes from the church of San Giovanni del Cantone in Fabriano, demolished in 1903.</figcaption>
</figure>

The fresco belongs to the group of wall paintings rescued from Fabriano's lost medieval churches and now preserved in the Pinacoteca Civica "Bruno Molajoli". It comes from the church of San Giovanni del Cantone, demolished in 1903. The painting was detached from its original wall and transferred to a canvas support before or at the time of the demolition. It thus survived the destruction of its architectural setting, but at the cost of its original context: we no longer know on which wall it was placed, whether it formed part of a larger programme, or whether it was an isolated votive image. Its present state, with large lacunae filled with neutral plaster, is typical of frescoes recovered in these circumstances. It allows the surviving parts to be read without inventing what has been lost.

The Pinacoteca attributes the fresco to Allegretto Nuzi, and the dating to 1360–1370 places it in the painter's full maturity, the decade of his dated works, several of them signed: the Vatican triptych of 1365, the Apiro polyptych and the San Severino Madonna of Humility of 1366, and the Macerata triptych of 1369. The attribution is supported by the facial types, the broad handling of the drapery and the pale, luminous palette. The bearded Christ, with his oval face, long straight nose and calm gaze, closely resembles the Christ figures in Nuzi's panel paintings of the 1360s. The Virgin's profile, with its small features and upturned face, belongs to the same repertoire of gentle, idealised female types that characterises his late production. As with Nuzi's other frescoes in Fabriano, such as the Chapel of San Lorenzo in the cathedral and the Chapel of Saint Ursula in San Domenico, the participation of assistants cannot be excluded. The damaged state of the surface, however, makes it impossible to distinguish different hands, and the design and the quality of the surviving heads are fully consistent with the master.

The composition sets the two figures on a diagonal that rises from the kneeling Virgin at lower left to the enthroned Christ at upper right. The asymmetry is deliberate: the Virgin's lower position and upward gaze express her role as a supplicant, while Christ's elevated position on the throne expresses his majesty as judge and redeemer. The two figures are linked by Christ's raised right hand, extended towards the Virgin's face, and by her gaze directed towards him. The empty space between them, now partly lost, was the charged interval of the dialogue, the place where prayer and response meet. Despite the losses, this central axis of communication remains perfectly legible.

Christ is represented as a mature, bearded figure with long reddish-blond hair falling onto his shoulders, framed by a large halo with incised ornament. He wears a tunic of a pale greenish-blue tone, described in the museum's entry as light blue. The present greenish cast may partly reflect the alteration of the original pigment or the loss of a *secco* layer. Over it he wears a rose-red mantle that covers his shoulders and falls in heavy folds over his knees. On the breast a small rectangular gold element can be seen, perhaps the clasp of the mantle or an ornamental plaque. His right hand is raised with the palm open towards the Virgin in a gesture that can be read as benediction, as acknowledgement of her prayer, or as a sign of acceptance of her intercession. His left hand rests calmly on his lap.

The throne on which Christ sits is rendered as a piece of green architecture with a flat back and moulded elements, of which only fragments survive. It follows the Giottesque convention of the throne as a three-dimensional structure that defines a coherent space around the figure. Its lower part, visible near the bottom edge of the fragment, suggests a raised platform or dais. Christ is therefore not a vision floating in the sky but a sovereign seated in majesty, a presentation consistent with his role as judge at the end of time.

The Virgin kneels in profile at the left, turned towards her Son. She wears a dark mantle edged with a gold border over a red dress, and her head is framed by a halo whose incised decoration is still partly visible. Her hands are crossed on her breast in the gesture of humility and supplication, the same gesture of the Annunciation, when she accepted the divine will. Her face is lifted towards Christ with an expression of intense but composed entreaty. Her posture, smaller in scale and lower in the field, embodies the hierarchy between the Mother of God and her Son, and her role as the first and most powerful intercessor on behalf of humanity.

The iconography derives from the Byzantine Deesis, in which the Virgin and John the Baptist stand on either side of the enthroned Christ, interceding for the souls of the dead at the Last Judgement. In the West, the scheme was frequently simplified to show the Virgin alone, sometimes baring her breast to remind Christ of the humanity he received from her. Here she crosses her hands in a more restrained gesture. Whether the fresco originally included a second intercessor on the right is uncertain. A small curved fragment of what may be a halo is visible at the right edge, next to the throne, and this could indicate the presence of another figure, perhaps John the Baptist, which would complete the Deesis. The possibility should be verified on the original before any firm conclusion is drawn.

Within the collection of the Pinacoteca, the fresco bears witness to two histories at once. The first is the devotional life of fourteenth-century Fabriano, in which the theme of intercession responded to widespread anxiety about death and judgement in the decades following the Black Death of 1348. The second is the loss of the city's medieval heritage, of which the demolition of San Giovanni del Cantone in 1903 is only one episode. Despite its fragmentary state, the image preserves the essential core of its message: the Virgin's prayer and Christ's gesture of response. Its detachment and conservation have preserved an important, if damaged, example of Allegretto Nuzi's wall painting from the years of his full maturity.

### Madonna and Child Enthroned, with the Blessing Christ and Seraphim

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-35.jpg" alt="Gothic cusped panel painting, much abraded. The crowned Virgin sits frontally on a green stone throne with scrolled armrests, set on a low dais. She wears a red dress and a dark blue-black mantle patterned with gold vine leaves and bunches of grapes, edged with a gold border, and a dark veil over her head. On her left knee she holds the Christ Child, who turns and reaches up towards her; his pale garment is touched with blue. Behind her hangs a white cloth of honour decorated with blue trefoils and small red crosses. In the gable above, a roundel contains the half-length blessing Christ holding a book, surrounded by three red seraphim">
  <figcaption>Madonna and Child Enthroned, with the Blessing Christ and Seraphim, 1370, tempera and gold on a cusped panel, 182.9 × 68.3 cm, Fabriano, Pinacoteca Civica "Bruno Molajoli", from the church of San Nicolò in Fabriano.</figcaption>
</figure>

The panel comes from the church of San Nicolò in Fabriano, a provenance of particular significance for the painter's biography. According to Ghisalberti's entry in the *Enciclopedia dell'Arte Medievale*, Nuzi died in Fabriano in 1373, some time after 26 September, when he drew up his will, and he was buried in the church of San Niccolò. The Madonna therefore comes from the very church in which the painter chose to be laid to rest. It is tempting to see in it a work produced for a community with which he had a particularly close relationship, although no document links the commission to his burial. The panel is now in the Pinacoteca Civica "Bruno Molajoli", where the museum dates it to 1370 and assigns it to the master's hand.

The panel's format is that of a tall, cusped panel, 182.9 by 68.3 centimetres, crowned by a steep triangular gable and framed by a pointed arch with mouldings. Its proportions and its self-contained iconographic programme suggest that it may have been an independent devotional image rather than the central element of a polyptych. The possibility of lost lateral panels cannot, however, be excluded without technical examination of the edges. The architecture of the frame, with its trilobed arch and small lateral capitals, follows the Gothic vocabulary common to Nuzi's altarpieces. Within it, the composition is dominated by the monumental, frontal figure of the enthroned Virgin, who fills almost the entire width of the field.

The Virgin's face displays the features of Nuzi's late female type: a long, narrow oval, a thin straight nose, small eyes set beneath arched brows, and a closed, slightly melancholy mouth. Her head, slightly inclined, is framed by a dark veil and surmounted by a delicate crown with fleurs-de-lis and small gems, marking her as Queen of Heaven. Her gaze is directed outward towards the beholder rather than towards the Child, a convention that establishes a direct devotional relationship with the viewer. The heavy, somewhat abstract modelling of the face, now worn down to its underlayers, reflects the late tendency described in the literature: in Nuzi's works of the 1370s, forms become more rigid and simplified, and volumes are progressively flattened.

The most striking element of the panel is the Virgin's mantle. Its dark blue-black ground is covered with a regular pattern of gold vine branches, leaves and clusters of grapes, and it is edged with a broad gold border that traces an elegant curve across her knees. As the museum's entry notes, the vine and the grapes are symbols of Christ's sacrifice. Christ describes himself in the Gospel of John as "the true vine", and the wine of the Eucharist is the blood he shed for humanity. By wrapping the Virgin in this pattern, the painter turns her mantle into an image of the Eucharist, anticipating the Passion within the tender image of the Mother and Child. The treatment of the fabric as a flat decorative surface, spread across the lower half of the panel, exemplifies the "decorative vocation" that the museum identifies as characteristic of Nuzi's last years.

The Child sits on the Virgin's left knee, supported by her hands, and turns towards her with his head raised and his arm extended to reach her. He wears a pale garment with touches of blue, now much abraded. His pose conveys a natural intimacy that contrasts with the hieratic frontality of the Mother. The museum's entry refers to the mechanical gestures of the figures. The observation is apt: the movements of the hands and of the Child's body, repeated from the formulas of the workshop, have lost some of the spontaneity of Nuzi's works of the 1350s and 1360s, and become signs within a decorative system rather than the expression of a spontaneous emotional relationship.

Behind the Virgin, a white cloth of honour is draped over the back of the throne and falls on either side of her figure. It is decorated with a regular pattern of blue trefoils alternating with small red crosses. The museum interprets the trefoil as a probable allusion to the Trinity. Such a reading is plausible in a panel whose programme combines the Incarnation, the Eucharist and the heavenly glory of Christ, although the motif was also widespread as a textile ornament. The throne itself is a solid structure of green stone with scrolled armrests and small columns, set on a low dais. It retains the three-dimensional conception inherited from Giotto and his Florentine followers, even as the textiles spread across it tend to dissolve its spatial depth into pattern.

In the gable, within a gold roundel, appears the half-length figure of Christ, bearded, dressed in a red tunic and a blue mantle. He raises his right hand in blessing and holds a closed book in his left. The roundel is surrounded by three red seraphim, the highest order of the angelic hierarchy, whose wings fill the triangular field around it. The vertical axis thus leads from the Child on the Virgin's knee to the adult Christ in glory above. The panel presents at once the Incarnate Word and the eternal Word, the Son of Mary and the Pantocrator surrounded by the heavenly court.

The condition of the panel requires a careful reading. The museum's phrase "profound unveiling of the pictorial film" refers to the severe abrasion of the paint surface. Much of the final modelling of the faces and flesh has been lost, exposing the green underlayers, and the gold and the colours have been worn down, giving the image its present faded appearance. The attribution nevertheless rests on solid ground: the facial types, the structure of the throne, the design of the textiles and the treatment of the gable correspond closely to Nuzi's dated late works, above all the Madonna Enthroned of 1372 in Urbino. The panel from San Nicolò is thus a significant document of the final phase of Allegretto Nuzi's career, when the leading master of the Fabriano school, at the end of his life, pushed his painting towards an increasingly rich and ornamental language.

### Head of the Christ Child (fragment of a Saint Christopher?)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-36.jpg" alt="">
  <figcaption>Head of the Christ Child (fragment of a Saint Christopher?), c. 1360, detached fresco mounted on a support, 52.7 × 29.4 cm, Fabriano, Pinacoteca Civica "Bruno Molajoli", from the great vaulted passageway (voltone) of the Palazzo del Podestà in Fabriano.</figcaption>
</figure>

The fragment comes from the Palazzo del Podestà, the Gothic civic palace that closes the main square of Fabriano. The building is raised over a monumental vaulted passage, the *voltone*, through which one of the town's principal streets runs beneath the palace. The fresco was painted on the surfaces of this passage, a public space of transit rather than a church or chapel. It was later detached and mounted on a support, and it is now preserved in the Pinacoteca Civica "Bruno Molajoli". The detachment rescued what survived of the painting from the damage caused by exposure, traffic and time, but it isolated the head from the larger composition to which it belonged. Today that composition can only be reconstructed through the fragment itself and through the iconographic conventions of the period.

The subject can be recovered with reasonable confidence. The museum's entry notes that the Child was shown with his arms raised and extended towards a figure at his right, and it identifies that figure as Saint Christopher, whose hair the Child grasps. According to the *Golden Legend*, Christopher was a giant who served travellers by carrying them across a dangerous river on his shoulders. One night he carried a child who grew so heavy that the giant could barely reach the far bank; the child then revealed himself as Christ, who bore the weight of the whole world and its Creator. In Italian painting of the fourteenth century, the Child is frequently shown seated on the saint's shoulder, holding onto his head or hair, which corresponds exactly to the pose described by the museum.

The location of the image is fully consistent with this identification. Late medieval devotion held that whoever looked upon an image of Saint Christopher would be protected from sudden death, *mors improvisa*, for the rest of that day. For this reason, gigantic figures of the saint were painted on façades, near town gates, on bridges and along roads, where travellers and passers-by could see them. A monumental Saint Christopher under the *voltone* of the Palazzo del Podestà, over a busy public thoroughfare, would have offered daily protection to the citizens and travellers passing beneath it. The saint's colossal scale, of which the small head of the Child gives only an indirect idea, was an essential part of the image's effectiveness.

What survives is the Child's face in three-quarter view, turned towards the left. The single visible eye is almond-shaped, with a dark iris and a softly modelled lid, and it gazes forward with a calm, almost pensive expression. The cheek is full and rounded, and the contours of the nose, lips and chin are defined by subtle transitions of tone rather than by strong outlines. The modelling of the flesh proceeds by gentle gradations of warm pink and ochre, which confer on the face a soft, luminous volume. The museum's entry rightly draws attention to the delicacy of the flesh tones, which are the most distinctive quality of the fragment.

The traces to the left of the face offer clues to the lost composition. A series of horizontal brownish strands, rendered with parallel strokes, may correspond to the hair of Saint Christopher, which the Child held with his raised hands, or to the curls of the Child himself. The reddish pigment visible at upper left may belong to the Child's garment, the saint's clothing or the red cross of a cruciform halo. Given the fragmentary state and the warm lighting of the photograph, these identifications must remain tentative. Nevertheless, they are consistent with a composition in which the Child and the giant were closely joined, heads almost touching, in the intimate relationship that characterises many Trecento images of Saint Christopher.

The attribution to Allegretto Nuzi and the dating to around 1360 place the fragment in the years of the painter's greatest activity as a fresco painter in Fabriano. The soft, rounded modelling of the face, the gentle transitions of tone and the calm expression find close parallels in the Child figures of his panel paintings and in the youthful heads of his frescoes, such as the maidens in the Ursula cycle in San Domenico. The fragment reveals a painter who had absorbed the Florentine lesson of plastic modelling and adapted it to a softer, more luminous idiom. At this scale, the quality of the handling is surprisingly high for an image intended to be seen from a distance by passers-by in a public passage.

The fresco's condition is fragile, and its state of preservation must be taken into account in any reading. The paint surface is covered by a dense network of cracks and small losses, and the painted layer breaks off to the right along an irregular edge that exposes the bare plaster. The present warm, golden tonality of the image is partly the result of the ageing of the surface and of any materials applied during detachment and restoration. It is also accentuated by the lighting of the photograph. The original colours were probably cooler and more varied, and the flesh tones would have stood out against the saint's garments and the background more sharply than they do today.

Despite its modest size and fragmentary state, the head of the Christ Child is an important document of Nuzi's activity for civic patrons and of the role of images in the public spaces of the medieval Italian commune. It shows that the leading painter of Fabriano did not work only for churches and religious orders, but also for the civic institutions that governed his town. Their commissions invested the seat of communal power with the protection of the saints. The delicate face of the Child, detached from the lost colossus that once carried him, remains a moving witness of a monumental image that watched over the daily life of Fabriano.

### Four Roundels with King David, a Prophet and Two Apostles

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-37.jpg" alt="Four fragments of fresco arranged in a square grid within a wooden frame, each showing a haloed half-length figure inside a painted grey-white circular frame on a black ground. Top left: King David, crowned, with short curly brown hair and beard, holding a sceptre in his right hand and a closed book in his left, dressed in red and ochre. Top right: an elderly bearded prophet in profile wearing a white pointed cap, raising his right hand and holding an unrolled scroll with an inscription. Bottom left: a saint with long wavy white hair and a long beard, wrapped in an orange mantle, probably the apostle Andrew; part of this roundel is lost and filled with plain plaster. Bottom right: a saint with short grey hair and beard, raising his right hand in a gesture of speech and holding a scroll, probably the apostle Peter">
  <figcaption>Four Roundels with King David, a Prophet and Two Apostles, 1360–70, four detached fresco fragments transferred to a support and reassembled in a single frame, 34 x 34 cm (each), Fabriano, Pinacoteca Civica "Bruno Molajoli", from the church of San Giovanni del Cantone in Fabriano, demolished in 1903.</figcaption>
</figure>

The four roundels come from the church of San Giovanni del Cantone in Fabriano, demolished in 1903. The same church also yielded the detached fresco of the Virgin interceding with Christ enthroned, now likewise in the Pinacoteca Civica "Bruno Molajoli" and also attributed to Allegretto Nuzi. The two works therefore allow a partial reconstruction of the decoration of a lost church, painted in the 1360s by the leading master of the Fabriano school. The roundels were detached from the wall, transferred to a new support and reassembled in a single frame. The present square arrangement is modern and does not reflect their original sequence. They were most probably part of a continuous series of *clipei* running along the intrados of an arch, the ribs of a vault or a painted frieze.

The *clipeus*, a circular frame containing a portrait bust, has a long history that goes back to Roman funerary and imperial imagery. In medieval art, it became the standard device for presenting series of prophets, apostles, saints and ancestors of Christ on arches, vaults and borders. In the fourteenth century, painters in the Giottesque tradition used such series to frame narrative cycles and to construct typological programmes, in which the figures of the Old Testament announced those of the New. Here the painted frames are rendered as simple rings of grey-white stone against a black ground, which lends the figures an effect of sculptural relief. The busts project slightly beyond the edge of the frame, and their gestures and attributes animate the circular field.

The first roundel, at upper left, shows a crowned king with short curly brown hair and a trimmed beard. The museum identifies him as King David. He holds a sceptre in his right hand, its thin shaft rising diagonally across the field, and a closed book in his left. The book is a fitting attribute for the author of the Psalms, which the medieval Church read as the most important prophecies of Christ. David was also the royal ancestor from whose line the Messiah was to descend, and his presence establishes the genealogical and prophetic link between the Old and New Testaments. His frontal pose, direct gaze and regular features give him a dignified, almost iconic presence.

The second roundel, at upper right, contains an elderly prophet shown in strict profile, turned towards the left. He has long white hair and a flowing white beard, and he wears a white pointed cap. This form of headgear was often used in medieval art to characterise Old Testament figures. He raises his right hand with the palm open in a gesture of speech or prophecy, and holds with his other hand an unrolled scroll that curves across the right side of the roundel. The scroll bears an inscription in dark letters, now difficult to read. Its transcription on the original could permit the identification of the prophet through the text of his prophecy. The profile pose, rare in the series, suggests that he was turned towards an adjacent figure or towards the centre of the programme.

The third roundel, at lower left, shows a saint with long, wavy white hair falling onto his shoulders and a long, full beard, wrapped in an orange mantle. The museum proposes his identification as the apostle Andrew, whose traditional iconography features precisely such dishevelled white hair and a long beard. The face is modelled with care: the heavy-lidded eyes, the long straight nose and the downturned mouth convey an expression of grave contemplation. A large part of this roundel is lost at the lower left, where the missing plaster has been filled with a neutral surface. The surviving part nevertheless preserves the head and the upper part of the mantle almost intact.

The fourth roundel, at lower right, contains a saint with short, curly grey hair and a thick, short beard. He raises his right hand with two fingers extended in a gesture of speech or blessing and holds a scroll in his left. The museum identifies him as the apostle Peter, whose physiognomy corresponds closely to the traditional type. In the absence of his usual attribute, the keys, the identification rests on the face alone and remains probable rather than certain. The pairing of Peter and Andrew, brothers and the first apostles called by Christ, would be entirely natural within a series of apostles. It suggests that the original sequence of roundels included at least part of the apostolic college, alongside prophets and kings of the Old Testament.

Stylistically, the roundels are consistent with Nuzi's mature manner of the 1360s. The heads are constructed with solid, rounded volumes and modelled with soft transitions of flesh tones over a greenish underlayer, while the hair and beards are rendered with long, fluid strokes that give them a sense of movement. The expressive range is notable, from the regal composure of David to the prophetic intensity of the elder in profile and the grave meditation of the apostles, and it testifies to the painter's ability to individualise figures within a repetitive decorative format. The black ground, which may originally have been a deep blue applied *a secco*, now sets off the pale flesh and warm garments with particular force.

Together with the Virgin interceding with Christ enthroned, the four roundels offer a glimpse of the lost decoration of San Giovanni del Cantone. It is plausible that the kings, prophets and apostles in their *clipei* framed or accompanied the central image of Christ enthroned with the interceding Virgin, forming a programme centred on the Last Judgement and the communion of saints. Such a hypothesis cannot be verified without further documentation. The fragments nevertheless testify to the quality of Nuzi's fresco painting in his maturity. Reassembled in the Pinacoteca, these four small heads preserve the memory of a church that disappeared in 1903.

### The Mystic Marriage of Saint Catherine of Alexandria, with a Female Saint Presenting a Kneeling Donor

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Allegretto-Nuzi/allegretto-nuzi-38.jpg" alt="Detached fresco from a street shrine, displayed in a round-arched niche. On a blue ground, the crowned Virgin stands at the centre in a blue mantle scattered with gold stars over a red dress, holding the Christ Child on her right arm. The Child holds a scroll and turns towards Saint Catherine of Alexandria on the left, who wears a pale blue gown and extends her hand to receive the ring. On the right, a second haloed female saint in a golden gown raises her hand; below her, the kneeling donor, probably a knight, is almost entirely lost. Two winged angels hover above the Virgin. The vault of the niche is painted with dark red foliage and a lozenge at the top. In the right embrasure stands Saint Lucy; in the left, only the halo of another saint survives. The lower part of the fresco is heavily abraded.">
  <figcaption>The Mystic Marriage of Saint Catherine of Alexandria, with a Female Saint Presenting a Kneeling Donor, 1360-70, detached fresco mounted on a support, 216.4 × 150.2 cm, Fabriano, Pinacoteca Civica "Bruno Molajoli", from a street shrine (*edicola*) in Via San Filippo, Fabriano, detached in 1971.</figcaption>
</figure>

The fresco decorated a street shrine in Via San Filippo in Fabriano, a district that in the fourteenth century was occupied by the convent of the church of San Francesco alle Logge. It was detached in 1971 for conservation reasons and transferred to the Pinacoteca Civica "Bruno Molajoli", where it is displayed in a niche that reproduces the original arched shape of the shrine. The work has exceptional documentary importance, since it is the only fresco by Nuzi that survives with his signature. It therefore constitutes the fixed point of reference against which all the attributions of his wall paintings must be measured, from the Chapel of San Lorenzo in the cathedral to the Ursula cycle in San Domenico and the fragments from San Giovanni del Cantone.

Street shrines were a characteristic feature of the medieval Italian city. Small niches or aedicules opened on the façades of houses, at street corners or along convent walls, they offered passers-by an image of the Virgin and the saints for prayer and protection. They were often commissioned by private individuals as ex-votos or as public acts of piety, and they testify to the diffusion of sacred images beyond churches into the spaces of daily life. The Via San Filippo shrine, with its elaborate programme of Virgin, Child, saints, angels and a kneeling donor, represents an unusually ambitious example of this type. Its quality and its signature show that the leading painter of Fabriano did not disdain such commissions.

The composition is organised around the standing Virgin, who occupies the centre of the niche. She wears a crown over a white veil and a deep blue mantle scattered with gold stars over a red dress. The stars allude to her titles of Queen of Heaven and *Stella Maris*, the Star of the Sea. Her face, with its long oval, narrow eyes and small mouth, corresponds to Nuzi's mature female type. She gazes out towards the beholder rather than towards the Child or the saints, a convention that connects the devotional image with the passer-by in the street. Two winged angels hover above her, turned towards the centre, in a gesture that seems to frame and honour the Mother of God.

The Christ Child, seated on the Virgin's right arm, is the active protagonist of the scene. He turns towards Saint Catherine on the left and holds a scroll, while extending his hand towards her to place the ring on her finger. Catherine, identified by her halo and her position, wears a pale blue gown with a gold neckline and extends her hand to receive the ring. According to the legend, Catherine was a princess of Alexandria in the fourth century, to whom Christ appeared in a vision and took as his bride, and upon waking she found the ring of the mystic marriage still on her finger. The theme is a late medieval development of her legend, not found in its earliest versions. It was particularly popular in the fourteenth century, when it expressed the ideal of the soul's spiritual union with Christ.

On the right, a second female saint, not yet identified, stands in a golden gown and raises her hand in a gesture of presentation. According to the museum's entry, she accompanied a kneeling donor, probably a knight, whose sword and armour can still be made out. Most of the donor figure is lost in the heavily abraded lower zone of the fresco. The donor's presence reveals the votive nature of the shrine. The patron had himself represented in eternal prayer before the Virgin, recommended by his patron saint, in a public place where his piety would be visible to his fellow citizens. The identity of the knight remains unknown. Should coats of arms or inscriptions emerge from technical examination, they could shed light on his family.

The decoration extended beyond the main wall into the embrasures and vault of the niche. The vault is painted with dark red foliate scrolls, with a lozenge-shaped motif at its apex. In the right embrasure stands Saint Lucy, the martyr of Syracuse, still recognisable; in the left embrasure, only the halo of another saint survives. The presence of Lucy, patron of eyesight and of light, may reflect the devotion of the donor or of the neighbourhood. The whole shrine formed a small, coherent sacred space. The viewer standing in the street found himself enclosed by the Virgin, the saints and the angels, as if before a miniature chapel.

Stylistically, the fresco displays the characteristics of Nuzi's mature manner. The figures are elongated and elegant, the faces gentle and idealised, and the drapery falls in long, rhythmic folds. The palette combines blue, red, pale blue and gold in a decorative harmony typical of his work of the 1360s. The gold haloes, worked with incised rays, and the starry mantle of the Virgin show the care devoted to the precious details. The rigorous frontality of the Virgin, contrasting with the lively movement of the Child and the saints, creates a balanced tension between devotional icon and narrative scene. The signature confirms, if confirmation were needed, the stylistic consistency between Nuzi's fresco and panel painting.

The condition of the fresco reflects centuries of exposure to the weather in a street shrine. The lower part, including the kneeling donor, is heavily abraded, and the paint surface is covered with losses and discolorations. The blue ground has partly faded or altered, and some areas, such as the right saint's gown, have lost their original modelling. The heads of the Virgin, the Child, the angels and the saints nevertheless survive in legible condition, and they allow the quality of the work to be appreciated. The detachment of 1971 saved the fresco from further decay, and it remains an irreplaceable document: the signed touchstone of Allegretto Nuzi's wall painting and a vivid witness to the public devotion of fourteenth-century Fabriano.

## Workshop, Followers and the Road to Gentile da Fabriano

The measure of Allegretto Nuzi's historical importance lies not only in his own production but in the school he founded. Before his activity, as one early twentieth-century survey bluntly put it, a school of painting could hardly be said to have existed in the Marche; after it, Fabriano possessed a continuous local tradition that would culminate, within two generations, in one of the supreme masters of the International Gothic. Allegretto was, in the words of the 2021 exhibition that consecrated his rediscovery, the reference point around which a genuine Fabrianese school developed.

The most important of his direct followers was Francescuccio di Cecco Ghissi, documented between 1359 and 1395, at once his pupil, his emulator and his collaborator. Ghissi took over Allegretto's compositional types — above all the Madonna of Humility, of which the San Severino panel of 1366 furnished the prototype — and translated them with a mystical gentleness and an exquisite decorative sense of his own, though with, in the traditional critical judgement, less marked originality than his master. His works are scattered across the Marche and beyond — in Santa Lucia at Fabriano (1359), in Sant'Andrea at Montegiorgio (1374), in the Pinacoteca of San Severino (1366), in Fermo and Ascoli Piceno — and his dismembered altarpiece of the life of Saint John the Evangelist, its nine panels long dispersed among museums and recently reunited for study and exhibition, has become a celebrated case in the reconstruction of Trecento polyptychs. The precise nature of the Nuzi–Ghissi relationship — where the master's hand ends and the pupil's begins — remains one of the central problems of Fabrianese studies, and it was the subject of an influential hypothesis by Federico Zeri.

Around these two principals gathered a wider circle. The sources name the shadowy Master of the Magi of Fabriano, a sculptor in polychrome wood whose sacred groups echo Allegretto's painting in the tenderness of their gestures and the richness of their painted fabrics; the anonymous painters of the San Domenico sacristy, once gathered under the problematic name of "Angelo di Meo Cartaiolo" but certainly the work of several hands; and the various local masters — the Master of Staffolo among them — who carried Allegretto's manner into the last decades of the century. The influence radiated beyond Fabriano across Umbria and the Marche, and frescoes as far afield as San Domenico in Perugia have been drawn into his orbit.

The ultimate significance of this school lies in the road it opened to Gentile da Fabriano, born around 1370 and thus overlapping with Allegretto's life by only three years. Gentile, who would become the greatest Italian exponent of the International Gothic and the painter of the dazzling Adoration of the Magi of 1423, began, according to the consensus of scholarship, within the Fabrianese tradition shaped by Allegretto Nuzi and Francescuccio Ghissi — that "precious, anti-naturalistic decorativism, at the limits of abstraction," as the 2014 exhibition catalogue characterised it, from which Gentile drew before renewing and transcending it. It is no small thing to have prepared, however indirectly, the ground for such a flowering, and it is above all as the founder of the tradition that produced Gentile that Allegretto claims his place in the larger narrative of Italian art. The love of tooled gold and sumptuous textile, the fusion of monumental form with decorative surface, the courtly refinement of colour — all the elements that Gentile would raise to their apogee were present, in embryo, in the art of the older Fabrianese master.

## Historiography and Critical Fortune

The critical fortune of Allegretto Nuzi is itself an instructive chapter in the history of taste, tracing the long arc by which a provincial master, all but forgotten in the centuries after his death, was gradually restored to view. [Giorgio Vasari](/endnotes.html#fn-giorgio-vasari), whose Lives fixed the canon of Italian art around a Florentine and Tuscan axis, passed him over in silence, as he did most of the painters of the Marche; the rediscovery of Allegretto would therefore have to await the rise of local antiquarian scholarship and, later, of connoisseurship.

The founding text of Marchigian art history, and the point of departure for all study of Allegretto, is the Memorie storiche delle arti e degli artisti della Marca di Ancona, published at Macerata in 1834 by the Marchese Amico Ricci (1794–1862), the first systematic survey of the art of the region. Ricci gathered the local traditions, recorded works and inscriptions since lost, and gave Allegretto his first sustained modern treatment; it is to Ricci that we owe, among much else, the report of the lost dated works in Santa Lucia and the early notice of the Urbino Madonna in the Castrica collection. The later nineteenth and early twentieth centuries saw the documentary foundations laid: Anselmi published the testament in 1893 and established the date of death and place of burial in 1906; local scholars such as Marcoaldi, Sassi and Zonghi combed the Fabriano archives; and the panels began to attract the attention of the international connoisseurs — Bernard Berenson, who devoted a pioneering study to Allegretto's "early works" in 1921–1922, Raimond van Marle, Lionello Venturi, whose 1915 "Attraverso le Marche" reconstructed key inscriptions, and Richard Offner, whose Corpus of Florentine Painting situated Allegretto within the Florentine orbit.

The modern scholarly foundation, however, was laid at mid-century, and here the specialist literature converges on a small number of authoritative voices. The first is Alessandro Marabottini, whose monographic study of 1951–1952 in the Rivista d'arte and whose entry in the second volume of the Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani (1960) together constituted the first rigorous modern account of the artist's life and work, establishing the chronological framework — the movement from an early Daddesque phase, through the Orcagnesque turn of the mid-1350s, to the increasingly Sienese and decorative maturity, and finally to the Venetian-inflected last works — that has structured all subsequent discussion. Marabottini's reading, with its insistence on the dominance of Ambrogio Lorenzetti throughout Allegretto's career and its cautious hypothesis of a late Venetian journey, remains the indispensable point of reference.

The second decisive contribution came from Miklós Boskovits, the pre-eminent connoisseur of early Italian painting in the later twentieth century, whose studies of Umbrian and Marchigian painting (from 1973 onward) and whose authoritative catalogue of the thirteenth- and fourteenth-century Italian paintings of the National Gallery of Art in Washington placed Allegretto's dated works and their attribution on a secure critical footing, refined the reconstruction of his Florentine sources — locating the essential Florentine component in Maso di Banco rather than Daddi alone — and integrated the documentary record with the material evidence of the panels themselves. It is largely through the systematic catalogues of the great museums, of which Boskovits's Washington volume is the model, that Allegretto has entered the international art-historical mainstream.

The third and most recent protagonist of Nuzi studies is Andrea De Marchi, the leading contemporary authority on Trecento painting in the Marche, who together with Matteo Mazzalupi curated the landmark exhibition Oro e colore nel cuore dell'Appennino. Allegretto Nuzi e il '300 a Fabriano, held at the Pinacoteca Civica "Bruno Molajoli" and the Museo Diocesano of Fabriano from 14 October 2021 to 30 January 2022 — the first exhibition ever devoted wholly to the artist. As the curators themselves described the achievement, the exhibition succeeded in "returning to Fabriano some thirty works by the great Master lent for the occasion, among them eleven panels from foreign museums" ("riportare a Fabriano una trentina di opere del grande Maestro prestate per l'occasione, fra cui undici tavole da musei stranieri"), and it reunited long-dispersed portions of polyptychs — marking the definitive rehabilitation of Allegretto as a major protagonist of the fourteenth century rather than a mere provincial epigone.

De Marchi's characterisation of the artist — as a painter who, "strong in his Tuscan education," exercised an enormous influence across Umbria and the Marche, importing a calm and monumental language matured on the tenderness of the Lorenzetti and the caressing volumes of Florentine Giotteschi like Maso di Banco and Bernardo Daddi, an innovator in iconography and an experimenter in technique who combined punches with abandon and displayed shimmering worked fabrics patterned with fancies of birds and tortoises — represents the current state of scholarship and has restored to Allegretto the full measure of his artistic personality. The 2021 exhibition was itself the sequel to the broader 2014 Fabriano exhibition Da Giotto a Gentile, which had already set Allegretto within the longue durée of the Fabrianese school from its Giottesque origins to Gentile's International Gothic, and it was accompanied by a rich programme of restoration and technical study, including the exemplary restoration of the Fabriano pentaptych.

The trajectory of Allegretto's critical fortune thus runs from Vasarian silence, through antiquarian retrieval and documentary excavation, to the connoisseurial reconstruction of Marabottini and Boskovits and the definitive modern consecration achieved by De Marchi and his collaborators — a curve that mirrors in miniature the broader modern rehabilitation of the "primitives" and of the regional schools once dismissed as peripheral to the Florentine-centred canon.

## Conclusion

Allegretto Nuzi was a provincial master in the most honourable sense of the term: an artist who took the highest achievements of the metropolitan centres — the plastic gravity of Florence, the linear sweetness of Siena — and made them speak in the dialect of his own region, with such conviction and such craftsmanship that he founded a school where none had existed before. His art is a mirror of the artistic geography of the mid-Trecento: in his early panels one reads the lessons of Daddi and Maso di Banco; in his maturity, the monumentality of Orcagna and the pervasive tenderness of Ambrogio Lorenzetti; in his last works, the first intimations of Venetian decorativism. Through it all runs a constant that is his alone — the love of gold worked into shimmering pattern, of textile rendered as pure ornament, of the sacred image made both grave and precious.

If the altarpieces carry Allegretto's public voice, the small panels reveal his private one. The diptychs and portable triptychs now scattered between Berlin and other collections — the enthroned Virgin paired with the Crucifixion, the Virgin and Child set opposite the Man of Sorrows, the tabernacles in which the Nativity, the Annunciation and the Crucifixion unfold within a few hand-spans of gilded wood — were objects to be opened, handled and contemplated at close range, and in them the monumental forms of his polyptychs are distilled into images of intimate scale. The reviewers of the 2021 exhibition were right to see in these works an innovation of type as well as of style, and to connect them with a spirituality of personal prayer increasingly close to the devotio moderna. Together with the Madonna of Humility, which he carried into the Adriatic world and handed on to Francescuccio Ghissi, they show that Allegretto's originality lay as much in what he gave his patrons to pray before as in the manner in which he painted it.

His art was also, and inescapably, a collaborative one. The Washington triptych of 1354 bears witness to his partnership with the Florentine Puccio di Simone; the frescoes of San Domenico betray at least two hands besides his own; and the London panel of Saints Catherine and Bartholomew, in which Allegretto's punches frame what appears to be Ghissi's brushwork, shows master and pupil working with the same tools in the same shop. To speak of "Allegretto Nuzi" is therefore to speak not only of an individual painter but of an enterprise: a workshop that designed, gilded, painted and delivered sacred images for the churches, religious houses and confraternities of Fabriano and its neighbours. The instruments of modern connoisseurship, from the study of punch-marks to the technical analysis that accompanies restoration, have made it possible to glimpse the division of labour within that enterprise, but the boundary between the master's hand and those of his assistants remains, in many works, a matter of judgement rather than of proof.

The fate of his works after his death has in turn shaped the way in which he can be known. The polyptychs that once stood on the altars of the Marche were dismembered, their panels sold separately and dispersed across the museums of Europe and North America, so that a single altarpiece may today be divided between several cities, and its original appearance can be recovered only by the patient comparison of dimensions, frames, punchwork and provenance. The catalogue assembled in this essay bears the marks of that history: the lateral compartments of the Saint Augustine polyptych, the wings of the Southampton Coronation, the recomposed Fribourg polyptych and the fragments of predellas and pinnacles are the surviving pieces of larger wholes. Paradoxically, the very prestige that made his gold grounds coveted by collectors ensured both his dispersal and his survival, and it was only with the 2021 exhibition, which brought eleven panels back from foreign museums, that some of these fragments were seen together again in the city for which they were made.

Much, finally, remains open. The contents of the testament of 1373 await a fresh reading of Anselmi's transcription or of the notarial original; the chronology of the undated panels still oscillates between competing proposals, as the distance between the prevailing early dating of the Berlin diptych and Romagnoli's date of 1365 makes clear; the respective shares of Allegretto, Ghissi and the workshop in the panels and frescoes continue to divide specialists; and the Venetian journey of his last years remains a hypothesis. These questions are not a weakness in the study of Allegretto but a sign of its vitality. The nearly one hundred years that separate Romagnoli's monograph of 1927 from the Fabriano exhibition of 2021 transformed a local glory into a painter of international standing, and the panels themselves, with their inscriptions, their punches and their pigments, still hold evidence that future research may yet recover.

The documentary record, from the Florentine enrolment of 1346 to the testament of 26 September 1373, allows us to trace the outline of a working life anchored in Fabriano; the sequence of dated panels, several of them signed, from the Washington triptych of 1354 to the Urbino Madonna of 1372, gives that life a firm chronological spine; and the frescoes of San Venanzio, San Domenico and Sant'Agostino, half-hidden in the churches of his native city, preserve the monumental art by which his contemporaries knew him. Where the evidence fails — in the lost dated works of Santa Lucia, in the illegible inscriptions, in the disputed attributions and the unverifiable early dates — honesty requires that the gaps be named, and they have been named here. What remains, when the uncertainties are set aside, is the figure of a painter of real distinction and genuine historical consequence: the founder of the Fabrianese school, the disseminator of Tuscan forms across the Apennines, the innovator who carried the Madonna of Humility into the Adriatic world, and the ancestor, across the short span of a single generation, of Gentile da Fabriano and the golden autumn of the International Gothic. It is fitting that his own city, after centuries in which his finest works drifted away to the museums of the world, should at last have gathered them home to honour him.
